be any vertex from
\Pi(x)
.
Next, let
S^{H}_{i}
be the
(k+4-i)
-th outermost layer of
A
for
i\in[k+3]
, so we count the layers starting from
x
. From (1) we get that
R_{H}(x,S^{H}_{i})\subseteq R_{H}(x,S^{H}_{i+1})
for
i\in[k+2]
and
R_{H}[x,S^{H}_{k+3}]\subseteq A
.
Since
\Pi
is a contraction model, the separation properties can be lifted from
H
to
G
(see
4.2
).
Therefore

R_{G}(v,\Pi(S^{H}_{i}))\subseteq R_{G}(v,\Pi(S^{H}_{i+1}))
for
i\in[k+2]
and
R_{G}[x,\Pi(S^{H}_{k+3})]\subseteq\Pi(A)
.
We have thus constructed a sequence of
k+3
sets which are disjoint, connected, and nested with respect to
v
.
By (2) these sets are
v
-planarizing.
Finally
Lemma

3.19
implies that
v
is
k
-irrelevant.
∎

In order to prove
Lemma

4.3
we need several observations about how inserting additional edges into
\boxtimes_{k}
affects its susceptibility to planarization.
First we show that under some conditions inserting an edge into a subgraph of
\boxtimes_{k}
makes it non-planar.
It is an adaptation of an argument from
[
CFK+15
, §7.8]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
H
be an extension of
\boxtimes_{k}
and let
U\subseteq[k]\times[k]
induce a connected subgraph of
\boxtimes_{k}
.
Suppose that there exist distinct
x,y\in U
such that
x
is internal,
T[x]\subseteq U
, and
xy\in E(H)\setminus E(\boxtimes_{k})
.
Then
H[U]
is not planar.

Proof.

We will construct a
K_{5}
-minor within
H[U]
.
Let
x=(i,j)
.
The first branch set
B_{1}
is just
\{x\}
.
Next, we divide the neighborhood of
x
in
\boxtimes_{k}
into sets
B_{2}=\{(i,j-1),(i+1,j-1)\}
,
B_{3}=\{(i+1,j),(i,j+1)\}
,
B_{4}=\{(i-1,j),(i-1,j+1)\}
.
Each
B_{i}
for
i\in[4]
induces a connected subgraph of
\boxtimes_{k}
and each pair of them is connected by an edge from
E(\boxtimes_{k})
.
Let
B_{1,2,3,4}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{4}B_{i}
and

B^{\prime}_{5}=T[x]\setminus B_{1,2,3,4}
.
Then
B^{\prime}_{5}
is adjacent in
\boxtimes_{k}
to
B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}
, but not to
B_{1}
.
Since
xy\not\in E(\boxtimes_{k})
, the vertex
y
is not contained in
B_{1,2,3,4}
.
Furthermore,
B^{\prime}_{5}
is an unrestricted
(y,B_{1,2,3,4})
-separator in
\boxtimes_{k}
.
By assumption
\boxtimes_{k}[U]
is connected, so
there exists a path
P
within
U
connecting
y
to
B^{\prime}_{5}
, which is disjoint from
B_{1,2,3,4}
.
This path can be a singleton when
y\in B^{\prime}_{5}
.
We set
B_{5}=B^{\prime}_{5}\cup V(P)
.
Then
E_{H}(B_{1},B_{5})\neq\emptyset
because
xy\in E(H)
.
Therefore
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4},B_{5}
form branch sets of a minor model of
K_{5}
in
H[U]
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
S\subseteq[k]\times[k]
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then the graph
\boxtimes_{k}-S
has at most
|S|+1
connected components.

Proof.

If follows from the definition that
\boxtimes_{k}
admits a Hamiltonian path; let
P
denote such a path. Removing
|S|
vertices can divide the path
P
into at most
|S|+1
connected components. The graph
P-S
can be turned into
\boxtimes_{k}-S
by adding edges and
this operation can only decrease the number of connected components, which implies the claim.
∎

Next, we show that if some branch set of a contraction model of an extension of some
\boxtimes_{\ell}
contains a connected subgraph
C
with edges to sufficiently many other branch sets, which are represented by internal vertices from the grid, then any solution of size at most
k
must intersect
C
.
This will imply a criterion for the necessity of a vertex.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
k,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
H
be an extension of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
, and
\Pi
be a contraction model of
H
in
G
.
Furthermore, let
S\subseteq V(G)
be of size at most
k
.
Suppose there exists
x\in V(H)
and a vertex set
C\subseteq\Pi(x)
such that
G[C]
is connected,
C\cap S=\emptyset
and there are
\ell=30(k+2)
distinct internal vertices
x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}
from
V(H)
such that
x_{i}\neq x
and
N_{G}(C)\cap\Pi(x_{i})\neq\emptyset
for each
i\in[\ell]
.
Then
G-S
is not planar.

Proof.

Let
H_{S}\subseteq[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
be the vertices that contain a vertex from
S
in their branch sets, together with
x
.
Clearly
|H_{S}|\leq k+1
, where the additive term accounts for
x
.
Let
I_{S}=\{y\in Int(\lambda)\mid T[y]\cap H_{S}\neq\emptyset\}
.
Since any vertex
z\in[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
can belong to at most 25 different sets
T[y]
, we have
|I_{S}|\leq 25\cdot(k+1)
.
Let
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\}\setminus I_{S}
.
We have
|X|\geq 5(k+2)
.
By
Lemma

4.6
, the graph
\boxtimes_{\lambda}-H_{S}
has at most
k+2
connected components, so there is
a set
U\subseteq[\lambda]\times[\lambda]

inducing a connected component of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}-H_{S}
which contains at least 5 vertices from
X
. Note that
x\notin U
since
x\in H_{S}
.
Since the graph
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
does not contain
K_{5}
as a subgraph,
there exists distinct vertices
x_{1},x_{2}\in X\cap U
such that
x_{1}x_{2}\not\in E(\boxtimes_{\lambda})
.
Note that
T[x_{1}],T[x_{2}]\subseteq U
by definition of
U
and
I_{S}
.
By assumption, there exist vertices
v_{1}\in\Pi(x_{1})
,
v_{2}\in\Pi(x_{2})
, contained in
N_{G}(C)
.
The last argument is based on contracting
C
into
x_{1}
, which creates the edge
x_{1}x_{2}
if it does not already exist.
Let us consider a graph
H^{\prime}
obtained from
H
by inserting the edge
x_{1}x_{2}
(if this edge is present in
H
, then
H^{\prime}=H
).
Since
C\cap S=\emptyset
,
x\not\in U
, and
C\subseteq\Pi(x)
, the graph
H^{\prime}[U]
is a minor of
G-S
.
From
Lemma

4.5
we get that
H^{\prime}[U]
is not planar and the same holds for
G-S
.
∎

We now use the lemma above to argue that if some branch set
\Pi(x)
of a contraction model of an extension of some
\boxtimes_{\ell}
contains a star-like structure of paths leading to neighbors in
\Pi(x)
of vertices in different branch sets, the central vertex of this star is
k
-necessary.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
k,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
H
be an extension of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
, and
\Pi
be a contraction model of
H
in
G
.
Suppose that
x\in V(H)
,
v\in\Pi(x)
, and there exists a family of
\ell=31(k+2)
paths
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{\ell}
in
G
, such that

1.

for each
i\in[\ell]
,
P_{i}
is a
(v,v_{i})
-path, where
v_{i}\in\Pi(x_{i})
for some internal
x_{i}\neq x
, and all interior vertices of
P_{i}
are contained in
\Pi(x)
,

2.

for each
i\neq j\in[\ell]
it holds that
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
and
V(P_{i})\cap V(P_{j})=\{v\}
.

Then
v
is
k
-necessary.

Proof.

Consider any vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus v
of size at most
k
.
We are going to show that
G-S
cannot be planar.
Since the paths
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{\ell}
are vertex-disjoint except for
v
, the set
S
can intersect at most
k
of them.
Let
C\subseteq\Pi(x)
be the union of the interiors of the paths not intersecting
S
, together with
v
.
Then
G[C]
is connected,
C\cap S=\emptyset
and there are
\ell-k\geq 30(k+2)
different internal vertices
x^{\prime}_{i}
for which
N_{G}(C)\cap\Pi(x^{\prime}_{i})\neq\emptyset
.
Therefore the assumptions of
Lemma

4.7
are satisfied and thus
G-S
is not planar.
∎

When a vertex in
H
has sufficiently many internal neighbors, then its branch set contains a tree spanning vertices adjacent to their branch sets, which is difficult to disconnect by
any solution of size at most
k
.
The next lemma shows that in this case either
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
or some vertex meets the condition of
Lemma

4.8
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
k,\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell=2\cdot 31^{2}\cdot(k+2)^{3}
,
H
be an extension of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
, and
\Pi
be a contraction model of
H
in
G
.
Suppose that there exists distinct vertices
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}
in
[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
such that for each
i>0
vertex
x_{i}
is internal and
x_{0}x_{i}\in E(H)
.
Then either
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
or
G
contains a
k
-necessary vertex.
Furthermore, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that
either finds a
k
-necessary vertex or correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.

Proof.

By assumption there is a mapping
\rho\colon[\ell]\to\Pi(x_{0})
such that for
i\in[\ell]
we have
N_{G}(\rho(i))\cap\Pi(x_{i})\neq\emptyset
, i.e.,
\rho(i)
is a neighbor of
\Pi(x_{i})
in
\Pi(x_{0})
.
Let
R=\{\rho(1),\dots,\rho(\ell)\}
.

Since
G[\Pi(x)]
is connected, there exists a tree
T
in
G[\Pi(x)]
connecting vertices from
R

such that each leaf of
T
belongs to
R
.
Such a tree can be computed in polynomial time as follows.
We initialize
T=G[\Pi(x)]
and while there is an edge or a vertex from
\Pi(x)\setminus R
whose removal maintains connectivity of
T
, we remove it.
While
T
is not a tree with leaves in
R
, then we can always find a removable edge or vertex.

Suppose there exists a vertex
v\in R
such that
\rho(i)=v
for at least
31(k+2)
indices
i\in[\ell]
.
Then
v
trivially satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

4.8
and hence
v
is
k
-necessary.
Suppose now there exists a vertex
v\in V(T)
such that the degree of
v
in
T
is at least
31(k+2)
.
Since every leaf of
T
belongs to
R
, there at
31(k+2)
paths in
G[\Pi(x)]
starting at
v
and ending at distinct vertices from
R
, so that these paths are vertex-disjoint except for their starting point
v
. Since each such path can be extended with an edge from
R
to a unique branch set
x_{i}
, this again meets the conditions of
Lemma

4.8
, so
v
is
k
-necessary.

Suppose none of the previous two cases hold and consider any vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus\{v\}
of size at most
k
.
Since we have
|\rho^{-1}(u)|\leq 31(k+2)
for each
u\in R
, there are at least
\ell-31\cdot k(k+2)\geq 31^{2}\cdot k(k+2)^{2}
indices for which
\rho(i)\not\in S
.
Next, observe that removing
k
vertices from a tree of maximal degree bounded by
31(k+2)
can split it into at most
31\cdot k(k+2)
connected components.
Therefore, there is a vertex set
C
inducing a connected component of
T-S
, such that
|\rho^{-1}(C)|\geq\frac{31^{2}\cdot k(k+2)^{2}}{31\cdot k(k+2)}>30(k+2)
.
In particular this means that
G[C]
is connected,
C\cap S=\emptyset
and
N_{G}(C)\cap\Pi(x_{i})\neq\emptyset
for at least
30(k+2)
distinct vertices
x_{i}
.
We take advantage of
Lemma

4.7
to infer that
G-S
is not planar.
Since the choice of
S
was arbitrary, this implies that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.
∎

We are ready to prove
Lemma

4.3
(restated below) and thus finish the proof of
Lemma

4.4
.
When there is a vertex in
H
with sufficiently many internal neighbors then the proof reduces to
Lemma

4.9
.
Otherwise, as
\widehat{H}
contains many non-isolated internal vertices, removing any
k
of them cannot hit all the edges in
\widehat{H}
.
Therefore any potential solution of size at most
k
leaves out some subgraph to which
Lemma

4.5
is applicable and thus it cannot be a planar modulator.

See
4.3

Proof.

We denote by

A\subseteq Int(\lambda)
the set of internal vertices which are non-isolated in
\widehat{H}
.
If there is a vertex
x\in[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
(not necessarily internal) which is adjacent in
\widehat{H}
to at least
\ell=2\cdot 31^{2}\cdot(k+2)^{3}
vertices from
A
, then we arrive at the case considered in
Lemma

4.9
, so we are done.
Suppose for the rest of the proof that there is no such vertex and consider any vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
k
.
We are going to show that
G-S
is not planar, implying
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.

We proceed similarly as in the proof of
Lemma

4.7
.
Let
H_{S}\subseteq[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
be the vertices that contain a vertex from
S
in their branch set.
Clearly
|H_{S}|\leq k
.
Let
I_{S}=\{x\in Int(\lambda)\mid T[x]\cap H_{S}\neq\emptyset\}
.
Since any vertex
y\in[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
can belong to at most 25 different sets
T[x]
, we have
|I_{S}|\leq 25\cdot k
.
Let
A_{S}=A\setminus(I_{S}\cup N_{\widehat{H}}[H_{S}]
).
Because we have assumed an upper bound on
|N_{\widehat{H}}(x)\cap A|
for each
x\in[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
,
we have
|A_{S}|\geq 4\cdot 31^{2}\cdot(k+2)^{4}-25\cdot k-k\cdot(\ell+1)\geq 31^{2}\cdot(k+2)^{4}>5\cdot(k+1)^{2}
.
Note that every vertex
x\in A_{S}
is internal,
T[x]\cap H_{S}=\emptyset
, and if
xy\in E(\widehat{H})
, then
y\not\in H_{S}
.

By
Lemma

4.6
, the graph
\boxtimes_{\lambda}-H_{S}
has at most
k+1
connected components, so there is
a set
U\subseteq[\lambda]\times[\lambda]

inducing a connected component of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}-H_{S}
containing at least
5(k+1)
vertices from
A_{S}
.
This gives
5(k+1)
vertices from
U\cap A_{S}
adjacent in
\widehat{H}
to vertices from
[\lambda]\times[\lambda]\setminus H_{S}
. We distinguish two cases, based on whether there is a
\widehat{H}
-neighbor of
U\cap A_{S}
that lies in the same component
U
or not.
Suppose first that there are vertices
x_{1},x_{2}\in U

such that
x_{1}\in A_{S}
and

x_{1}x_{2}\in E(\widehat{H})
.
By the assumption,
E(\boxtimes_{k})\cap E(\widehat{H})=\emptyset
so
x_{1}x_{2}\not\in E(\boxtimes_{k})
.
As
T[x_{1}]\subseteq U
, this meets the conditions of
Lemma

4.5
, which means that
H[U]
is not planar and, since it is a minor of
G-S
, the graph
G-S
is also not planar.

In the remaining case, each vertex of
U\cap A_{S}
has a neighbor in
\widehat{H}
that does not lie in
U
. Since there are at least
5(k+1)
vertices having such a neighbor and at most
k+1
components in
\boxtimes_{\ell}-H_{S}
,
by a counting argument there is a vertex set
U^{\prime}\subseteq[\lambda]\times[\lambda]
inducing another connected component of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}-H_{S}
and
at least 5 vertices in
U\cap A_{S}
adjacent in
\widehat{H}
to
U^{\prime}
.
Since the graph
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
does not contain
K_{5}
as a subgraph, there must be a pair of them which is non-adjacent in
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
.
Therefore,
there exist vertices
x_{1},x_{2}\in U\cap A_{S}
,
y_{1},y_{2}\in U^{\prime}
such that
x_{1},x_{2}
are distinct and internal,
y_{1},y_{2}
are not necessarily internal nor distinct,
x_{1}x_{2}\not\in E(\boxtimes_{\lambda})
,
x_{1}y_{1},x_{2}y_{2}\in E(\widehat{H})
.
Our final argument is based on contracting
U^{\prime}
into
x_{1}
.
Let us consider a graph
H^{\prime}
obtained from
H
by inserting the edge
x_{1}x_{2}
.
As
(U\cup U^{\prime})\cap H_{S}=\emptyset
, we get that
H^{\prime}[U]
is a minor of
G-S
.
By the definition of
A_{S}
we have that
T[x_{1}]\subseteq U
, so we can use
Lemma

4.5
to conclude that
H^{\prime}[U]
(and so
G-S
) is not planar.
The claim follows.
∎

4.2
Finding a large grid minor

In order to take advantage of
Lemma

4.4
, we need a way to find a large triangulated grid minor when the input graph has large treewidth. Since the triangulated grid
\boxtimes_{k}
is a minor of a
\mathcal{O}(k)\times\mathcal{O}(k)
-grid, for this purpose it suffices to find a large grid minor.
It is known that any graph
G
excluding a
k\times k
-grid minor has treewidth bounded by
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
The best known bound on the exponent at
k
is 9 (modulo polylogarithmic factors)
[
CT21
]
.
Furthermore, there is a randomized polynomial-time algorithm that outputs either a minor model of a
k\times k
-grid in
G
or a tree decomposition of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{98}(\log k)^{\mathcal{O}(1)})

[
CC16
]
.
However, we would like to avoid randomization and instead we show that in our setting we can rely on the deterministic algorithm finding a large grid minor in a planar graph of large treewidth
[
GT12
,
FLSZ19
]
.
For our convenience we state a version of the planar grid theorem that already provides us with a triangulated grid (and even with a contraction model which will come in useful in further applications).
All other algorithms summoned in this section are also deterministic.

Proposition 4.10

(
[
FLSZ19
, Thm. 14.58]
)
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a connected planar graph
G
and an integer
t>0
, outputs either a tree decomposition of
G
of width
27\cdot t
or a contraction model of
\Gamma_{t}
in
G
.

In the original statement the guarantee on the decomposition width is
\frac{27}{2}\cdot(t+1)
, but for the sake of keeping the formulas simple, we trivially upper bound
t+1
by
2t
.

A concept more general than a grid minor is a bramble
[
ST93
]
which is, similarly as a grid minor, an object dual to a tree decomposition.
Kreutzer and Tazari
[
KT10
]
introduced the notion of a perfect bramble which enjoys some interesting properties.
First,
if a graph
G
admits a perfect bramble of order
k
, then
\mathrm{tw}(G)\geq\lceil{\frac{k}{2}}\rceil-1
, and if
\mathrm{tw}(G)=\Omega(k^{4}\sqrt{\log k})
then
G
admits a perfect bramble of order
k
.
What is particularly interesting for us, the union of subgraphs constituting a perfect bramble forms a subgraph of maximal degree 4.
This allows us to find a subgraph of constant degree whose treewidth is still large.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
KT10
, Thm. 5.1(c), Cor. 5.1]
)
.

There is a constant
c_{1}>0
and a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
with
\mathrm{tw}(G)\geq c_{1}\cdot t^{7}
,
finds a subgraph
H
of
G
, such that the maximal degree in
H
is at most 4 and
\mathrm{tw}(H)\geq t
.

Given an
n
-vertex subgraph
H
of constant degree, we can take advantage of the approximation algorithm for
Vertex planarization
by Kawarabayashi and Sidiropoulos
[
KS19
]

whose approximation factor depends on the maximal degree in
H
and
\log n
, which we will later bound by
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
We state it below in a form with two possible outcomes which is convenient for our application.
The first algorithm of this kind was given by Chekuri and Sidiropoulos
[
CS18
]
but with larger exponents in the approximation factor.
If we conclude that
\mathrm{mvp}(H)>k
and
H
is a subgraph of
G
, then also
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
and we can terminate the algorithm.
Otherwise, by adjusting the constants, we can find another (possibly large) subgraph that is planar and has large treewidth.
This allows us to use the planar grid theorem on this subgraph to find a grid minor, which is also a grid minor in the original graph
G
.

Theorem 4.12

(
[
KS19
, Cor. 1.2]
)
.

There is a constant
c_{2}>0

and a polynomial-time algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
of maximum degree
\Delta
and an integer
k
, either correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
or returns a planar modulator of size at most
c_{2}\cdot\Delta^{3}\cdot(\log n)^{\frac{7}{2}}\cdot k
.

If the treewidth of
G
is not too large, we would like to efficiently find a tree decomposition of moderate width.
To this end, we will use the polynomial-time approximation algorithm for treewidth by Feige et al.
[
FHL08
]
.

Theorem 4.13

(
[
FHL08
, Thm. 6.4]
)
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
such that
\mathrm{tw}(G)\leq t
, outputs a tree decomposition of
G
of width
\mathcal{O}(t\sqrt{\log t})
.

We wrap up these ingredients into the following algorithm.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
d>0
be any integer constant.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
and integer
k>0
such that
\log n\leq k\log k
, either outputs a tree decomposition of
G
of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{36})
, or outputs a minor model of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
, where
\lambda=d\cdot k^{5}
, or correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.

Proof.

We refer to constants
c_{1},c_{2}
from Theorems
4.11
and
4.12
.
Let
d_{1}=28\cdot d
and
d_{2}
be a constant for which
(\log k)^{\frac{7}{2}}\leq d_{2}\cdot\sqrt{k}
holds for all
k\geq 1
.
We begin with
4.13
for
t=c_{1}\cdot(4^{3}\cdot c_{2}\cdot d_{2}+d_{1})^{7}\cdot k^{5\cdot 7}
.
If
\mathrm{tw}(G)\leq t
, we obtain a tree decomposition of
G
of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{5\cdot 7}\sqrt{\log k})=\mathcal{O}(k^{36})
and we are done.
Otherwise
\mathrm{tw}(G)>c_{1}\cdot(c_{2}\cdot 4^{3}\cdot d_{2}+d_{1})^{7}\cdot k^{5\cdot 7}
and we can use the algorithm from
4.11
to find a subgraph
H
of
G
, such that the maximal degree in
H
is
\Delta\leq 4
and
\mathrm{tw}(H)\geq(c_{2}\cdot 4^{3}\cdot d_{2}+d_{1})\cdot k^{5}
.
As the next step, we execute the algorithm from
4.12
for the graph
H
with
\Delta\leq 4
.
In the first case, it
concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(H)>k
, which implies that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
and we are done.
Otherwise it
returns a planar modulator
S_{H}\subseteq V(H)
of size at most

c_{2}\cdot\Delta^{3}\cdot(\log n)^{\frac{7}{2}}\cdot k\leq c_{2}\cdot{4^{3}}\cdot(k\log k)^{\frac{7}{2}}\cdot k\leq c_{2}\cdot 4^{3}\cdot d_{2}\cdot k^{5}.

The graph
H^{\prime}=H-S_{H}
is planar and
\mathrm{tw}(H^{\prime})\geq\mathrm{tw}(H)-|S_{H}|\geq d_{1}\cdot k^{5}>27\cdot d\cdot k^{5}
because removing a single vertex can decrease the treewidth by at most one.
For the graph
H^{\prime}
we can take advantage of the planar grid theorem.
If it is disconnected, we focus on the connected component having large treewidth.
We use
Proposition

4.10
with
t=\lambda=d\cdot k^{5}
and, since treewidth of
H^{\prime}
is sufficiently large, the algorithm must return a contraction model of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
in
H^{\prime}
.
Since
H^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
G
, we can turn it into a minor model of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}
in
G
.
∎

Finally, we need to justify the assumption that
\log n
is small.
If
\log n
is larger than
k\log k
, then the exponential term of the form
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
becomes polynomial.
In such a case, we can use the known FPT algorithm for
Vertex planarization
.

Theorem 4.15

(
[
JLS14
]
)
.

There is an algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
and an integer
k
,
runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}\cdot n
and either correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
or outputs a planar modulator of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
.

After a large grid minor is identified, we invoke
Lemma

4.4
to find either a necessary vertex or an irrelevant vertex.
Once such a vertex is located we can remove it from the graph and proceed recursively.
In the end, we either solve the problem or arrive at the case where treewidth is bounded.

Proposition 4.16
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
and an integer
k
,
either

1.

outputs a planar modulator in
G
of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
, or

2.

correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
, or

3.

outputs a graph
G^{\prime}
together with a tree decomposition of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{36})
and an integer
k^{\prime}\leq k
, such that if
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
, then
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)-(k-k^{\prime})
.
Furthermore, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G,k,G^{\prime}
, and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
k^{\prime}
,
outputs a planar modulator
S
in
G
such that

|S|=|S^{\prime}|+(k-k^{\prime})
.

Proof.

First, we check if
k\log k<\log n
.
If yes, then the running time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}\cdot n
from
4.15
becomes polynomial.
In this is the case, we take advantage of the exact FPT algorithm to either conclude that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
or find a planar modulator of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
.
Otherwise
\log n\leq k\log k
and we can execute the algorithm from

Lemma

4.14
.
We choose a sufficiently large constant
d
to ensure that

31^{2}\cdot(2k+7)^{3}+4\leq d\cdot k^{5}
for all
k\geq 1
.
If the algorithm returns a tree decomposition of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{36})
or concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
, we are done.
Otherwise we obtain a minor model of
\boxtimes_{\lambda}

in
G
, where
\lambda=31^{2}\cdot(2k+7)^{3}+4
(if the returned grid is larger, we can easily truncate the minor model).
We supply the computed minor model to the algorithm from
Lemma

4.4
.
It either outputs a
k
-irrelevant or
k
-necessary vertex
v
, or concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.
In the first case we recurse on the instance
(G-v,k)
, in the second case we recurse on
(G-v,k-1)
, and on the last case we terminate the algorithm.

If
v
is
k
-irrelevant then any planar deletion set of size at most
k
in
G-v
is also valid in
G
.
This entails that whenever
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
then
\mathrm{mvp}(G-v)=\mathrm{mvp}(G)
.
If
v
is
k
-necessary then
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G-v)=\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
and so
\mathrm{mvp}(G-v)>k-1
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.
Furthermore,
for any planar deletion set
S
in
G-v
, clearly
S\cup\{v\}
is a planar deletion set in
G
.

Let
(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})
be the instance on which the recursion has stopped.
Observe that when we are given
a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
k^{\prime}

then by inserting the removed necessary vertices to
S^{\prime}
we can turn it into a planar modulator in
G
of size
|S^{\prime}|+(k-k^{\prime})
.
If the algorithm has terminated with
a planar modulator in
G^{\prime}
of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}

then we turn it into a planar modulator in
G
of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})+(k-k^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)
.
If we received a message that
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})>k^{\prime}
then it implies that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.
Finally, if the algorithm terminated with a tree decomposition of
G^{\prime}
of width
\mathcal{O}((k^{\prime})^{36})
we can return it and whenever the lifting algorithm receives a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}
, it can be turned into a planar modulator in
G
of size
|S^{\prime}|+(k-k^{\prime})
.
∎

5
Constructing a strong modulator

In this section we work with a given tree decomposition of
G
of width
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
We are going to process such a decomposition several times and so we begin with presenting a general procedure which scans the tree decomposition bottom-up and detects subgraphs with certain properties. The procedure is based on the well-known covering/packing duality for connected subgraphs of a bounded treewidth host graph (cf.
[
RT17
, Lemma 3.10]
). While such arguments are typically applied to pack and cover cycles or minor models, we will need it in a slightly more general form. In the lemma below, we therefore work with a family
\mathcal{F}
of vertex subsets of
G
which is hereditary (so that
\mathcal{F}
is closed under taking subsets), requiring only a polynomial-time subroutine to recognize whether a set belongs to
\mathcal{F}
or not.

Lemma 5.1
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
, a tree decomposition
(T,\chi)
of
G
, an integer
k
, and a polynomial-time subroutine that tests membership of vertex subsets
S\subseteq V(G)
in a hereditary family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{V(G)}
,
returns one of the following:

1.

a subset
T_{0}\subseteq V(T)

of size at most
k
, such that
every set
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus\bigcup_{t\in T_{0}}\chi(t)
, for which
G[C]
is connected, belongs to
\mathcal{F}
, or

2.

a collection of
k+1
disjoint vertex subsets
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{k+1}
, such that each
G[V_{i}]
is connected and
V_{i}\not\in\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Recall that notation
T_{t}
stands for the subtree of
T
rooted at
t\in V(T)
.
Observe that for a given set
U\subseteq V(G)
, we can check if all its connected subsets belong to
\mathcal{F}
by checking if each connected component of
G[U]
belongs to
\mathcal{F}
.

We consider an iterative process: in each step we identify a node
t\in V(T)
whose bag separates some connected subsets which belong to
\mathcal{F}
from the rest of the graph
but their union together with the bag of
t
contains a connected subset which does not belong to
\mathcal{F}
.

Initialize
S_{0}=\emptyset
and
U_{0}=\emptyset
.
In the
i
-th step,
i\geq 1
, we consider the induced subgraph
G-U_{i-1}
.
For
t\in V(T)
, let
\chi_{i}(t)=\chi(t)\setminus U_{i-1}
.
The pair
(T,\chi_{i})
is a tree decomposition of
G-U_{i-1}
.
We choose
t_{i}\in V(T)
to be a deepest node (breaking ties arbitrarily) so that
\chi_{i}(T_{t_{i}})
contains a connected vertex set which does not belong to
\mathcal{F}
– let us refer to this set as
V_{i}
.
Let
U_{i}=U_{i-1}\cup\chi_{i}(T_{t_{i}})
and
S_{i}=S_{i-1}\cup\chi_{i}(t_{i})
.
By
3.5
, the set
\chi_{i}(t_{i})
separates
G-U_{i-1}
into
V(G)\setminus U_{i}
and
\chi_{i}(T_{t_{i}})\setminus\chi_{i}(t_{i})
.
By the choice of
t_{i}
, we know that all the connected subsets of
\chi_{i}(T_{t_{i}})\setminus\chi_{i}(t_{i})
belong to
\mathcal{F}
.
We continue this process until all connected subsets of
V(G)\setminus U_{i}
belong to
\mathcal{F}
,
so there is no way to choose
t_{i+1}
.

If we have performed at least
k+1
iterations, we return the sets
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k+1}
.
Since
V_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
and
V_{j}\cap U_{i}=\emptyset
for
j>i
, these sets are disjoint.
Furthermore, each of them is connected and does not belong to
\mathcal{F}
.

If we have terminated in at most
\ell\leq k
iterations, then the set
S_{\ell}
is a union of
\ell
sets
\chi_{i}(t_{i})

and
\chi_{i}(t_{i})\subseteq\chi(t_{i})
.
Observe that
V(G)\setminus U_{\ell}
and sets
\chi_{i}(T_{t_{i}})\setminus\chi_{i}(t_{i})
,
i\in[\ell]
,
are pairwise separated by
S_{\ell}
in
G
.
These sets contain only connected subsets from
\mathcal{F}
.
In this case we return the set of nodes
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\}
.
∎

5.1
Planarizing small neighborhoods

In this section we work towards proving Lemma
5.4
, which essentially gives a reduction to problem instances with a
tidy
planar modulator
U
such that each component of
G-U
remains planar even when reinserting two arbitrary vertices from
U
. To get there, we need two ingredients.

The following simple lemma uses the fact that obstructions to planarity are connected together with covering/packing duality in a tree decomposition to either find a planar modulator of bounded size or conclude that any planar modulator must be large.

Lemma 5.2
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
with a tree decomposition of width
t-1
and an integer
k
,
either returns a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
k\cdot t
or correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
5.1
for the family
\mathcal{F}
of vertex subsets which induce planar subgraphs of
G
.
This property is hereditary and decidable in polynomial time
[
HT74
]
, so the conditions of the lemma are satisfied.
In the first scenario,
the algorithm returns a set of
at most
k
nodes, so that the union of their bags is a
planar modulator of size at most
k\cdot t
.
Otherwise,
the algorithm returns
k+1
vertex-disjoint non-planar subgraphs.
This testifies that every planar modulator must have at least
k+1
vertices.
∎

The next ingredient needed for Lemma
5.4
is the following encapsulation of Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 5.3
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
, sets
X_{0}\subseteq X\subseteq V(G)
, such that
G-X
is planar, a tree decomposition of
G-X
of width
t-1
, and integer
k
, returns one of the following:

1.

a set
Y\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
of size at most
k\cdot t
, such that for each vertex set
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus(X\cup Y)
, for which
G[C]
is connected, the graph
G[C\cup X_{0}]
is planar, or

2.

a collection of
k+1
disjoint vertex subsets
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{k+1}
such that
V_{i}\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
,
G[V_{i}]
is connected, and
G[V_{i}\cup X_{0}]
is non-planar.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
5.1
for the graph
G-X
and family
\mathcal{F}
of its vertex subsets defined
as follows:
C\in\mathcal{F}
if
G[C\cup X_{0}]
is planar.
In the first case, the union of bags of the returned nodes has size at most
k\cdot t
and satisfies condition (1).
Otherwise, we obtain
k+1
disjoint connected vertex sets that do not belong to
\mathcal{F}
.
∎

Using these ingredients we now present the lemma that reduces an instance to one with a suitably tidy modulator, or detects a small vertex set that allows an approximation-preserving reduction to a smaller graph.

Lemma 5.4
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
, a set
X\subseteq V(G)
of size
m
, such that
G-X
is planar, a tree decomposition of
G-X
of width
t-1
, and an integer
k
, returns one of the following:

1.

a set
U\supseteq X
of size at most
2^{3}\cdot m^{2}\cdot k^{3}\cdot t^{3}
, such that for each vertex set
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
, for which
G[C]
is connected,
and for each
X_{0}\subseteq U
of size at most 2,
the graph
G[C\cup X_{0}]
is planar, or

2.

a set
X_{0}\subseteq V(G)
of size at most 2,
such that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G-X_{0})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
.

Proof.

For every
x\in X
we
apply Lemma
5.3
with
X_{0}=\{x\}
and remaining parameters as in the statement of this lemma.

Suppose that for at least one call we obtain answer (2): a collection of
k+1
disjoint vertex subsets
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{k+1}
such that
V_{i}\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
and
G[V_{i}\cup\{x\}]
is non-planar.
Assume that
G
admits a planar modulator
S
of size
\ell\leq k
.
Then for some
i\in[k+1]
it holds that
V_{i}\cap S=\emptyset
.
Since
G[V_{i}\cup\{x\}]
is non-planar, we have
x\in S
.
Therefore
S\setminus\{x\}
is a planar modulator for
G-\{x\}
of size
\ell-1
and the set
\{x\}
satisfies condition (2).

Suppose otherwise, that for each
x\in X
we computed a set
Y_{x}\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
of size at most
k\cdot t
, such that for each vertex set
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus(X\cup Y_{x})
, for which
G[C]
is connected, the graph
G[C\cup\{x\}]
is planar.
Let
Y=\bigcup_{x\in X}Y_{x}
.
For each
x\in X
it holds that every connected component of
G-(X\cup Y)
is contained in some connected component of
G-(X\cup Y_{x})
.
Therefore for each
x\in X
and each
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus(X\cup Y)
, for which
G[C]
is connected, the graph
G[C\cup\{x\}]
is planar.

Next, we iterate over all subsets
X_{0}\subseteq X\cup Y
of size 2 and for each we apply Lemma
5.3
to the tree decomposition of
G-(X\cup Y)
and sets
X_{0},X\cup Y
.
Note that we can easily turn a tree decomposition of
G-X
into a tree decomposition of
G-(X\cup Y)
without increasing the width.

Again, suppose that for at least one call we obtain answer (2): a collection of
k+1
disjoint vertex subsets
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{k+1}
such that
V_{i}\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
and
G[V_{i}\cup X_{0}]
is non-planar.
Assume that
G
admits a planar modulator
S
of size
\ell\leq k
.
Then for some
i\in[k+1]
it holds that
V_{i}\cap S=\emptyset
.
Since
G[V_{i}\cup X_{0}]
is non-planar, we have
X_{0}\cap S\neq\emptyset
.
Therefore
S\setminus X_{0}
is a planar modulator for
G-X_{0}
of size at most
\ell-1
and the set
X_{0}
satisfies condition (2).

In the remaining case, for each considered
X_{0}=\{x,y\}
we have obtained a set
Z_{\{x,y\}}\subseteq V(G)\setminus(X\cup Y)
of size at most
k\cdot t
, such that for each vertex set
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus(X\cup Y\cup Z_{\{x,y\}})
, which is connected in
G
, the graph
G[C\cup\{x,y\}]
is planar.
Let
Z=\bigcup_{{\{x,y\}}\subseteq X\cup Y}Z_{\{x,y\}}
and
U=X\cup Y\cup Z
.
For each set
{\{x,y\}}\subseteq X\cup Y
it holds that every connected component of
G-U
is contained in some connected component of
G-(X\cup Y\cup Z_{\{x,y\}})
.
Therefore for each
{\{x,y\}}\subseteq X\cup Y
and each
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
, which is connected in
G
, the graph
G[C\cup\{x,y\}]
is planar.

To argue that the constructed set
U
satisfies condition (2), it remains to handle pairs
{\{x,y\}}\subseteq U
, which are not contained in
X\cup Y
.
Let
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
be a vertex set connected in
G
.
If
x,y\in Y\cup Z
, then

C\cup\{x,y\}\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
and this set induces a planar graph because
G-X
is planar by the assumption.
The remaining case is when

x\in X
and
y\in Z
.
If
y\in N_{G}(C)
,
then
C\cup\{y\}
is a connected subset of
V(G)\setminus(X\cup Y)
and it remains planar with
x
attached due to the first round of the construction.
If
y\not\in N_{G}(C)
, then
G[C\cup\{x\}]
is planar by the same argument as above and adding a vertex which has degree 1 or is isolated preserves planarity.

We established that attaching any pair of vertices from
U
to any connected component of
G-U
preserves its planarity.
It follows that the same holds if we consider attaching just a single vertex from
U
.
Finally, we estimate the size of
U
.
The set
Y
is a union of
m
sets
Y_{x}
and so
|Y|\leq m\cdot k\cdot t
.
The set
Z
is a union of
{\binom{X\cup Y}{2}}\leq(2|Y|)^{2}\leq 4\cdot(m\cdot k\cdot t)^{2}

many sets
Z_{\{x,y\}}
and so

|Z|\leq 4\cdot m^{2}\cdot k^{3}\cdot t^{3}
.
As
|X\cup Y|\leq|Z|
, the upper bound follows.
∎

5.2
Reducing the neighborhood of planar components

Using Lemma
5.4
we can reduce to an instance which has a tidy planar modulator
U
such that each component of
G-U
remains planar when reinserting two vertices from
U
. In this section we work towards obtaining even
stronger
properties for components outside the modulator as described in Definition
3.4
, so that each component outside the modulator induces a planar subgraph when taken together with its entire neighborhood.

The following lemma is inspired by existing tools to compute protrusion decompositions for problems on planar graphs (cf.
[
FLSZ19
, Lemma 15.13]
). It is used to either build an enhanced modulator which gives a bound on the size of the neighborhood of each remaining component, or identify a triple of vertices that allows an approximation-preserving graph reduction step by detecting three vertices which are part of many minor models of
K_{3,3}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
, set
X\subseteq V(G)
of size
m
such that
G-X
is planar, a tree decomposition
(T,\chi)
of
G-X
of width
t-1
, and an integer
k>0
, returns one of the following:

1.

a set
Y\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
of size at most
2^{3}\cdot m^{3}\cdot k\cdot t
, such that for each connected vertex set
C
of
G-(X\cup Y)
it holds that
|N_{G}(C)\cap X|\leq 2
and
|N_{G}(C)\cap Y|\leq 2t
,

2.

a triple
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\subseteq X
and a collection of
k+3
disjoint vertex subsets
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{k+1}
such that
V_{i}\subseteq V(G)\setminus X
,
G[V_{i}]
is connected, and
E({V_{i}},v_{j})\neq\emptyset
for all
i\in[k+3],\,j\in[3]
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
5.1
for the graph
G-X
, parameter
k^{\prime}=m^{3}\cdot(k+2)
and family
\mathcal{F}
of its vertex subsets defined
as follows:
C\in\mathcal{F}
if
|N_{G}(C)\cap X|\leq 2
.

In the first scenario,
the algorithm returns a set
S

of at most
k^{\prime}
nodes from
V(T)
, so that
for each connected component
C
of
G-(X\cup\bigcup_{t\in S}\chi(t))
it holds that
|N_{G}(C)\cap X|\leq 2
, which implies the same statement for each connected vertex set
C
.
Let
M=\overline{\mathsf{LCA}}(S)
and
Y=\bigcup_{t\in M}\chi(t))
.
By Lemma
3.9
, point (b),
|M|\leq 2|S|-1

and so
|Y|\leq 2\cdot m^{3}\cdot(k+2)\cdot t
.
We upper bound
k+2
with
3k\leq 2^{2}k
to get the formulated estimation. By Lemma
3.9
, point (a), for each connected vertex set
C
of
G-(X\cup Y)
(which is contained in a union of bags from a connected subtree of
T-M
by Observation
3.6
) the set
N_{G}(C)\cap Y
is contained in at most 2 bags, hence
|N_{G}(C)\cap Y|\leq 2t
.
We have
M\supseteq S
, so it also holds that
|N_{G}(C)\cap X|\leq 2
.

In the second scenario, we obtain a collection of
m^{3}\cdot(k+2)+1
disjoint vertex sets
V_{i}
which induce connected subgraphs of
G
and do not belong to
\mathcal{F}
.
Each
V_{i}
contains some triple from
X
in its neighborhood and,
since there are
\binom{m}{3}\leq m^{3}
distinct triples from
X
, one triple has to appear in the common neighborhood of at least
(k+3)
sets
V_{i}
.
It can be identified in polynomial time by enumerating all the triples from
X
.
∎

Using the preceding lemma, the next algorithm makes progress towards obtaining a strong modulator by ensuring planarity for each connected component outside the modulator, even when taken together with its neighborhood.

Lemma 5.6
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
, set
X\subseteq V(G)
of size
m
such that
G-X
is planar, a tree decomposition of
G-X
of width
t-1
, and an integer
k>0
, returns one of the following:

1.

a set
Z\supseteq X
of size at most
2^{13}\cdot m^{6}\cdot k^{10}\cdot t^{10}
, such that for each connected vertex set
C
in
G-Z
the graph
G[N_{G}(C)]
is planar and
|N_{G}(C)\cap Z|\leq 2t+2
, or

2.

a set
X_{0}\subseteq V(G)
of size at most 3,
such that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G-X_{0})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
.

In particular, when
G
is planar (so
\mathrm{mvp}(G)=0
) then the algorithm must return answer (1).

Proof.

We apply Lemma
5.4
for the same arguments as in the statement of this lemma.
We either obtain a set
X_{0}\subseteq V(G)
of size at most 2, which satisfies condition (2) or a set
U\supseteq X
of size at most
2^{3}\cdot m^{2}\cdot k^{3}\cdot t^{3}
, such that each connected vertex set
C\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
remains planar after attaching any vertex or a pair of vertices from
U
.
In the first case we are done and
in the second case, we proceed with Lemma
5.5
by substituting
X\leftarrow U
. We distinguish two cases, depending on the outcome of Lemma
5.5
.

Suppose first that we have obtained a triple
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\subseteq U
and a collection of
k+3
disjoint vertex subsets
(V_{i})_{i=1}^{k+1}
such that
V_{i}\subseteq V(G)\setminus U
,
G[V_{i}]
is connected, and
E({V_{i}},v_{j})\neq\emptyset
for all
i\in[k+3],\,j\in[3]
.
Assume that
G
admits a planar modulator
S
of size
\ell\leq k
.
Then for some
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}\in[k+3]
it holds that
V_{i_{j}}\cap S=\emptyset
for
j\in[3]
.
If
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\cap S=\emptyset
, then
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},V_{i_{1}},V_{i_{2}},V_{i_{3}}\}
would form branch sets of a
K_{3,3}
minor in
G-S
, which is not possible.
Hence,
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\cap S\neq\emptyset

and
S\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
is a planar modulator in
G-\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
of size at most
\ell-1
.
We can thus return
X_{0}=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
to satisfy condition (2).

Now suppose that the algorithm from Lemma
5.5
terminated with scenario (1).
We have obtained a set
Y
of size at most
2^{3}\cdot|U|^{3}\cdot k\cdot t\leq 2^{12}\cdot m^{6}\cdot k^{10}\cdot t^{10}
.
Let
Z=U\cup Y
, which satisfies the claimed size bound since
|U|\leq|Y|
. For each connected vertex set
C
of
G-(U\cup Y)
Lemma
5.5
guarantees that
|N_{G}(C)\cap U|\leq 2
and
|N_{G}(C)\cap Y|\leq 2t
, so
|N_{G}(C)\cap Z|\leq 2t+2
.
The set
N_{G}[C]\setminus U
is a connected vertex subset of
V(G)\setminus U
and
by the properties of
U
guaranteed by Lemma
5.4

for
X_{0}=N_{G}(C)\cap U
,
this implies that
G[(N_{G}[C]\setminus U)\cup(N_{G}(C)\cap U)]
is planar, and so

G[N_{G}[C]]
is planar.
∎

We are ready to state and prove the main result of this subsection, which encapsulates the previous reduction steps using the notion of strong modulators (Definition
3.4
). In addition, it gives a bound on the number of connected components with at least three neighbors which occur outside the new modulator
X^{\prime}
, using the terminology of fragmentation (Definition
3.3
).

Proposition 5.7
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
with a tree decomposition of width
t-1
, and an integer
k>0
, either correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
, or outputs a graph
G^{\prime}
with a
(2t+2)
-strong planar modulator
X^{\prime}
, and an integer
k^{\prime}\leq k
, so that:

1.

if
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
, then
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-(k-k^{\prime})
,

2.

the fragmentation of
X^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{O}(k^{49}t^{48})
,

3.

given a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, one can turn it into a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S^{\prime}|+3\cdot(k-k^{\prime})
in polynomial time.

Proof.

We begin with Lemma
5.2
to either find a planar modulator
X
of size at most
\lambda=k\cdot t
or conclude that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
.
In the first case,
we can easily turn the given tree decomposition of
G
into a tree decomposition of
G-X
of no greater width.
We keep the values of
t,\lambda
intact within this proof, while
k
is a free variable in the recursion.

We present the graph modification as a recursive process in which we are given a tuple
(G,X,T,\chi,k)
where
X
is a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
\lambda
and
(T,\chi)
is a tree decomposition of
G-X
of width at most
t
.
We either conclude that
(G,X,k)
satisfies the claim or recurse on some tuple
(G^{\prime},X^{\prime},k-1,T^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
so that
|X^{\prime}|\leq|X|
.
In order to prove the forward safeness we will show that when
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
then
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
.
To prove the backward safeness we show that when given a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}

we can turn in into a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S^{\prime}|+3
.

Given a tuple
(G,X,T,\chi,k)
,
we apply
Lemma

5.6
.
If we get the outcome (2), we obtain a set
X_{0}\subseteq V(G)
of size at most 3 such that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G-X_{0})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
: this yields the forward safeness.
The backward safeness follow from the fact that
when
S
is a planar modulator in
G-X_{0}

then
S\cup X_{0}
is a planar modulator in
G
.
Since
G-(X\cup X_{0})
is a subgraph of
G-X
, we can turn
(T,\chi)
into a tree decomposition of
G-(X\cup X_{0})
of no greater width.

If we get the outcome (1) from
Lemma

5.6
, we obtain a
(2t+2)
-strong planar modulator
Z\supseteq X
.
We have
|Z|\leq 2^{13}\cdot|X|^{6}\cdot k^{10}\cdot t^{10}\leq 2^{13}\cdot k^{16}\cdot t^{16}
.
We stop the recursion at this point.

In order to bound the fragmentation of
Z
, let us consider
another recursive process,
in which we process a triple
(G,Z,k)
where
Z
is a
(2t+2)
-strong planar modulator in
G
.
Whenever we recurse on
(G^{\prime},Z^{\prime},k-1)
we demand
|Z^{\prime}|\leq|Z|
and consider the same safeness conditions as before: (a)
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
, and (b) when given a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}

we can turn in into a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S^{\prime}|+3
.

Given a triple
(G,Z,k)
we enumerate all size-3 subsets of
Z
and look for a subset
Z_{0}:=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}\subseteq Z
, such that for at least
k+3
connected components
(C_{i})_{i=1}^{k+3}
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-Z)
we have
Z_{0}\subseteq N_{G}(C_{i})
.
Consider any planar modulator
S
in
G
of size
\ell\leq k
.
Analogously as in Lemma
5.3
,
we observe that for some
\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\}\in[k+3]
it must hold that
C_{i_{j}}\cap S=\emptyset
for each
j\in[3]
.
If
Z_{0}\cap S=\emptyset
, then
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},C_{i_{1}},C_{i_{2}},C_{i_{3}}\}
would form branch sets of a
K_{3,3}
minor in
G-S
, which is not possible.
Hence,
Z_{0}\cap S\neq\emptyset

and
S\setminus Z_{0}
is a planar modulator in
G-Z_{0}
of size at most
\ell-1
.
In such a scenario, we recurse on
(G-Z_{0},Z\setminus Z_{0},k-1)
.
We have
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G-Z-0)\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-1
.
Again,
when
S
is a planar modulator in
G-Z_{0}

then
S\cup Z_{0}
is a planar modulator in
G
.
Furthermore,
Z\setminus Z_{0}
is a
(2t+2)
-strong planar modulator in
G-Z_{0}
.

When we can no longer find such a set
Z_{0}\subseteq Z
, we stop the recursion and return
G
and
X^{\prime}=Z
.
By a counting argument, the number of connected components in
G-Z
with at least 3 neighbors cannot be larger than
|Z|^{3}\cdot(k+2)=\mathcal{O}(k^{49}t^{48})
, which gives a bound on the fragmentation of
Z
.
∎

6
Reducing the radial diameter

From now on we work with an extended modulator so that
G\langle C\rangle
is planar for each connected component
C
of
G-X
.
We will consider a boundaried plane graph
H
given by a plane embedding of
G\langle C\rangle
; we begin with an arbitrary embedding.
We can describe modifications to the graph
G
by modifications to
H
as every vertex or edge in
H
represents a vertex or edge in
G
.
To make the arguments clear, we avoid modifying vertices from
\partial(H)
or edges incident to
\partial(H)
, so no such modification to
H
affects other parts of the graph
G
outside
C
.

In order to replace
H
with a smaller boundaried graph
H^{\prime}
, which approximately preserves its structure, we are going to reduce the task to the case where the vertices of
\partial(H)
are embedded in a convenient way.
In particular, we are interested in the case there
\partial(H)
is located on a single face of
H
.
As a step in this direction, we first reduce the radial diameter of
H
to
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
One obstacle towards this is given by the existence of a large subset
X\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
, with a small (constant) boundary, that separates some vertices from
\partial(H)
in
H
.
We are going to compress such subsets using the technique of protrusion replacement.
This technique will also come in useful in the final part of the proof.

6.1
Protrusion replacement

An
r
-protrusion in a graph
G
is a vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
, such that
|\partial_{G}(X)|\leq r
and
\text{tw}(G[X])\leq r
.
For a vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
with
|\partial_{G}(X)|=r
, a bijection
\lambda\colon[r]\to\partial_{G}(X)
, and a labeled
r
-boundaried graph
H
, the act of replacing
X
by
H
through
\lambda
means (1) creating labeled
r
-boundaried graphs
H^{X}=(G[X],\partial_{G}(X),\lambda)
,
H^{V(G)\setminus X}=(G[(V(G)\setminus X)\cup\partial_{G}(X)],\partial_{G}(X),\lambda)
, and (2)
replacing
G=H^{V(G)\setminus X}\oplus H^{X}
by
G^{\prime}=H^{V(G)\setminus X}\oplus H
.
The graph
H^{X}
is introduced above only for the sake of giving a more detailed view on the process.
We say that
G^{\prime}
is obtained from
G
by replacing a set
X\subseteq V(G)
if there exist
H,\lambda
so that
X
is replaced by
H
through
\lambda
.

The standard protrusion replacement technique
[
BFL+16
,
FLMS12
]
is aimed at reducing large
r
-protrusions, where
r=\mathcal{O}(1)
, while preserving the optimum value of the instance for the considered optimization problem.
Since we want to perform lifting of a potentially non-optimal solution, we use the lossless variant of the technique which provides us with such a mechanism.
The definition below can be instantiated for any

min-CMSO
(or
max-CMSO
)
vertex subset problem
\Pi
.
In such a problem we are given a graph
G

and the objective is to find a set
S\subseteq V(G)
minimizing
|S|
such that the
predicate
P_{\Pi}(G,S)
, expressible in Counting Monadic Second-Order (
CMSO
) logic, is satisfied.
For our purposes it is only relevant that this definition captures the
Vertex planarization
problem.
We specify the labeling
\lambda
directly in the definition whereas the formulation in
[
FLMS20
]
assumes an implicit labeling on the boundary of
X
.

Definition 6.1

(Lossless Protrusion Replacer
[
FLMS12
,
FLMS20
]
)
.

A lossless protrusion replacer for a
min-CMSO

vertex subset problem
\Pi
is a family of algorithms, with one algorithm for every
constant
r
. The
r
-th algorithm has the following specifications. There exists a constant
\gamma(r)

such that given an instance
G
and an
r
-protrusion
X
in
G
containing at least
\gamma(r)
vertices,
|\partial_{G}(X)|=r^{\prime}\leq r
, the
algorithm runs in polynomial time and outputs an instance
G^{\prime}
with the following properties.

•

G^{\prime}
is obtained from
G
by replacing
(G[X],\partial_{G}(X))
by a labeled
r^{\prime}
-boundaried graph
H
with less than
\gamma(r)
vertices, through some labeling
\lambda\colon[r^{\prime}]\to\partial_{G}(X)
, and thus
V(G^{\prime})<V(G)
.

•

\mathsf{OPT}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{OPT}(G)
.

•

There is an algorithm that runs in polynomial time and, given a feasible solution
S^{\prime}

to
G^{\prime}
,
outputs a set
X^{*}\subseteq X
such that
S=(S^{\prime}\setminus V(H))\cup X^{*}
is a feasible solution to
G
and

|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}(G)-\mathsf{OPT}(G^{\prime})
.

In the original statement, the lossless protrusion replacer is defined to run in time
O(|X|)
.
This is important if we aim at optimizing the running time of the algorithm but requires some care with specifying the data structures for maintaining the decomposition into
r
-protrusion.
For the sake of keeping the presentation simple, we have chosen a weaker specification with just a polynomial running time guarantee.

Lemma 6.2
.

The
Vertex planarization
problem admits a lossless protrusion replacer.
Moreover, the choice of the replacement
H
and the transposition value
\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
depends only on the labeled boundaried graph
(G[X],\partial_{G}(X),\lambda)
.

Proof.

The
Vertex planarization
problem is a special case of
\mathcal{F}
-Minor Deletion
for the family of forbidden minors
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{5},K_{3,3}\}
.
Fomin et al.
[
FLMS12
,
FLMS20
]
have shown that every
min-CMSO
vertex subset problem
\Pi
that is strongly
monotone admits a lossless protrusion replacer.
Without going into details, both conditions hold for
\mathcal{F}
-Minor Deletion
for every family
\mathcal{F}
composed of connected graphs
[
BFL+16
, Lem. 8.4]
.
We remark that the proof of the strong monotonicity property in
[
BFL+16
]
is formulated for the case where the family
\mathcal{F}
contains at least one planar graph, but this condition is not used in this particular proof.
Finally, by examining the proof of
[
FLMS20
, Thm. 9]
it follows that the choice of the replacement
H
and the transposition value
\mathsf{OPT}(G)-\mathsf{OPT}(G^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
depends only on the labeled boundaried graph
(G[X],\partial_{G}(X),\lambda)
and is indifferent to the structure of
G-X
.
∎

In our applications, we need to replace a vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
, such that
G[X]
is planar and
|\partial_{G}(X)|\leq r
for constant
r
, but the treewidth of
G[X]
is not necessarily bounded.
It is easy to show that for any minimum planar modulator
S
it holds that
|S\cap X|\leq r
, as otherwise the solution
(S\setminus X)\cup\partial_{G}(X)
would be smaller.
By an argument based on grid minors, if
\mathrm{tw}(G[X])
is larger than some function of
r
, then
X
contains a vertex irrelevant for minimum solutions.
As we work with solutions which are not necessarily optimal, we need an additional argument that allows us to assume that
|S\cap X|\leq r
.

Definition 6.3
.

We say that a planar modulator
S
in
G
is
r
-locally optimal
if there are no sets
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
S^{*}\subseteq V(G)\setminus S
such that
|S^{\prime}|>r
,
|S^{*}|\leq r
, and
(S\setminus S^{\prime})\cup S^{*}
is also a planar modulator.

Lemma 6.4
.

Given an
n
-vertex graph
G
with a planar modulator
S\subseteq V(G)
, and an integer
r
, one can find, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(r)}
, an
r
-locally optimal planar modulator
\widehat{S}
of size at most
|S|
.

Proof.

Suppose that
S
is not
r
-locally optimal,
so there are sets
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
S^{*}\subseteq V(G)\setminus S
such that
|S^{\prime}|>r
,
|S^{*}|\leq r
, and
(S\setminus S^{\prime})\cup S^{*}
is also a planar modulator.
Let
S^{\prime\prime}
be an arbitrary subset of
S^{\prime}
of size
r+1
.
Then
(S\setminus S^{\prime\prime})\cup S^{*}
is a superset of
(S\setminus S^{\prime})\cup S^{*}
, so it is also a planar modulator, and it is smaller than
S
.
The algorithm checks all
\mathcal{O}(n^{r+1})
candidates for
S^{\prime\prime}
, and all
\mathcal{O}(rn^{r})
candidates for
S^{*}
.
If for any pair
(S^{\prime\prime},S^{*})
, the set
(S\setminus S^{\prime\prime})\cup S^{*}
is also a planar modulator (this can be checked in time
\mathcal{O}(n+m)

[
HT74
]
), we set
S\leftarrow(S\setminus S^{\prime\prime})\cup S^{*}
and repeat the algorithm.
In every step we decrease the size of
S
,
so after at most
n
steps the process must terminate.
Since at this moment we cannot find any pair
(S^{\prime\prime},S^{*})
that would provide further refinement, the obtained planar modulator must be
r
-locally optimal.
∎

Consider a set
X=G\langle A\rangle
for some
A
, for which
|N_{G}(A)|\leq r
.
The lifting algorithm can use
Lemma

6.4
to find a solution
S
satisfying
|S\cap X|\leq r
.
Therefore any vertex
v\in X
which can be surrounded by
r+3
connected
(v,\partial_{G}(X))
separators is irrelevant for
S
.
We follow the standard argument based on a grid contraction to find such a vertex whenever
\mathrm{tw}(G[X])
is large.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
G
be a graph,
A\subseteq V(G)
, and
G\langle A\rangle
be a
(\leq r)
-boundaried connected planar graph.
If
\text{tw}(G\langle A\rangle)>27(2r+7)(r+1)
, then there exists a
vertex
v\in A
and a sequence of
r+3
connected pairwise-disjoint
(v,N_{G}(A))
-separators in
G\langle A\rangle
.
Such a vertex
v
can be found in polynomial time, when given
G
and
A
.
Furthermore, given
G,A,v
, and a planar modulator
S
in
G-v
, one can find, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(r)}
, a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S|
.

Proof.

We apply
Proposition

4.10
for
t=(2r+7)(r+1)
.
As the treewidth of
G\langle A\rangle
is larger than
27\cdot t
,
we can find, in polynomial time,
a contraction model of
\Gamma_{t}
in
G\langle A\rangle
.
We have
(r+1)^{2}>r
trivially, so this contraction model
contains a
(2r+7)\times(2r+7)
-subgrid without vertices from
N_{G}(A)
.
A
(2r+7)\times(2r+7)
-subgrid contains
r+3
nested connected separators
S^{\prime}_{1},\dots,S^{\prime}_{r+3}
between the central branch set and the rest of the grid.
Let
v
be any vertex from the central branch set of this subgrid
and
S_{i}
be the union of vertices contracted into
S^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in[r+3]
.
By
4.2
,
the sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{r+3}
are nested connected
(v,N_{G}(A))
-separators.
Therefore each set
S_{i}
,
i\in[r+3]
, is
v
-planarizing.

Suppose now that one is given a planar modulator
S
in
G-v
.
By
Lemma

6.4
, one can find, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(r)}
, an
r
-locally optimal planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G-v
of size at most
|S|
.
Observe that
|S^{\prime}\cap A|\leq r
because otherwise
(S^{\prime}\setminus A)\cup N_{G}(A)
would be a smaller planar modulator, contradicting
S^{\prime}
being
r
-locally optimal.
Therefore there exists distinct indices
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in[r+3]

such that
S_{i_{1}},S_{i_{2}},S_{i_{3}}
are disjoint from
S^{\prime}
.
For each
j\in[3]
we have that
R_{G-S^{\prime}}[v,S_{i_{j}}]\subseteq A
induces a planar subgraph of
G-S^{\prime}
.
By the assumption
G-S^{\prime}-v
is planar, so together, by
Lemma

3.17
,
G-S^{\prime}
is planar.
We have thus shown how to efficiently turn a planar modulator in
G-v
into a planar modulator in
G
.
∎

Once we are able to reduce the treewidth of a planar subgraph with a small (constant) boundary, we can use protrusion replacement to reduce its size.
The setting in which we work implies some convenient corollaries about the result of the replacement act, of which we make note in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\gamma
be the function from
Definition

6.1
for the
Vertex planarization
problem,
r
be a constant and
r^{\prime}=27(2r+7)(r+1)
.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given an
n
-vertex graph
G
, a vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
, such that
G\langle A\rangle
is planar and connected,
N_{G}(A)=r
and
|A|\geq\gamma(r^{\prime})
, outputs a graph
G^{\prime}
so the following hold.

1.

G^{\prime}
is obtained from
G
by replacing
N_{G}[A]
with some labeled
r
-boundaried graph
H
through some bijection
\lambda\colon[r]\to N_{G}(A)
.

2.

H

has less than
\gamma(r^{\prime})
vertices.

3.

\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)
.

4.

For any vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus N_{G}[A]
, if
G-X
is planar and connected then
G^{\prime}-X
is planar and connected.

5.

Given
G,G^{\prime}
and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
one can, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(r)}
, turn it into a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

First, we reduce the treewidth of
G\langle A\rangle
to be at most
r^{\prime}
using

Lemma

6.5
.
While
\mathrm{tw}(G\langle A\rangle)>r^{\prime}
, we can find a vertex
v\in A
such that
any planar modulator
S
in
G-v
can be turned, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(r)}
, into a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S|
.
Hence, removing
v
is safe and we can continue the process on the graph
G-v
.
Furthermore, there is at least one connected restricted
(v,N_{G}(A))
-separator in
G\langle A\rangle
so removal of
v
does not disconnect any vertices from
N_{G}(A)
within
G\langle A\rangle
.
If some vertices get disconnected from
N_{G}(A)
then they form a planar connected component in
G
, which can be discarded.
Therefore, we maintain connectivity of the graph
G\langle A\rangle
.
Let
G_{0}
be obtained from
G
after the exhaustive application of
Lemma

6.5
and
A_{0}\subseteq A
be obtained from
A
by removing the irrelevant vertices.
Then the set
N_{G_{0}}[A_{0}]
forms an
r^{\prime}
-protrusion. We can treat
G_{0}
as obtained from
G
by replacing
A
.
Note that
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)
because the lifting algorithm can lift any solution losslessly.
If
|N_{G_{0}}[A_{0}]|<\gamma(r^{\prime})
, then we are done.

Suppose that
|N_{G_{0}}[A_{0}]|\geq\gamma(r^{\prime})
.
By
Lemma

6.2
,
the
Vertex planarization
problem admits a lossless protrusion replacer,
so we can replace
N_{G_{0}}[A_{0}]
with a labeled
r
-boundaried graph
H
, with at most
\gamma(r^{\prime})
vertices, through some labeling
\lambda
,
obtaining a graph
G^{\prime}
.
From the definition of a lossless protrusion replacer
we have that
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)
and any planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
can be turned, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator
S_{0}
in
G_{0}
of size at most
|S^{\prime}|+\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})-\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
.
Thanks to
Lemma

6.5
, we can compute a planar modulator
S
in
G
of size at most
|S_{0}|
, in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(r)}
.

We argue that actually
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})
.
From
Lemma

6.2
we know that the choice of the replacement
H
and the transposition value
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})-\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
depends only on the boundaried graph
G_{0}^{A_{0},\lambda}=(G_{0}[N_{G_{0}}[A_{0}]],N_{G_{0}}(A_{0}),\lambda)
.
Let
I_{r}
be a labeled
r
-boundaried graph with only the boundary vertices and no edges.
Consider graphs
J_{0},J^{\prime}
obtained by gluing
I_{r}
to respectively
G_{0}^{A_{0},\lambda}
and
H
(equivalently, one can regard this operation as taking the underlying non-boundaried graphs).
The graph
J_{0}
is planar so
\mathrm{mvp}(J_{0})=0
.
Because
\mathrm{mvp}(J^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(J_{0})
we get that
\mathrm{mvp}(J^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(J_{0})
.
Since the transposition value depends only on
G_{0}^{A_{0},\lambda}
we infer that
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})-\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(J_{0})-\mathrm{mvp}(J^{\prime})=0
.
If the graph
H
contains a connected component disconnected from
\partial(H)
then the argument above implies that this component must be planar.
Therefore if such a component appears, it can be safely removed as a planar component in
G_{0}
.

Finally we show that for any vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)\setminus N_{G}[A]
, if
G-X
is planar and connected then
G^{\prime}-X
is planar and connected.
The first modification step does not affect these conditions so
G_{0}-X
is planar and connected.
Let
J_{0}^{X}

be the labeled boundaried graph for which it holds
G_{0}-X=J_{0}^{X}\oplus G_{0}^{A_{0},\lambda}
and
G^{\prime}-X=J_{0}^{X}\oplus H
.
Similarly as before, we use the fact that the relation between
G_{0}^{A_{0},\lambda}
and
H
is indifferent to the boundaried graph being glued to them.
Therefore
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime}-X)=\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0}-X)=0
, so
G^{\prime}-X
is
planar.
Now suppose that
G^{\prime}-X
is not connected.
Because we have removed all the connected components of
H
with empty intersection with
\partial(H)
, this means that there exist
u,v\in N_{G_{0}}(A_{0})=\partial(H)
which are connected in
G_{0}\langle A_{0}\rangle
but not in
H
.
Let
\widehat{K_{5}}
denote the graph
K_{5}
with one edge removed.
Consider graphs
R_{0},R^{\prime}
obtained by gluing
\widehat{K_{5}}
to respectively
G_{0}^{A_{0},\lambda}
and
H
so that the vertices
u,v
are glued to the non-adjacent pair in
\widehat{K_{5}}
.
Since there is a
(u,v)
-path in
G\langle A\rangle
, the graph
R_{0}
contains
K_{5}
as a minor.
On the other hand,
u,v
belong to different connected components of
H
, so every biconnected component of
R^{\prime}
is planar and so
R^{\prime}
is planar.
We get that
\mathrm{mvp}(R_{0})>\mathrm{mvp}(R^{\prime})=0
, which contradicts the assumption that such vertices
u,v
exist.
The claim follows.
∎

6.2
Outerplanar decomposition

In order to conveniently analyze plane graphs with large radial diameter
we take advantage of the concept of a decomposition into outerplanarity layers used by Jansen et al.
[
JPvL19
]
in their work on kernelization for
Multiway cut
on planar graphs.
We refer to the definitions from the full version
[
JPvL18
]
of this article.

Definition 6.7

(
[
JPvL18
, Definition 5.1]
)
.

Let
G
be a plane graph. The outerplanarity layers of
G
form a partition of
V(G)
into
L_{1},\ldots,L_{m}
defined recursively. The vertices incident with the outer face of
G
belong to layer
L_{1}
. If
v\in V(G)
lies on the outer face of the plane subgraph obtained from
G
by removing
L_{1},\ldots,L_{i}
, then
v
belongs to layer
L_{i+1}
. For a vertex
v\in V(G)
, the unique index
i\in[m]
for which
v\in L_{i}
is the
outerplanarity index
of
v
and denoted
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{G}(v)
.

A plane graph is
k
-outerplanar if this process partitions it into exactly
k
non-empty layers. A graph is
k
-outerplanar if it admits a
k
-outerplanar embedding.

Definition 6.8

(
[
JPvL18
, Definition 5.2]
)
.

For a plane graph
G
, let
T
be the simple graph obtained by simultaneously contracting all edges
uv
whose endpoints belong to the same outerplanarity layer, discarding loops and parallel edges. For a node
u\in V(T)
, let
\kappa(u)\subseteq V(G)
denote the vertex set of
G
whose contraction resulted in
u
.
Let
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{G}(u)
denote the outerplanarity index that is shared by all nodes in
\kappa(u)
.

We make note of several properties of this definition.

Lemma 6.9

(
[
JPvL18
, Lemma 5.3]
)
.

For a connected plane graph
G
and its outerplanar decomposition
(T,\kappa)
based on the outerplanarity layers
L_{1},\ldots,L_{m}
, the following holds:

1.

T
is a tree.

2.

For any node
u\in V(T)
, the graph
G[\kappa(u)]
is connected.

3.

If
x,y
are distinct vertices of
T
, then for any internal node
z
of the unique
(x,y)
-path in
T
, the set
\kappa(z)
is a restricted
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-separator in
G
.

4.

There is a unique node
x_{1}\in V(T)
such that
\kappa(x_{1})=L_{1}
.

5.

Root
T
at vertex
x_{1}
. If
u\in V(T)
is a child of
p\in V(T)
, then
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{G}(u)=1+\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{G}(p)
.

We call the pair
(T,\kappa)
, where
T
is the tree rooted at
x_{1}
accordingly to
Lemma

6.9
, the
outerplanar decomposition
of
G
.
Note that it is uniquely defined for a given connected plane graph
G
.
Let
\delta_{T}
denote the shortest path distance on the tree
T
.
For distinct
x,y\in V(T)
we define
T^{P}_{x,y}
to be the unique
(x,y)
-path in
T
and for non-adjacent
x,y\in V(T)
we define
T_{x,y}
to be the connected component of
T-\{x,y\}
containing the internal vertices of
T^{P}_{x,y}
.
For a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
we use the notation
\kappa(T^{\prime})
to denote the union of
\{\kappa(t)\mid t\in V(T^{\prime})\}
.
Note that
N_{G}(\kappa(T_{x,y}))\subseteq\kappa(x)\cup\kappa(y)
.

Observation 6.10

(
[
JPvL18
, Observation 5.4]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph and
(T,\kappa)
be its outerplanar decomposition.
If
u,v\in V(G)
share a face, then either there is
x\in V(T)
such that
u,v\in\kappa(x)

or there are
x,y\in V(T)

which are adjacent in
T
and
u\in\kappa(x),v\in\kappa(y)
.
Conversely, if
x
is a child of
y
in
T

and
v\in\kappa(x)
, then there exists
u\in\kappa(y)
, so that
u,v
share a face.

This observation allows us to make a link between the depth of the outerplanar decomposition and the radial diameter of the graph.
We define the depth of a rooted tree as the longest root-leaf distance plus 1 (so a singleton tree has depth 1).

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph and
(T,\kappa)
be its outerplanar decomposition.
If the maximal depth in
T
is
d
, then the radial diameter of
G
is at most
2d-1
.

Proof.

By
6.10
any vertex in
L_{i+1}
shares a face with a vertex from
L_{i}
.
Let
u,v\in V(G)
be any pair of vertices.
We have
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{G}(u),\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{G}(v)\leq d
so they are at radial distance at most
d-1
from some vertices from
L_{1}
which are at radial distance 1, hence
d_{G}(u,v)\leq 2d-1
.
∎

Since
T
is a contraction of
G
, when a set
S\subseteq V(G)
separates
\kappa(x)
from
\kappa(y)
, it also separates
\kappa(x)
from the image of the entire subtree behind
y
.
We make note of this observation for the case where we separate
\kappa(x)
from two directions at once.

Observation 6.12
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph and
(T,\kappa)
be its outerplanar decomposition.
Let
x,y,z\in V(T)
be distinct pairwise non-adjacent vertices such that
z
lies on the unique
(x,y)
-path in
T
.
Suppose that
S\subseteq V(G)
is a restricted
(\kappa(z),\kappa(x)\cup\kappa(y))
-separator.
Then the connected component of
G-S
containing
\kappa(z)
is contained in
\kappa(T_{x,y})
.

We also state
a strengthening of
Lemma

6.9
(
3
) which says that the separators within outerplanarity layers are in fact cycles.

Observation 6.13
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph,
(T,\kappa)
be its outerplanar decomposition, and
x,y,z\in V(T)
be distinct.
If
z
lies on the unique
(x,y)
-path in
T
, then there is a cycle
C
in
G
such that
V(C)\subseteq\kappa(z)
and one of
\kappa(x),\kappa(y)
lies entirely in the interior of
C
and the other one in the exterior of
C
.

6.3
Outerplanarity layers and vertex-disjoint paths

We will be working with an outerplanar decomposition
(T,\kappa)
of a plane graph
G
and families of internally vertex-disjoint paths connecting two outerplanarity layers, so that one is drawn inside the other one.
First we show that paths in such a family can be assumed to cross each intermediate outerplanarity layer only once.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph,
L_{1}
be the set of vertices lying on the outer face of
G
, and
F\subseteq V(G)\setminus L_{1}
induce a connected subgraph of
G
.
Suppose that
u,v\in L_{1}
are non-adjacent and connected by a path
P
with all its internal vertices disjoint from
(L_{1}\cup F)
.
Then there exists a
(u,v)
-path
P^{\prime}
such that
V(P^{\prime})\subseteq L_{1}
and the internal vertices of
P^{\prime}
belong
to a connected component of
G-V(P)
which is disjoint from
F
.

Proof.

As
u,v
are non-adjacent, the interior of
P
is non-empty.
Since
G[L_{1}]
is connected, there is a
(u,v)
-path within
L_{1}
, which is internally disjoint from
P
.
Therefore
u,v
belong to a common biconnected component
B
of
G
and the subgraph
B
contains a cycle
C_{1}
such that
V(C_{1})=L_{1}\cap V(B)
.
Removing non-adjacent
u,v
from
C_{1}
separates
V(C_{1})
into two non-empty sets
S^{1},S^{2}
, which end up in different connected components of
G-V(P)
since
u
and
v
lie on the outer face.
Since
G[F]
is connected, it is fully contained in one such component.
We can thus use either
S^{1}
or
S^{2}
to make the desired path
P^{\prime}
.
∎

For a path
P
and a vertex set
X
, a segment of
P
in
X
is an inclusion-wise maximal subpath
P^{\prime}
of
P
such that
V(P^{\prime})\subseteq X
.
We define
S(P,X)
to be the set of segments of
P
in
X
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Consider a connected plane graph
G
,
its outerplanar decomposition
(T,\kappa)
,
and non-adjacent
x,y\in V(T)
such that
x
is an ancestor of
y
.
Suppose we are given a family of internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths
\mathcal{P}
.
Then we can compute, in polynomial time, a family
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths such that
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|=|\mathcal{P}|
and for each
P\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
z\in T^{P}_{x,y}
it holds that
|S(P,\kappa(z))|=1
.

Proof.

By replacing each
P\in\mathcal{P}
by the
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-subpath between the last visit to
\kappa(x)
and the first visit to
\kappa(y)
, we can assume that the internal vertices of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
belong to
\kappa(T_{x,y})
.
For a family of internally vertex-disjoint
\kappa(x),\kappa(y)
-paths
\mathcal{P}
we define the measure
\mu(\mathcal{P})=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{z\in T^{P}_{x,y}}|S(P,\kappa(z))|
.
While
\mathcal{P}
does not satisfy the claim, we are going to refine some path from
\mathcal{P}
to decrease the measure
\mu(\mathcal{P})
.

Let
x^{\prime}\in V(T)

be the vertex on
T^{P}_{x,y}
which is closest to
x
among those for which there exists a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
so that
|S(P,\kappa(x^{\prime}))|>1
.
Note that
x^{\prime}
is an ancestor of
y
.
Let us consider a plane graph
G^{\prime}
obtained by removing from
G
all the vertices from
\kappa(z)
over all
z\in V(T)
outside the subtree of
T
rooted at
x^{\prime}
.
Note that
G^{\prime}
is connected and
\kappa(x^{\prime})
is the set of vertices lying on the outer face of
G^{\prime}
.
By the choice of
x^{\prime}
, for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
there is exactly one segment of
P
in
V(G^{\prime})
: otherwise some path would have multiple segments in
\kappa(x^{\prime\prime})
, for
x^{\prime\prime}
being a parent of
x^{\prime}
, which is a superset of
N_{G}(V(G^{\prime}))
.
Let
P^{\prime}
denote this segment of
P
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be a path for which
|S(P,\kappa(x^{\prime})|>1
.
Then there exist vertices
u,v\in V(P^{\prime})\cap\kappa(x^{\prime})
and a
(u,v)
-subpath
P_{u,v}
of
P^{\prime}
whose internal vertices do not belong to
\kappa(x^{\prime})
.
If
u,v
are adjacent, then we can shortcut
P
by replacing
P_{u,v}
with the edge
(u,v)
thus decreasing
\mu(\mathcal{P})
.
Suppose that
u,v
are non-adjacent.
We apply
Lemma

6.14
to the graph
G^{\prime}
with the outer face lying on
\kappa(x^{\prime})
, vertices
u,v
, path
P_{u,v}
, and
F=\kappa(y)
, which induces a connected subgraph of
G^{\prime}
.
We obtain a
(u,v)
-path
P^{\prime}_{u,v}
such that
V(P^{\prime}_{u,v})\subseteq\kappa(x^{\prime})
and the internal vertices of
P^{\prime}_{u,v}
belong
to a connected component of
G^{\prime}-V(P_{u,v})
which is disjoint from
\kappa(y)
.
Since all the other paths from
\{P^{\prime}\mid P\in\mathcal{P}\}
connect
\kappa(x^{\prime})
to
\kappa(y)
in
G^{\prime}
, they cannot have any vertices in this component of
G^{\prime}-V(P_{u,v})
.
Therefore by replacing
P_{u,v}
with
P^{\prime}_{u,v}
in
P

we obtain a new family of internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths of the same size for which the measure
\mu
is lower than
\mu(\mathcal{P})
.

We apply this refinement exhaustively as long as there exist
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
z\in T^{P}_{x,y}
with
|S(P,\kappa(z))|>1
.
It is clear that the refinement step can be performed in polynomial time.
At each step the value of
\mu
decreases so at some point the process terminates and gives the desired path family.
∎

It is important that the sets considered in
Lemma

6.15
are given by the consecutive outerplanarity layers.
For other choice of connected separators, such a claim might not be true.

By inspecting the outerplanar decomposition
(T,\kappa)
of an
r
-boundaried plane graph
H
, we can
find the set of nodes in
T
whose images cover the boundary
\partial(H)
, and then mark the LCA closure of this set (recall
Definition

3.8
), thus marking at most
2r
nodes.
We consider the nodes which were not marked and we want to compress the graph whenever there is a long path of non-marked nodes.
This translates to a situation
where one group
T_{1}
of relevant vertices (that is, located in a marked bag) lies on one outerplanarity layer, there is a deep well of nested outerplanarity layers, and another group
T_{2}
of relevant vertices is located inside the well.
We want to say that only the
\mathcal{O}(k)
layers closest to
T_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}(k)
layers closest to
T_{2}
are important and the middle layers can be compressed.

Let
S
be some solution and
suppose that there at least 4 internally vertex-disjoint
(T_{1},T_{2})
-paths in
H-S
.
We need an argument that in this case, for any vertex
v
in the middle layers that does not lie on any of these four paths, we can draw two vertex-disjoint cycles around
v
that separate
v
from all the relevant vertices, and in particular from
\partial(H)
.
We begin with a topological argument that such cycles exist.
We do not rely on
Lemma

6.15
and give the argument in a general form.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
G
be a plane graph,
C_{1},\dots,C_{6}
be vertex-disjoint cycles in
G
, so that
C_{i+1}
is drawn inside
C_{i}
for
i\in[5]
, and let
P_{A},P_{B},P_{C},P_{D}
be vertex-disjoint
(C_{1},C_{6})
-paths.
Suppose that a vertex
v\in V(G)
with
v\not\in\bigcup_{i\in[4]}V(P_{i})
lies in the intersection of the proper interior of
C_{3}
and the proper exterior of
C_{4}
.
Then there exist two vertex-disjoint cycles in
G
which are nested with respect to
v
and separate
v
from
V(C_{1})\cup V(C_{6})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, by shortcutting the paths if needed, we may assume that the paths
P_{A},\ldots,P_{D}
do not contain any vertex from
V(C_{1})\cup V(C_{6})
in their interior.

For subsets of indices
I\subseteq[6]
and
J\subseteq\{A,B,C,D\}
, let
G^{J}_{I}
be the plane subgraph of
G
obtained by restricting the drawing of
G
to only the vertices and edges which lie on a cycle
C_{i}
with
i\in I
or a path
P_{X}
with
X\in J
. For ease of presentation, we abbreviate
\{A,B,C,D\}
by
[D]
.

Since
v
does not lie on any of the four paths, while it lies in the intersection of the proper interior of
C_{3}
and proper exterior of
C_{4}
, it follows that the image of
v
does not intersect the drawing of
G_{3,4}^{[D]}
. Let
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
denote the face of
G_{3,4}^{[D]}
containing the image of
v
. The face
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
is bounded since
G_{3,4}^{[D]}
contains
C_{3}
which has
v
in its proper interior, and due to the nesting of the cycles no vertex on
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
belongs to
C_{5}
or
C_{6}
. Similarly, since
v
lies in the proper exterior of
C_{4}
, face
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
does not contain any vertex of
C_{1}
or
C_{2}
. Since
G_{3,4}^{[D]}
is a connected graph that does not have any articulation points, the boundary of face
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
is a simple cycle
C^{\prime}
which contains
v
in its proper interior. As observed above,
C^{\prime}
is disjoint from
C_{1}\cup C_{2}\cup C_{5}\cup C_{6}
. Note that since
G_{3,4}^{[D]}
contains cycles
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
, the nesting of the cycles and placement of
v
in the interior of
C_{3}
and exterior of
C_{4}
ensures that
V(C^{\prime})
separates
v
from
V(C_{1})\cup V(C_{6})
.

Figure 3
:
Schematic representation of the graph
G^{[D]}_{[6]}
. In this example, the face of
G_{3,4}^{[D]}
containing
v
is highlighted with dashed white lines. Its boundary cycle forms
C^{\prime}
when applying the lemma for
v
. It contains segments of
C_{3}
,
C_{4}
, and the single path
P_{B}
. Additionally, the face of
G_{2,5}^{[A,C]}
containing
v
is shaded. It is bounded by segments of
C_{2}
,
C_{5}
, and the paths
P_{A}
and
P_{C}
. Its boundary cycle forms
C^{\prime\prime}
when applying the lemma for
v
.

From the observations above, we get that
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
is also a face in
G_{[6]}^{[D]}
.
Suppose that the boundary of
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
intersects at least three paths
P_{X},P_{Y},P_{Z}\in\{P_{A},\ldots,P_{D}\}
.
Note that one can always add a vertex to a drawing inside some face and make it adjacent to all vertices on the boundary of this face.
Consider a plane graph
G^{\prime}
obtained from
G_{[6]}^{[D]}
by inserting three new vertices
v_{1},v_{6},v_{F}
adjacent respectively to all vertices of
C_{1}
,
C_{6}
and
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
.
The sets
V(P_{X}),V(P_{Y}),V(P_{Z})
,
\{v_{1}\}
,
\{v_{6}\}
,
\{v_{F}\}
would form a minor model of
K_{3,3}
which gives a contradiction.

Therefore the boundary of
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
intersects at most two paths
P_{X},P_{Y}\in\{P_{A},\ldots,P_{D}\}
; see Figure
3
for an illustration. Note that it is possible that the boundary intersects only a single path. Let
P_{R},P_{S}
be two paths not intersecting the boundary of
F_{3,4}^{[D]}
.

In the graph
G_{2,5}^{R,S}
, the face
F_{2,5}^{R,S}
containing the image of
v
is again bounded. Let
C^{\prime\prime}
denote the cycle bounding
F_{2,5}^{R,S}
, which is simple since
G_{2,5}^{R,S}
is connected and has no articulation points. By the nesting of the cycles,
C^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from
C_{1}
and
C_{6}
. By definition of the graph
G_{2,5}^{R,S}
, the cycle
C^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from
V(C_{3})\cup V(C_{4})
, and therefore
V(C^{\prime})\cap V(C^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
. Since
G_{2,5}^{R,S}
contains cycles
C_{2}
and
C_{5}
, while the nesting and placement of
v
ensures that
v
is in the proper interior of
C_{2}
and proper exterior of
C_{5}
, it follows that
C^{\prime\prime}
separates
v
from
V(C_{1})\cup V(C_{6})
. Since both
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
have
v
in their interior and
C_{1}
in they exterior, they are nested. This concludes the proof.
∎

6.4
Compressing the outerplanarity layers

We focus on the case where

x,y\in V(T)
are some marked vertices,
x
is an ancestor of
y
and
T_{x,y}
contains no marked vertices (and thus its image contains no boundary vertices).
If
\delta_{T}(x,y)
