is large, we want to preserve just
\mathcal{O}(k)
outerplanarity layers closest to
\kappa(x)
,
\mathcal{O}(k)
layers closest to
\kappa(y)
, and in the middle layers we want to preserve only the maximal family internally of vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths, discarding the remaining vertices and edges.

We need to argue for the backward safeness of such a graph modification.
Consider a solution
S
such that
|S\cap\kappa(T_{x,y})|\leq k
.
If
H-S
contains at least 4 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths, we can surround the modified areas with two cycles using
Lemma

6.16
and argue that inserting them back does not affect planarity.
Otherwise we want to say that
S\cap\kappa(T_{x,y})
is sufficiently large so we can charge these vertices to “pay” for additional vertex removals which separate
\kappa(x)
from
\kappa(y)
in
H
.
The following statement remains correct if we assume the existence of just 4 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths, instead of 6, but with a worse approximation factor of the lifting algorithm.
On the other hand, factor 2 can be improved to
(1+\varepsilon)
if we assume that even more paths exist but for the sake of keeping the presentation clear we do not optimize this constant.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
G
be a graph,
A\subseteq V(G)
,
H
be a connected boundaried plane graph given by some plane embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
,

(T,\kappa)
be the outerplanar decomposition of
H
, and
k
be an integer.
Consider vertices
x,y\in V(T)
such that
x
is an ancestor of
y
,
\delta_{T}(x,y)>4k+11
,
\kappa(T_{x,y})\cap\partial(H)=\emptyset
,
and there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths in the graph
H\langle\kappa(T_{x,y})\rangle
.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given all the above, returns a vertex set
V_{D}\subseteq\kappa(T_{x,y})
and an edge set
E_{D}\subseteq E_{G}(\kappa(T_{x,y}),\kappa(T_{x,y}))
so that the following hold.

1.

For each vertex
v\in\kappa(y)
there exists a vertex
u\in\kappa(x)
so that the radial distance between
u
and
v
in
H^{\prime}=(H\setminus E_{D})-V_{D}
is at most
4k+11
.

2.

The boundaried graph
H^{\prime}=(H\setminus E_{D})-V_{D}
is connected.

3.

Given a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}=(G\setminus E_{D})-V_{D}
, such that
|S^{\prime}\cap\kappa(T_{x,y})|\leq k
, one can turn it, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator
S
in
G
such that
|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+2\cdot|S^{\prime}\cap\kappa(T_{x,y})|
and
S\setminus\kappa(T_{x,y})=S^{\prime}\setminus\kappa(T_{x,y})
.

Proof.

We set
D=\kappa(T_{x,y})
,
T_{1}=\kappa(x)
,
T_{2}=\kappa(y)
, and
J
be the boundaried plane graph given by the subgraph of
H
induced by
D\cup T_{1}\cup T_{2}
and the boundary
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
.
Note that
N_{G}(D)\subseteq T_{1}\cup T_{2}
and
T_{1},T_{2}
are non-adjacent.
Let
d
be the minimum size of a restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
J
.
By Menger’s theorem there exist
d
internally vertex-disjoint
(T_{1},T_{2})
-paths in
J
:
P_{1},\dots P_{d}
.
By the assumption
d\geq 6
.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

6.15
we can assume that each path
P_{i}
has only one segment in each
\kappa(z)
for
z\in T^{P}_{x,y}
.

Let
q_{1}\in V(T)
be the vertex on path
T^{P}_{x,y}
with distance
\delta_{T}(x,q_{1})
exactly
2k+5
.
Similarly,
q_{2}\in V(T)
is the vertex on path
T^{P}_{x,y}
with distance
\delta_{T}(y,q_{2})
exactly
2k+5
.
We define
Q=T_{q_{1},q_{2}}
.
By the assumption
Q
is non-empty.
Let
V_{Q}=\kappa(Q)
; then
V_{Q}
is a restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
H
and also in
J
.
We define
E_{D}
to be the set of edges with at least one endpoint in
V_{Q}
, which do not belong to any path
P_{i}
for
i\in[d]
.
The set
V_{D}
is the set of vertices in
V_{Q}
which become isolated in
H\setminus E_{D}
. See Figure
4
for an illustration.
Let
G^{\prime},H^{\prime},J^{\prime}
denote the graphs obtained respectively from
G,H,J
after removing the edges from
E_{D}
and vertices from
V_{D}
.
We have that
G^{\prime}=G_{\partial}[\overline{A}]\oplus H^{\prime}
. Note that
H^{\prime}
is connected and
J^{\prime}
still admits
d
internally vertex-disjoint
(T_{1},T_{2})
-paths.

We will show that any planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
with bounded intersection with
D

can be lifted to a planar modulator in
G
, by possibly increasing its size slightly.
We consider two cases.
First, if removing vertices from
S^{\prime}\cap D
preserves at least 4 internally vertex-disjoint
(T_{1},T_{2})
-paths in
J^{\prime}
,
then we show that any region in which the graph modifications occurred can be surrounded by 2 cycles separating this region from the boundary of
H
.
Then the planarity criterion can be applied to argue that
S^{\prime}
remains a planar modulator after undoing the graph modifications.
Otherwise, the set
S^{\prime}\cap D
is sufficiently large so we can charge it with adding to
S^{\prime}
a restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
J
.
Then each leftover connected component in
J
is disconnected from either
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
, which again allows us to apply the planarity criterion to justify undoing the graph modifications.

Figure 4
:
Schematic view of the proof of
Lemma

6.17
. The orange paths connect vertices from
T_{1}
(on the outside) with vertices from
T_{2}
(inside).
The middle layer corresponds to the area between
\kappa(q_{1})
and
\kappa(q_{2})
.
The paths can be assumed to cross the middle layer only once due to
Lemma

6.15
.
All the edges and vertices marked gray in the middle layer are being removed; these are the sets
E_{D}
and
V_{D}
.

Claim 6.18
.

Let
S^{\prime}
be a planar modulator in
G^{\prime}
such that
|S^{\prime}\cap D|\leq k
and the minimum size of a restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
J^{\prime}-(S^{\prime}\cap D)
is at least 4.
Then
S^{\prime}
is also a planar modulator in
G
.

Proof.

By Menger’s theorem, there exist 4 internally vertex-disjoint
(T_{1},T_{2})
-paths in
J^{\prime}-(S^{\prime}\cap D)
:
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},R_{4}
.
We will transform the graph
J^{\prime}-S^{\prime}
back into
J-S^{\prime}
by undoing the vertex and edge removals so that
G^{\prime}-S^{\prime}
is transformed into
G-S^{\prime}
, while maintaining planarity of the graph.
First, inserting an isolated vertex from
V_{D}
does not affect planarity of a graph.
Now consider a single insertion of an edge
e\in E_{D}
.
If both endpoints of
e
are disconnected from
(T_{1}\cup T_{2})\setminus S^{\prime}
in
J^{\prime\prime}-S^{\prime}
then this connected component of
G^{\prime\prime}
after the modification
is contained in
D
, which induces a planar graph by the assumption
(recall that we assumed that
D\cap\partial(H)=\emptyset
).

Suppose otherwise.
Let
v\in V_{Q}\setminus S^{\prime}
be an endpoint of
e
.
Since
|S^{\prime}|\leq k
, there exist vertices
q^{\prime}_{1},\dots,q^{\prime}_{6}\in V(T)
lying on this order on the path
T^{P}_{x,y}

such that
q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2},q^{\prime}_{3}
appear before
Q
(counting from
x
) and
q^{\prime}_{4},q^{\prime}_{5},q^{\prime}_{6}
appear after
Q
such that
\kappa(q^{\prime}_{j})\cap S^{\prime}=\emptyset
for
j\in[6]
.
By
6.13
for each
j\in[6]
there is a cycle
C^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq\kappa(q^{\prime}_{j})
,
so that
C^{\prime}_{j+1}
lies in the interior of
C^{\prime}_{j}
in
J^{\prime}-S^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,
v
lies in the intersection of the proper interior of
C_{3}
and the proper exterior of
C_{4}
in
J^{\prime}-S^{\prime}

and each of the paths
R_{i}
,
i\in[4]
, contains a
(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{6})
-subpath
R^{\prime}_{i}
so that these subpaths are vertex-disjoint (not just internally vertex-disjoint).
This setting allows us to apply
Lemma

6.16
to obtain that there exist cycles
C_{1},C_{2}
in
J^{\prime}-S^{\prime}
which are nested with respect to
v
and separate
v
from
C^{\prime}_{1}\cup C^{\prime}_{6}
and thus from
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
.
Therefore
R_{H^{\prime}-S^{\prime}}[v,C_{i}]\cap\partial(H)=\emptyset
for
i\in[2]
and these cycles are
v
-planarizing.
By
Lemma

3.16
we infer that if
(G^{\prime}-S^{\prime})\setminus e
is planar then also
G^{\prime}-S^{\prime}
is planar.
This allows us to insert back all the edges from
E_{D}
into
G^{\prime}-S^{\prime}
while maintaining the planarity of the graph and show that
G-S^{\prime}
is planar.
The claim follows.
∎

Claim 6.19
.

Let
S^{\prime}
be a planar modulator in
G^{\prime}
such that
|S^{\prime}\cap D|\leq k
and
there exists a restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
J^{\prime}-(S^{\prime}\cap D)
of size at most
3
.
Let
R
be a minimum-size restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
J
.
Then
S=S^{\prime}\cup R
is also a planar modulator in
G
and
|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+2\cdot|S^{\prime}\cap D|
.

Proof.

Recall that we have preserved the paths
P_{1},\dots,P_{d}
during the graph modification and
J^{\prime}
still admits
d\geq 6
internally vertex-disjoint
(T_{1},T_{2})
-paths.
The size of
S^{\prime}\cap D
is at least
d-3\geq 3
because removing one vertex from
D
can decrease the flow between
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
(which are disjoint from
D
) by at most one.
We have
|R|=d\leq|S^{\prime}\cap D|+3\leq 2\cdot|S^{\prime}\cap D|

and so
|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+|R|\leq|S^{\prime}|+2\cdot|S^{\prime}\cap D|
, as intended.
Also,
|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+|R|\leq|S^{\prime}|+|S^{\prime}\cap D|+3\leq 2k+3
.
By the definition of
Q
, there exist vertices
q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2},q^{\prime}_{3},q^{\prime}_{4}\in V(T)
lying in this order on the path
T^{P}_{x,y}

such that
q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2}
appear before
Q
(counting from
x
) and
q^{\prime}_{3},q^{\prime}_{4}
appear after the subpath
Q
such that
\kappa(q^{\prime}_{j})\cap S=\emptyset
for
j\in[4]
.

We will transform the graph
J^{\prime}-S
back into
J-S
by undoing the vertex and edge removals so that
G^{\prime}-S
is transformed into
G-S
, while maintaining planarity of the graph.
First, inserting an isolated vertex from
V_{D}
does not affect planarity of a graph.
Now consider a single insertion of an edge
e\in E_{D}
and let
J^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime\prime}
denote the graph obtained respectively from
J^{\prime},G^{\prime}
after the insertions done so far, and assume inductively that
S
is a planar modulator in
G^{\prime\prime}\setminus e
.
We are going to prove that
S
is a planar modulator in
G^{\prime\prime}
.
If both endpoints of
e
are disconnected from
(T_{1}\cup T_{2})\setminus S
in
J^{\prime\prime}-S
then this connected component of
G^{\prime\prime}
being modified
is contained in
D
, which induces a planar graph by assumption.

Suppose otherwise.
Let
v\in V_{Q}
be an endpoint of
e
.
Since
R
is a restricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
J
(and so in
J^{\prime\prime}
) and
R\subseteq S
,
v
can be connected to at most one of
T_{1},T_{2}
in
J^{\prime\prime}-S
.
Suppose w.l.o.g. that
v
is connected to some vertex from
T_{1}
in
J^{\prime\prime}-S
but none from
T_{2}
.
By the definition of
V_{Q}
, every
(T_{1},v)
-path in
J
must intersect
\kappa(q^{\prime}_{1})
and
\kappa(q^{\prime}_{2})
.
These sets are disjoint from
S

and by
6.13
there are two cycles separating
v
in
G^{\prime\prime}
from any vertex from
T_{1}\setminus S
.
By
Lemma

3.16
we conclude that
G^{\prime\prime}-S
is planar.
After undoing all the edge deletions we obtain that
G-S
is planar.
The claim follows.
∎

In the first case we do not change the size of the planar modulator and in the second case
we have
|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+2\cdot|S^{\prime}\cap D|
.
Therefore the lifting algorithm can turn any solution to
G^{\prime}
into a solution to
G
within the guarantees of the lemma.

Finally, let us examine the radial distances in
H^{\prime}
.
By
6.10
we have
that whenever
x_{1},x_{2}\in V(T)
are such that
x_{1}
is parent to
x_{2}
and
v\in\kappa(x_{2})
then there exists
u\in\kappa(x_{1})
so that
u,v
share a face.
Let us fix a vertex
v_{y}\in\kappa(y)
.
By the observation above, there is a vertex
v_{2}\in\kappa(q_{2})
so that
d_{H^{\prime}}(v_{y},v_{2})=d_{H}(v_{y},v_{2})\leq 2k+5
.
As each path
P_{i}
,
i\in[d]
, has only one segment in
\kappa(q_{1})
and one in
\kappa(q_{2})
, it also has only one segment in
V_{Q}
.
The region between
\kappa(q_{1})
and
\kappa(q_{2})
in
H^{\prime}
comprises
d
subdivided edges between
\kappa(q_{1})
and
\kappa(q_{2})
.
Hence, there is a vertex
v_{1}\in\kappa(q_{1})
which shares a face with
v_{2}
in
H^{\prime}
.
By the same argument as before, there is a vertex
v_{x}\in\kappa(x)
at radial distance
2k+5
from
v_{1}
in
H^{\prime}
.
This implies that
d_{H^{\prime}}(v_{y},v_{x})\leq(2k+5)+1+(2k+5)=4k+11
and concludes the proof.
∎

Our strategy is to
mark the set of nodes in
T
whose images cover the boundary
\partial(H)
together with its LCA closure.
Suppose that
x,y\in V(T)
were marked,
x
is an ancestor of
y
, and
T_{x,y}
contains no marked vertices.
If
\delta_{T}(x,y)
is large and there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths, we can use
Lemma

6.17
to compress the middle layers.
If the
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-flow is less than 6, there is a restricted
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-separator of size at most 5.
If the number of such separators is small, we could just mark some more nodes in
T
.
We cannot do this however if there is a long sequence of small
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-separators.
But in this case the subgraph of
H
between the first and the last separator is disjoint from
\partial(H)
and has boundary of size at most 10.
Recall that the
Vertex planarization
problem admits a lossless protrusion replacer (
Lemma

6.2
)
which allows us to compress planar subgraphs of small boundary (
Lemma

6.6
).
We use this tool in a preprocessing step to ensure that the distance between the first and last small separator cannot be too large.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
\gamma
be the function from
Definition

6.1
for the
Vertex planarization
problem.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
, integers
k,r
, a set
A\subseteq V(G)
, such that
G\langle A\rangle
is planar,
G[A]
is connected, and
r=|N_{G}(A)|
, returns an
r
-boundaried plane graph
H^{\prime}
so the following hold.

1.

The radial diameter of
H^{\prime}
is at most
2\cdot(4k+\gamma(8019)+12)\cdot(6r+5)
and the graph
H^{\prime}\setminus\partial(H^{\prime})
is connected.

2.

\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)
, where
G^{\prime}
is the graph obtained from
G
after replacing
N_{G}[A]
with
H^{\prime}
.

3.

Let
A^{\prime}=V(H^{\prime})\setminus\partial(H^{\prime})
denote the set of the new vertices inserted in place of
A
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
such that
|S^{\prime}\cap A^{\prime}|\leq k
, outputs a planar modulator
S
in
G
such that
|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|+2\cdot|S^{\prime}\cap A^{\prime}|
and
S\setminus A=S^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
H
denote a boundaried plane graph obtained by an arbitrary plane embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
.
We shall perform a series of modifications of
H
which does not affect the boundary set.
First, we get rid of planar subgraphs with small boundary.
We iterate over all sets
X_{0}\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
of size at most 10 and inspect whether there is a connected component
C
of
H-X_{0}
which (a) does not contain any vertex from
\partial(H)
, (b) has at least
\gamma(8019)
vertices.
If we detect such a component
C
, we invoke
Lemma

6.6
for
r=|N_{H}(C)|\leq 10
and replace
N_{G}[C]
with a boundaried subgraph on less than
\gamma(27\cdot 27\cdot 11)=\gamma(8019)
vertices.
This does not affect the minimum vertex planarization number and allows to perform lossless solution lifting in polynomial time.
We perform such a replacement exhaustively until we cannot find a pair
(X_{0},C)
satisfying the requirements.
By
Lemma

6.6
(
4
) for
X=V(G)\setminus A
such a replacement does not affect planarity of the graph
G\langle A\rangle
.
By the same argument for
X=V(G)\setminus N_{G}[A]
we get that the replacement cannot disconnect the graph
G[A]
.
In each step, the size of the graph decreases, so at some point there are no more such pairs
(X_{0},C)
.
After this process terminates, we know that there is no separator of size at most 10, which would disconnect a subgraph of
H
which does not touch the boundary and has at least
\gamma(8019)
vertices.
We keep the variable
H
to denote the graph after this modification.

Let us now consider the outerplanar decomposition
(T,\chi)
of
H
.
For each
v\in\partial(H)
we mark the node
x\in V(T)
such that
v\in\kappa(x)
.
Let
Y_{0}
denote the set of marked nodes plus the root node,
|Y_{0}|\leq r+1
,
and
Y_{1}=\overline{\mathsf{LCA}}(Y_{0})
(recall
Definition

3.8
).
By
Lemma

3.9
we know that
|Y_{1}|\leq 2r+2
and every
connected component of
T-Y_{1}
has at most 2 neighbors.
Furthermore, there can be at most
2r+1
such components with exactly 2 neighbors because contracting them forms a tree on
2r+2
vertices.
Consider such a component
T_{C}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(T-Y_{1})
,
N_{T}(T_{C})=\{x,y\}
.
Then one of the vertices
x,y
is an ancestor of the other one.
Note that the path
T^{P}_{x,y}
is contained in
N_{T}[T_{C}]
.
Let
x^{\prime}\in T^{P}_{x,y}
be the furthest (from
x
) vertex on the
(x,y)
-path for which there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(x^{\prime}))
-paths in the graph
H\langle\kappa(T_{x,x^{\prime}})\rangle
.
It might be the case that
x^{\prime}=y
or
x^{\prime}=x
.
Next, let
y^{\prime}\in T^{P}_{x,y}
be the furthest (from
y
) vertex on the
(x^{\prime},y)
-path for which there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(y),\kappa(y^{\prime}))
-paths in the graph
H\langle\kappa(T_{y,y^{\prime}})\rangle
.
If
x^{\prime}=y
then
y^{\prime}=y
.

If
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
are distinct, we want to show that their distance
\delta_{T}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
cannot be larger than some constant.
Suppose that
\delta_{T}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})>3
.
Let
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}
be the neighbors of
x,y
, respectively, on the path
T^{P}_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}
.
By the definition of
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
there are no 6 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(x^{\prime\prime}))
-paths in the graph
H[\kappa(T_{x,x^{\prime\prime}})]
.
The sets
\kappa(x),\kappa(x^{\prime\prime})
are non-adjacent in
H
so
by Menger’s theorem there exists a restricted
(\kappa(x),\kappa(x^{\prime\prime}))
-separator
S_{x}
in
H
of size at most 5.
Analogous observations hold for
y,y^{\prime\prime}
and there exists a restricted
(\kappa(y),\kappa(y^{\prime\prime}))
-separator
S_{y}
in
H
of size at most 5.
Consider the set
X_{0}=S_{x}\cup S_{y}
of size at most 10
and the connected component of
H-X_{0}
containing the set
\kappa(T_{x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}})
.
By
6.12
,
it is disjoint from
\partial(H)
(as
\partial(H)
is contained in the
\kappa
-image of
Y_{0}
) so thanks to the preprocessing procedure we can conclude that it has less than
\gamma(8019)
vertices.
This implies that
|T_{x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}}|<\gamma(8019)
and so
\delta_{T}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=|T_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}|=|T_{x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}}|+2\leq\gamma(8019)+1
.

Let
Y_{2}
be the union of
Y_{1}
and all vertices marked as
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
in the previous step.
We have
|Y_{2}|\leq|Y_{1}|+2\cdot(2r+1)\leq 6r+4
.
Each connected component
T_{C}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(T-Y_{2})

with exactly two neighbors,
N_{T}(T_{C})=\{x,y\}
,
satisfies one of the following: (a)
\delta_{T}(x,y)\leq\gamma(8019)+1
, or (b) there are 6 internally vertex-disjoint
(\kappa(x),\kappa(y))
-paths in the graph
H\langle\kappa(T_{x,y})\rangle
.
Furthermore, one of the vertices
x,y
is an ancestor of the other one.
In order to reduce the depth of
T
we will apply
Lemma

6.17
to the components of type (b).
Let
T^{1}_{C},T^{2}_{C},\dots,T^{\ell}_{C}
,
N_{T}(T^{i}_{C})=\{x^{i},y^{i}\}
, be some ordering of the type (b) components of
T-Y_{2}
for which
\delta_{T}(x^{i},y^{i})>4k+11
.
We have
T_{x^{i},y^{i}}=T^{i}_{C}
and these sets are pairwise disjoint.
By applying
Lemma

6.17
to
x^{i},y^{i},T^{i}_{C}
we obtain a set of vertices
V_{D}^{i}\subseteq\kappa(T^{i}_{C})
and a set of edges
E_{D}^{i}\subseteq E_{G}(\kappa(T^{i}_{C}),\kappa(T^{i}_{C}))
to be removed.
Let
G_{0},H_{0}
be obtained from respectively
G,H
by removing the edges from all
E_{D}^{i}
and the vertices from all
V_{D}^{i}
.
Furthermore, let
G_{i}
,
i\in[\ell]
, be obtained from
G
by performing only the removals from
V^{j}_{D},E^{j}_{D}
for
j>i
.
Clearly
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{0})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)
as
G_{0}
is a subgraph of
G
.
Let
A_{0}=V(H_{0})\setminus\partial(H_{0})
be the set of vertices that form a replacement for
A
.

First, we argue that a solution to
G_{0}
can be lifted into a solution to
G
with moderate additional cost.
Let
S_{0}\subseteq V(G_{0})
be a planar modulator in
G_{0}
such that
|S_{0}\cap A_{0}|\leq k
.
We construct a series of sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}
where
S_{i}
is a refinement of
S_{i-1}
with respect to
T^{i}_{C}

so that
S_{i}
is a planar modulator in
G_{i}
.
This holds for
i=0
.
Let us consider
i\geq 1
and suppose that the claim holds for
i-1
.
By
Lemma

6.17
(
3
) we can compute a planar modulator
S_{i}
in
G_{i}
of size at most
|S_{i-1}|+2\cdot|S_{i-1}\cap\kappa(T^{i}_{C})|
and
S_{i}\setminus\kappa(T^{i}_{C})=S_{i-1}\kappa(T^{i}_{C})
.
The latter condition guarantees that
S_{j}\cap\kappa(T^{i}_{C})=S_{0}\cap\kappa(T^{i}_{C})
for
j<i
because in the
j
-step we only add new vertices from
\kappa(T^{j}_{C})
and these sets are disjoint.
Therefore
|S_{\ell}|\leq|S_{0}|+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2\cdot|S_{0}\cap\kappa(T^{i}_{C})|\leq|S_{0}|+2\cdot|S_{0}\cap A_{0}|

and
S_{\ell}\setminus A=S_{0}\setminus A_{0}
.
As
S_{\ell}
is a planar modulator in
G_{\ell}=G
, the solution lifting algorithm works as intended.

Now, we analyze the radial diameter of
H_{0}
.
Let
v\in\partial(H_{0})=\partial(H)
and
y\in Y_{2}
be such that
v\in\kappa(y)
.
Since the root node
x_{1}
belongs to
Y_{2}
and
|Y_{2}|\leq 6r+4
,
there a sequence
y=y_{1},\dots,y_{m}=r
of at most
m\leq 6r+4
vertices from
Y_{2}

so that
y_{i+1}
is an ancestor of
y_{i}
and
T_{y_{i},y_{i+1}}\cap Y_{2}=\emptyset
.
If
T_{y_{i},y_{i+1}}
is a component of type (b) then by

Lemma

6.17
(
1
) we know that for each
u\in\kappa(y_{i})
there is
u^{\prime}\in\kappa(y_{i+1})
such that their radial distance in
H_{0}
is at most
4k+11
.
If
T_{y_{i},y_{i+1}}
is a component of type (a), then by
6.10
we get the analogous property but with upper bound
\gamma(8019)+1
.
Therefore
v
is at radial distance at most
(4k+\gamma(8019)+12)\cdot(6r+4)
from some vertex on the outer face.

To analyze the situation for vertices from
V(H_{0})\setminus\partial(H_{0})
let us consider the outerplanar decomposition
(T_{0},\kappa_{0})
of
H_{0}
.
By the estimation above, if
v\in\partial(H_{0})=\partial(H)
then
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{H_{0}}(v)\leq(4k+\gamma(8019)+12)\cdot(6r+4)
.
Suppose there is a vertex
u\in V(H_{0})\setminus\partial(H_{0})
for which
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{H_{0}}(u)>(4k+\gamma(8019)+12)\cdot(6r+4)+k+3
.
Then there are at least
k+3
outerplanarity layers around
u
whose interiors are free of any boundary vertices.
Consider the sequence
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{k+3}\in V(T_{0})
of the first ancestors of
u
in
T_{0}
.
By
Lemma

6.9
(
2
) each set
\kappa(u_{i})
induces a connected subgraph of
G_{0}
.
If
v\in\partial(H_{0})
, then
\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{H_{0}}(v)<\mathrm{\textsc{idx}}_{H_{0}}(q_{i})
and
by
Lemma

6.9
(
3
)
\kappa(u_{i})
is a restricted
(u,v)
-separator in
H_{0}
.
Hence,
these sets form a nested sequence of
(u,\partial(H_{0}))
-separators in
H_{0}
and thus
these are
(u,N_{G_{0}}(A))
-separators in
G_{0}
.
We infer that these sets are
u
-planarizing and by
Lemma

3.17
the vertex
u
is
k
-irrelevant.
While such a vertex
u
exists we can safely remove it;
let
H^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
H_{0}
after these removals.
The depth of the outerplanar decomposition tree of
H^{\prime}
is bounded by
(4k+\gamma(8019)+12)\cdot(6r+4)+k+3
and the claim follows from
Lemma

6.11
.
∎

We will apply
Lemma

6.20
to each connected component
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
where
X
is a strong planar modulator supplied by
Proposition

5.7
.
We obtain some replacement
H_{C}
for the boundaried graph
G\langle C\rangle
equipped with a plane embedding of moderate diameter.
Because the lifting algorithm from
Lemma

6.20
increases the size of a solution only locally in
C
and does not affect the solution outside
C
, we can perform the lifting iteratively, increasing the size of the solution only by a constant factor in the end.

Proposition 6.21
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
with an
r
-strong planar modulator
X
of fragmentation
s
, and integer
k
,
outputs a graph
G^{\prime}
together with a
r
-strong planar modulator
X^{\prime}
of fragmentation
s
and a family of plane embeddings of
G^{\prime}\langle C^{\prime}\rangle
for each connected component
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime}-X^{\prime})
,
so that

1.

\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)
,

2.

for each connected component
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime}-X^{\prime})
, the computed plane embedding has radial diameter
\mathcal{O}(kr)
,

3.

given
G,G^{\prime}
, and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
k
, one can find, in polynomial time, a planar modulator
S
in
G
of size at most
3\cdot|S^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

Let
C_{1},C_{2},\dots,C_{\ell}
be some ordering of the connected components
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
.
We process each
G\langle C_{i}\rangle
with
Lemma

6.20
iteratively.
Namely, let
G_{0}=G
and
G_{i}
be obtained from
G_{i-1}
by applying
Lemma

6.20
to
G_{i-1}\langle C_{i}\rangle
, for
i\in[\ell]
.
We set
G^{\prime}=G_{\ell}
.
Let
C^{\prime}_{i}
be the set of new vertices inserted in place of
C_{i}
and
H^{\prime}_{i}
denote the plane graph given by the computed embedding of
G_{i}\langle C^{\prime}_{i}\rangle
.
We have
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{i})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G_{i-1})
so
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{\ell})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)
.
Furthermore, the radial diameter of
H^{\prime}_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(kr)

and we are guaranteed that the graphs
G_{i}[C^{\prime}_{i}]=H^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\partial(H^{\prime}_{i})
are connected so we do not increase the fragmentation of the modulator.
As
|N_{G_{i}}(C^{\prime}_{i})|=|N_{G_{i-1}}(C_{i})|
the modulator remains
r
-strong.

It remains to prove the solution lifting property.
Let
S_{\ell}\subseteq V(G_{\ell})
be a planar modulator in
G_{\ell}
of size at most
k
.
We construct a series of sets
S_{0},\dots,S_{\ell-1}
where
S_{i}
is a modification of
S_{i+1}

so that
S_{i}
is a planar modulator in
G_{i}

of size at most
|S_{\ell}|+\bigcup_{j>i}^{\ell}2\cdot|S_{\ell}\cap C^{\prime}_{j}|
.
Moreover, we show inductively that
S_{i}\cap C^{\prime}_{j}=S_{\ell}\cap C^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\leq i
, which holds for
i=\ell
.

We use the lifting algorithm from

Lemma

6.20

to turn
S_{i+1}
into a planar modulator
S_{i}
in
G_{i}
of size at most
|S_{i+1}|+2\cdot|S_{i+1}\cap C^{\prime}_{i+1}|
so that
S_{i}\setminus C_{i+1}=S_{i+1}\setminus C^{\prime}_{i+1}
.
The latter condition implies that we do not alter the solution
j\neq i+1
and shows the invariant
S_{i}\cap C^{\prime}_{j}=S_{\ell}\cap C^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\leq i
.
Therefore
|S_{i+1}\cap C^{\prime}_{i+1}|=|S_{\ell}\cap C^{\prime}_{i+1}|\leq k
as required by the lifting algorithm.
We estimate the final size of the planar modulator in
G_{0}
as
|S_{\ell}|+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2\cdot|S_{i}\cap C^{\prime}_{i}|=|S_{\ell}|+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2\cdot|S_{\ell}\cap C^{\prime}_{i}|\leq 3\cdot|S_{\ell}|
.
∎

7
Solution preservers

After the application of
Proposition

6.21
, we can assume that the considered graph
G
has a strong planar modulator
X\subseteq V(G)
of fragmentation
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

and for each
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
we are given a plane embedding of
G\langle C\rangle
with radial diameter
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
In order to further simplify
G\langle C\rangle
we take a two-step approach.
First, we mark a vertex set
S_{C}\subseteq C
so that
for any solution
S
we can replace the set
S\cap C
with a subset of
S_{C}\cup N_{G}(C)
by paying a constant cost for each replacement.
In particular, we get that there is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-approximately optimal planar modulator
S
such that
S\cap C\subseteq S_{C}
.
Afterwards, in Section
8
, we compress the subgraphs within
C\setminus S_{C}
for which we know that the approximately optimal solution does not remove any vertices inside them.
We begin with formalizing the concept of marking vertices to cover an approximate solution.

Definition 7.1
.

For a vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
, we say that
S_{A}\subseteq A
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
A
, if for any planar modulator
S
in
G
there exists a planar modulator
S^{\prime}\subseteq(S\setminus A)\cup N_{G}(A)\cup S_{A}
such that

S^{\prime}\setminus N_{G}[A]=S\setminus N_{G}[A]
and

|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap A|
.

We are going to construct these solution preservers recursively by reducing the task to more structured boundaried graphs.
We analyze this process by decomposing a boundaried graph
H
into a set
Y
which can be regarded as an augmentation of the boundary
\partial(H)
and a family of smaller boundaried graphs whose boundary lies on
\partial(H)\cup Y
.

Definition 7.2
.

A boundaried decomposition of a boundaried graph
H
is a pair
(Y,\mathcal{C})
, where
Y\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
and
\mathcal{C}
is a partition of
V(H)\setminus(\partial(H)\cup Y)
, such that for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
it holds that
N_{H}(C)\subseteq\partial(H)\cup Y
.

For example, when
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(H-(\partial(H)\cup Y))
is given by the connected components of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y)
then
(Y,\mathcal{C})
is a boundaried decomposition of
H
.
For our convenience, we will also consider cases when some elements of
\mathcal{C}
are disconnected.
Usually, we begin with finding a set
Y
that imposes some properties on boundaried graphs
H\langle C\rangle
for
C\in\mathcal{C}
.
In order to bound the number of subcases to recurse on, we would like to upper bound the maximal size of
\mathcal{C}
in terms of
Y
.
This is not always possible, but at least we can bound the number of sets
C\in\mathcal{C}
with more than two neighbors.

Lemma 7.3

(
[
FLSZ19
, Lem. 13.3]
)
.

Let
G
be a planar graph,
X\subseteq V(G)
, and let
N_{3}
be a set of vertices from
V(G)\setminus X
such that every vertex from
N_{3}
has at least three neighbors in
X
. Then,
|N_{3}|\leq 2\cdot|X|
.

For a vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
in a planar graph
G
,
consider the graph obtained from
G
by
contracting each connected component of
G-X
into a single vertex.
Since contractions preserve planarity,
we get the following corollary using the definition of fragmentation (Definition
3.3
).

Lemma 7.4
.

For a planar graph
G
and a vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
, the fragmentation of
X
in
G
is at most
2\cdot|X|
.

The following lemmas provide us with a framework to compute solution preservers recursively using boundaried decompositions.
The property
S^{\prime}\setminus N_{G}[A]=S\setminus N_{G}[A]
in
Definition

7.1
plays a role in the second lemma because by modifying a solution locally we do not affect its intersection with other parts of the decomposition.

Lemma 7.5
.

Consider a graph
G
and vertex sets
A,Y\subseteq V(G)
.
If
S_{A}\subseteq A
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
A
, for some
\alpha\geq 1
, then
S_{A}\cup Y
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
A\cup Y
.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
by any planar modulator in
G
.
By the definition of an
\alpha
-preserver, there exists a planar modulator
S^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq(S\setminus A)\cup N_{G}(A)\cup S_{A}
,
S^{\prime}\setminus N_{G}[A]=S\setminus N_{G}[A]
, and

|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap A|\leq|S|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap(A\cup Y)|
.
The first property can be restated as
S^{\prime}\subseteq(S\setminus(A\cup Y))\cup N_{G}(A\cup Y)\cup(S_{A}\cup Y)
because inserting
Y
at the end covers all the modifications to the first two terms.
Since
N_{G}[A]\subseteq N_{G}[A\cup Y]
we get
S^{\prime}\setminus N_{G}[A\cup Y]=S\setminus N_{G}[A\cup Y]
.
This satisfies all the three conditions of an
\alpha
-preserver for
A\cup Y
.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Consider a graph
G
and vertex sets
A,B\subseteq V(G)
, such that
N_{G}[A]\cap B=\emptyset
.
If
S_{A}\subseteq A,S_{B}\subseteq B
are
\alpha
-preservers for respectively
A,B
, for some
\alpha\geq 1
, then
S_{A}\cup S_{B}
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
A\cup B
.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
by any planar modulator in
G
.
By the definition of an
\alpha
-preserver, there exists a planar modulator
S_{1}\subseteq V(G)
such that
S_{1}\subseteq(S\setminus A)\cup N_{G}(A)\cup S_{A}
,
S_{1}\setminus N_{G}[A]=S\setminus N_{G}[A]
, and

|S_{1}|\leq|S|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap A|
.
The second property implies that
S_{1}\cap B=S\cap B
.
Next, we apply the definition for the set
B
and solution
S_{1}
.
We obtain a planar modulator
S_{2}\subseteq V(G)
such that
S_{2}\subseteq(S_{1}\setminus B)\cup N_{G}(B)\cup S_{B}
,
S_{2}\setminus N_{G}[B]=S_{1}\setminus N_{G}[B]
, and

|S_{2}|\leq|S_{1}|+\alpha\cdot|S_{1}\cap B|\leq|S|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap A|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap B|=|S|+\alpha\cdot|S\cap(A\cup B)|
.

We check that
S_{1}\setminus B\subseteq S\setminus(A\cup B)\cup N_{G}(A)\cup S_{A}
and
N_{G}(B)\cap A=N_{G}(A)\cap B=\emptyset
so
S_{2}\subseteq S\setminus(A\cup B)\cup N_{G}(A\cup B)\cup(S_{A}\cup S_{B})
.

Finally, both
N_{G}[A],N_{G}[B]
are subsets of
N_{G}[A\cup B]
so we trivially obtain

S_{2}\setminus N_{G}[A\cup B]=S_{1}\setminus N_{G}[A\cup B]=S\setminus N_{G}[A\cup B]
.
∎

From these two observations, we can easily obtain the following useful lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

Consider a graph
G
, vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
, and a boundaried decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{C})
of
G\langle A\rangle
.
Suppose that for each set
C\in\mathcal{C}
we are given an
\alpha
-preserver
S_{C}\subseteq C
for
C
.
Then the set
Y\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}S_{C}
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
A
.

Proof.

We fix any ordering
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
of
\mathcal{C}
.
For any distinct
i,j\in[m]
we have
N_{G}[C_{i}]\cap C_{j}=\emptyset
.
From
Lemma

7.6
we obtain that
S_{C_{1}}\cup S_{C_{2}}
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
C_{1}\cup C_{2}
and, by repeating this argument, that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}S_{C_{i}}
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}C_{i}
, for each
\ell\in[m]
.

Next, we apply
Lemma

7.5
to
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}C_{i}
and
Y
, to get that

\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}S_{C}\cup Y
is an
\alpha
-preserver for their union, that is
A
.
∎

Suppose that
G
is a graph,
X\subseteq V(G)
is a planar modulator in
G
, and for each component
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
we have computed an
\alpha
-preserver
S_{C}\subseteq C
.
We can apply
Lemma

7.7
to the boundaried graph
(G,\emptyset)
(with the empty boundary) and the boundaried decomposition
(X,\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X))
to
get that
X^{\prime}=X\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)}S_{C}
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
V(G)
.
In other words,
for every planar modulator
S\subseteq V(G)
in
G
, including the optimal one, there exists a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq\alpha\cdot|S|
.
This will allow us to restrict our attention only to the approximate solutions contained in
X^{\prime}
.

7.1
Reduction to single-faced boundaried graphs

In this section, we show
how to reduce the task of constructing a solution preserver for
G\langle A\rangle
with a given embedding of bounded diameter
to the case where in the embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
all the boundary vertices lie on the outer face.
As an intermediate step, we shall consider circumscribed boundaried plane graphs, which are slightly more general than single-faced plane graphs. This reduction will also come in useful in
Section

8
.

As we work with boundaried plane graphs of bounded radial diameter, we can mark a bounded-size set of faces covering the boundary and forming a connected area on the plane.
Then the remaining parts of the graph interact with their boundaries only through the outer faces. Recall the definition of a radial graph from Section
3
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
G
be a plane graph and let
\mathsf{Rad}(G)=(V(G)\cup F,E)
be the radial graph of
G
.
We say that
S\subseteq V(G)
is radially connected if there exists
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
such that
\mathsf{Rad}(G)[S\cup F^{\prime}]
is connected.

Given a set of vertices in a plane graph of moderate radial diameter we want to find its superset that is radially connected.
A bound on the radial diameter translates to a bound on the diameter of the radial graph.
We will take advantage of the following simple observation about bounded-diameter graphs.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
G
be a connected graph of diameter bounded by
d
.
Then for any non-empty vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
there exists
S^{\prime}\supseteq S
such that
G[S^{\prime}]
is connected and
|S^{\prime}|\leq d\cdot|S|
.
Furthermore, such a set
S^{\prime}
can be found in polynomial time when
G
and
S
are given.

Proof.

We prove by induction on
\ell
that if
G[S]
has
\ell
connected components, there is a connected superset
S^{\prime}\supseteq S
of size at most
|S|+\ell(d-1)
. This proves that any set
S
has a connected superset of size at most
d\cdot|S|
since
G[S]
has at most
\ell\leq|S|
connected components, and therefore gives the lemma.

If
\ell=1
then
S^{\prime}=S
suffices. Otherwise, let
C_{1},C_{2}
be distinct connected components of
G[S]
and let
v_{1}\in C_{1},v_{2}\in C_{2}
. Since the diameter of
G
is at most
d
, there is a
(v_{1},v_{2})
-path
P
in
G
of length at most
d
, which has at most
d-1
internal vertices
I
. Then
S\cup I
has at most
\ell-1
connected components. By induction, there is a connected superset
S^{\prime}
of
S\cup I
on at most
|S\cup I|+(\ell-1)\cdot(d-1)\leq|S|+\ell(d-1)
vertices, which concludes the proof. The inductive argument easily turns into a recursive polynomial-time algorithm.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
G
be a plane graph of radial diameter bounded by
d
.
Then for any vertex set
S\subseteq V(G)
there exists
S^{\prime}\supseteq S
such that
S^{\prime}
is radially connected in
G
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|\cdot(2d+2)
.
Furthermore, the set
S^{\prime}
can be found in polynomial time when
G
and
S
are given.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{Rad}(G)=(V(G)\cup F,E)
be the radial graph of
G
.
Note that
\mathsf{Rad}(G)
is always connected, even if
G
is not.
Consider some pair
u,v\in V(G)
.
By assumption
d_{G}(u,v)\leq d
and so there exists a
(u,v)
-path in
\mathsf{Rad}(G)
of length at most
2d
, as any jump between vertices that share a face
f
can be emulated in
\mathsf{Rad}(G)
by visiting the vertex representing
f
.
Furthermore, each vertex from
F
is adjacent in
\mathsf{Rad}(G)
to some vertex from
V(G)
, so

\mathsf{Rad}(G)
has diameter at most
2d+2
.
By Lemma
7.9
, there exists a set
S_{R}\supseteq S
,
S_{R}\subseteq V(\mathsf{Rad}(G))
, so that
\mathsf{Rad}(G)[S_{R}]
is connected and
|S_{R}|\leq|S|\cdot(2d+2)
, and this set can be efficiently computed.
The claim follows by
taking
S^{\prime}=S_{R}\cap V(G)
.
∎

We proceed with decomposing a boundaried plane graph of moderate diameter into circumscribed boundaried plane graphs by augmenting the boundary to be radially connected.
Recall
Definition

3.11
of a circumscribed boundaried plane graph
H
: all vertices of
\partial(H)
must be embedded in the outer face of
H-\partial(H)
.

Lemma 7.11
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a boundaried plane graph
H
of radial diameter at most
d
and a (possibly empty) vertex set
Y\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
,
outputs a vertex set
Y^{\prime}\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
of size at most
(2d+2)\cdot(|Y|+|\partial(H)|)
, such that for each connected component
C
of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y^{\prime})
the boundaried plane graph
H\langle C\rangle
is circumscribed.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

7.10
for
S=\partial(H)\cup Y
,
to find a set
S^{\prime}\supseteq\partial(H)
of size at most
(2d+2)\cdot(|Y|+|\partial(H)|)
, which is radially connected in
H
.
Consider a connected component
C
of
H-S^{\prime}

and the plane embedding of
H
truncated to the subgraph induced by
C
.
Suppose there are at least two faces of
H[C]
containing vertices from
N_{H}(C)
(which is a subset of
S^{\prime}
).
Then there exists a cycle in
H-S^{\prime}
with at least one vertex of
S^{\prime}
in the interior and at least one vertex of
S^{\prime}
in the exterior.
This contradicts the fact that
S^{\prime}
is radially connected in
H
.
We infer that there is only one face of
H[C]
which contains vertices from
N_{H}(C)
, hence the graph
H\langle C\rangle
is circumscribed.
Note that we can always modify the embedding by swapping the outer face with any other chosen face.
The claim follows by taking
Y^{\prime}=S^{\prime}\setminus\partial(H)
.
∎

We are already close to the case with the boundary lying on the outer face.
There is however a subtle difference between circumscribed and single-faced boundaried plane graphs.
If
H
is circumscribed, then the edges incident to the boundary of
H
may separate some vertices of
\partial(H)
from the outer face of
H
.

We shall employ
Lemma

3.7

to decompose
H
into boundaried subgraphs adjacent to at most two vertices from
\partial(H)
.
We want to say that such a boundaried subgraph
H\langle C\rangle
becomes single-faced after discarding these two special vertices.
This is done by exploiting minimality of a particular separator which guarantees that there exists a subgraph disjoint from
N_{H}[C]
, that can be made connected without violating planarity, adjacent to
each vertex in
N_{H}(C)
.
Contracting this subgraph gives the closure graph of
H\langle C\rangle
and allows us to use the criterion from
3.13
.

In order to use

Lemma

3.7
we need a tree decomposition of width
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Such a decomposition is computed in

Section

4
but later we apply several graph modifications in
Section

6
, including protrusion replacement.
This might invalidate the tree decomposition and we need to compute it anew.

Lemma 7.12
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given an
r
-boundaried circumscribed plane graph
H
of radial diameter at most
d
, such that
r\geq 3
,
outputs a vertex set
Y\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
of size at most
18\cdot dr
, such that for each connected component
C
of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y)

it holds that

1.

|N_{H}(C)\cap\partial(H)|\leq 2
, and

2.

the boundaried graph
H\langle C\rangle-(N_{H}(C)\cap\partial(H))
is single-faced.

Proof.

The graph
H
has at most
d
outerplanarity layers, so its treewidth is at most
3d-1

[
Bod98
]
.
We invoke the polynomial-time
\frac{3}{2}
-approximation algorithm for treewidth on planar graphs
[
ST94
]
3
3

3

The work
[
ST94
]
focuses on computing branchwidth exactly on planar graphs and the
\frac{3}{2}
-approximation for treewidth follows from the close relation between treewidth and branchwidth.
to compute a tree decomposition
(T,\chi)
of
H
of width at most
\eta=\frac{3}{2}(3d-1)
.

We supply the computed tree decomposition to
Lemma

3.7
for
S=\partial(H)
and obtain a vertex set
Y\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
such that
|Y|\leq 4(\eta+1)\cdot r\leq 18\cdot dr
and
each connected component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y)
has at most 2 neighbors in
\partial(H)
and at most
2\eta
neighbors in
Y
.
The latter property is not used in this proof.

In order to ensure condition (
2
) of the lemma, we need to trim the set
Y
.
Consider the following reduction rule: while there exists a vertex
v\in Y
such that
Y\setminus\{v\}
still satisfies condition (
1
), remove
v
from
Y
.
Let
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
be the set obtained by exhaustive application of the reduction rule.
Observe that
Y^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
because otherwise the only connected component of
H-\partial(H)
would have
r\geq 3
neighbors in
\partial(H)
which does not satisfy condition (
1
).

Consider a connected component
C
of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y^{\prime})
and
v\in N_{H}(C)\cap Y^{\prime}
.
Since
v
has not been removed in the application of the reduction rule, there is a different connected component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y^{\prime})
which is adjacent to
v
and to some
t\in\partial(H)
.
Let
H^{1}
be obtained from
H
by
(1) removing
N_{H}(C)\cap\partial(H)
, and (2)
replacing
\partial(H)\setminus N_{H}(C)
with a single vertex
u
adjacent to entire set
N_{H}(\partial(H))
.
This operation can be seen as inserting a vertex adjacent to a subset of vertices lying on the outer face of
H-\partial(H)
, so
H^{1}
is planar.
By the observation above, the set
R_{H^{1}}(u,Y^{\prime})
is adjacent to each
v\in N_{H^{1}}(C)\cap Y^{\prime}
and it is disjoint from
C
.
Let
H^{2}
be obtained from
H^{1}
by contracting the connected vertex set
R_{H^{1}}(u,Y^{\prime})
into
u
. This graph contains
H\langle C\rangle-(N_{H}(C)\cap\partial(H))
as a boundaried subgraph
H^{2}\langle C\rangle
, together with
u\not\in C
adjacent to each vertex in
N_{H^{2}}(C)
.
Therefore,
H^{2}
is the closure graph of
H\langle C\rangle-(N_{H}(C)\cap\partial(H))
which, as
H^{2}
is planar and connected, implies that
H\langle C\rangle-(N_{H}(C)\cap\partial(H))
is single-faced (see
3.13
).
∎

Retrieving solution preservers from boundaried decompositions

We have shown how to decompose the bounded-diameter case into the circumscribed case, and the circumscribed case into the single-faced case.
Now we want to use
Lemma

7.7
to “backpropagate” these reductions and retrieve the solution preservers.
We first state a crucial proposition which allows us to construct a solution preserver for a single-faced boundaried graph.

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
A\subseteq V(G)
be such that
G\langle A\rangle
is a single-faced boundaried graph.
Then there exists a set
S_{A}\subseteq A
of size at most
|N_{G}(A)|^{2}
which is a 168-preserver for
A
.
Furthermore,
S_{A}
can be computed in polynomial time.

In order to keep the focus of the current section, we postpone the
proof of
Proposition

7.13
to the end of
Section

7.3
and first discuss its implications.
As the case of a circumscribed boundaried graph can be reduced to the case with a single-faced boundaried graph via
Lemma

7.12
, our framework yields a construction of solution preservers for circumscribed boundaried graphs.

This construction may require removal of at most two vertices from the obtained boundaried subgraph
G\langle A\rangle
to make it single-faced.
This however does not impose much trouble: if
S
is a solution and
S\cap A
is non-empty we can “charge” this part of the solution to pay for the removal of the two vertices, turning an
\alpha
-preserver into an
(\alpha+2)
-preserver.
Similarly we argue that when
|N_{G}(A)|\leq 2
then if
S\cap A
is non-empty then we may just remove
N_{G}(A)
so in this case the empty set is a 2-preserver for
A
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Consider a graph
G
, a vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
such that
|N_{G}(A)|=r\geq 3
, and a circumscribed embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
of radial diameter at most
d
.
Then there exists a 170-preserver
S_{A}\subseteq A
for
A
of size
\mathcal{O}(d^{3}r^{3})
. Furthermore,
S_{A}
can be computed in polynomial time when given
G,A
, and the embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

7.12

to compute a set
Y\subseteq A
of size at most
18\cdot dr
, such that for each connected component
C
of
G\langle A\rangle-(N_{G}(A)\cup Y)
there exists a set
D_{C}=N_{G}(C)\cap N_{G}(A)
of size at most 2 so that the boundaried graph
G\langle C\rangle-D_{C}
is single-faced.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the family of connected components of
G\langle A\rangle-(N_{G}(A)\cup Y)
.
Then
(Y,\mathcal{C})
is a boundaried decomposition of
G\langle A\rangle
.
We partition
\mathcal{C}
into families
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}
, comprising those components that have at most 2 neighbors, and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}
, which contains all the remaining components.

For
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}
a 2-preserver is given by the empty set: for any planar modulator
S\subseteq V(G)
satisfying
|S\cap C|\geq 1
we can replace
S\cap C
with
N_{G}(C)
.
Consider now a component
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}
.
Let
G^{\prime}=G-D_{C}
;
we have that
G^{\prime}\langle C\rangle=G\langle C\rangle-D_{C}
is a single-faced boundaried graph.
We apply
Proposition

7.13
to compute a 168-preserver
S_{C}\subseteq C
for
C
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
|N_{H}(C)|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(d^{2}r^{2})
.
We claim that
S_{C}
is a 170 preserver for
C
in
G
.
To see this, consider a planar modulator
S\subseteq V(G)
in
G
satisfying
|S\cap C|\geq 1
.
Then
S_{1}=S\setminus D_{C}
is a planar modulator in
G^{\prime}
.
By the definition of an
\alpha
-preserver, there exists a planar modulator
S_{2}
in
G^{\prime}
, such that (1)
S_{2}\subseteq(S_{1}\setminus C)\cup N_{G^{\prime}}(C)\cup S_{C}
, (2)
S_{2}\setminus N_{G^{\prime}}[C]=S_{1}\setminus N_{G^{\prime}}[C]
, and (3)
|S_{2}|\leq|S_{1}|+168\cdot|S_{1}\cap C|
.
We set
S_{3}=S_{2}\cup D_{C}
, so
|S_{3}|\leq|S|+170\cdot|S\cap C|
.
It is a planar modulator in
G
and, since
D_{C}\subseteq N_{G}(C)
, it is a correct replacement for
S
.

By
Lemma

7.7
we obtain that
S_{A}=Y\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}}S_{C}
is a 170-preserver for
A
.
We estimate
|\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}|\leq 2\cdot|N_{G}(A)\cup Y|=\mathcal{O}(dr)
with
Lemma

7.4
so the total size of
S_{A}
is
\mathcal{O}(d^{3}r^{3})
.
∎

Finally, we employ the reduction from
Lemma

7.11
to extend the construction above to the case of boundaried plane graphs with bounded diameter.
The following proposition encapsulates all the results about solution preservers into a statement to be used in the main proof.

Proposition 7.15
.

Consider a graph
G
, a vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
such that
|N_{G}(A)|=r
, and a plane embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
of radial diameter at most
d
.
Then there exists a 170-preserver
S_{A}\subseteq A
for
A
of size
\mathcal{O}(d^{7}r^{4})
. Furthermore,
S_{A}
can be computed in polynomial time when
G,A
, and the embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
are given.

Proof.

Let
H
be a boundaried plane graph of radial diameter at most
d
given by the embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
.
We apply
Lemma

7.11
to
H
with
Y=\emptyset
to compute a set
Y^{\prime}
of size at most
(2d+2)\cdot r
such that for each connected component
C
of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y^{\prime})
the boundaried plane graph
H\langle C\rangle
is circumscribed.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the family of connected components of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y)
.
We partition
\mathcal{C}
into families
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}
, comprising those components that have at most 2 neighbors, and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}
, which contains all the remaining components. We proceed similarly as in
Lemma

7.14
.

For
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}
a 2-preserver is given by the empty set: for any planar modulator
S\subseteq V(G)
satisfying
|S\cap C|\geq 1
we can replace
S\cap C
with
N_{G}(C)
.
For each component
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}

we have that
|N_{H}(C)|\leq|Y\cup\partial(H)|=\mathcal{O}(dr)
.
Furthermore, the radial diameter bound from
G\langle A\rangle
transfers to
G\langle C\rangle
.
We take advantage of
Lemma

7.14
to compute a 170-preserver
S_{C}\subseteq C
of size
\mathcal{O}(d^{6}r^{3})
.

By
Lemma

7.7
we obtain that
S_{A}=Y\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}}S_{C}
is a 170-preserver for
A
.
We estimate
|\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}|\leq 2\cdot|N_{G}(A)\cup Y|=\mathcal{O}(dr)
with
Lemma

7.4
so the total size of
S_{A}
is
\mathcal{O}(d^{7}r^{4})
.
∎

7.2
Inseparability in single-faced boundaried graphs

This section, together with the following one, are devoted to proving
Proposition

7.13
.
To this end, we first develop a framework of inseparability in single-faced boundaried graphs and reduce computing a solution preserver to the case where the given single-faced boundaried graph is inseparable.
In the next section, we will focus on the inseparable case.

Given a single-faced boundaried plane graph
H
we can consider a natural cyclic ordering of
\partial(H)
.
Since the vertices of
\partial(H)
lie on the outer face, we can augment the embedding by inserting a special vertex
u_{H}
adjacent to all vertices in
\partial(H)
.
This operation turns
H
into its
closure graph
\widehat{H}
(see
3.13
).
After this operation we get a unique cyclic ordering of the neighbors of the special vertex
u_{H}
, which are the vertices from
\partial(H)
.
When considering a single-faced boundaried plane graph
H
, we always implicitly assume a fixed cylic ordering of
\partial(H)
, given by some embedding of
\widehat{H}
.
For such an ordering, we
define a
canonical division
as a partition
\partial(H)=T_{1}\uplus T_{2}
such that
T_{1},T_{2}
are non-empty connected segments in the cyclic ordering of
\partial(H)
.
We say that sets
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T
are
non-crossing
if they are non-empty and there exists a canonical division
(T^{\prime}_{1},T^{\prime}_{2})

such that
T^{\prime}_{1}\supseteq T_{1}
,
T^{\prime}_{2}\supseteq T_{2}
.

Observation 7.16
.

Consider a single-faced boundaried plane graph
H
and a canonical division
(T_{1},T_{2})
of
\partial(H)
.
Let
H^{\prime\prime}
be obtained by adding two new vertices
t_{1},t_{2}
, adjacent to
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, respectively.
Then
H^{\prime\prime}
is planar.

The following definition plays a key role in this section.

Definition 7.17
.

A
c
-separation
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
in a boundaried graph
H
is given by an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator
S
, such that
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\partial(H)
are non-empty and disjoint, and
c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|)
.
We say that a boundaried graph
H
is
c
-inseparable if it does not admit a
c
-separation.

Given a single-faced boundaried plane graph
H
,
we say that a
c
-separation
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
is canonical if
(T_{1},T_{2})
is a canonical division of
\partial(H)
and
S
is an inclusion-wise minimal unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator.

Our current goal is to reduce the task of finding solution preservers into the case when the given single-faced boundaried graph is
c
-inseparable for some constant
c
.
As long as some
c
-separation exists, ideally we would like to find it and recurse on the both sides of the separator.
It is however unclear whether a
c
-separation can be detected in polynomial time or whether the obtained boundaried subgraphs are always single-faced.
First we show that when we can separate some non-crossing subsets of the boundary with a small separator, we can do the same for some canonical division.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
H
be a single-faced boundaried plane graph and
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\partial(H)
be non-crossing.
Suppose there exists an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator
S
,
such that
c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|)
.
Then
H
admits a canonical
c
-separation.

Proof.

Let
T^{\prime}_{1}\supseteq T_{1},T^{\prime}_{2}\supseteq T_{2}
be disjoint segments of
\partial(H)
witnessing the non-crossing property.
Let
H^{\prime\prime}
be obtained from
H
by adding a new vertex
t_{1}
adjacent to all
v\in T_{1}
and a new vertex
t_{2}
adjacent to all
v\in T_{2}
.
Since
T_{1},T_{2}
are non-crossing,
H^{\prime\prime}
is planar by
7.16
. We can assume that each vertex
v\in T^{\prime}_{1}
either belongs to
T_{1}
or to
R=R_{H^{\prime\prime}}(t_{2},S)\setminus\{t_{2}\}
, since if neither holds then we can add
v
to
T_{1}
while preserving the preconditions to the lemma.

We introduce some terminology for the rest of the proof. Suppose
V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq T_{1}
are two disjoint segments of terminals.
Then the set
\partial(H)\setminus(V_{1}\cup V_{2})
comprises of two segments
U_{1},U_{2}
(
U_{1}
possibly empty) such that
T_{2}\subseteq U_{2}
.
We say that a vertex
v\in\partial(H)
lies
between

V_{1}
and
V_{2}
when
v\in U_{1}
.

We divide
T_{1}
into a sequence of (maximal) segments
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{m}
, ordered with respect to the embedding, such that the boundary vertices
between

Q_{i}
and
Q_{i+1}
belong to
R
.
Let
C_{i}
be the set of vertices reachable from any
v\in Q_{i}
in
H^{\prime\prime}
without going through
R
or
t_{1}
, that is,

C_{i}=R_{H^{\prime\prime}}(Q_{i},R\cup\{t_{1}\})
.

We claim that the sets
(C_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
are pairwise disjoint. Assume otherwise that
C_{i}\cap C_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
i<j
.
Then there exist vertices
v_{1}\in Q_{i}
and
v_{3}\in Q_{j}
connected by a path
P_{1,3}
in
V(H)-R
.
Let
v_{2}\in T^{\prime}_{1}\cap R
be some vertex lying between
Q_{i}
and
Q_{j}
.

Since
v_{2}\in R
, there exists a path
P_{2,4}
connecting
v_{2}
to some vertex
v_{4}\in T^{\prime}_{2}
such that
V(P)\subseteq R
.
Note that the numbering of vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
obeys the cyclic ordering of terminals given by the single-faced embedding.
Let
H_{0}
be a graph obtained from
H
by inserting edges
v_{1}v_{2},v_{2}v_{3},v_{3}v_{4},v_{4}v_{1}
and a new vertex
v_{5}
adjacent
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
.
By the choice of the ordering,
H_{0}
is planar.
The path
P_{1,3}
within
V(H)\setminus R
connecting
v_{1}
and
v_{3}
is disjoint from the path
P_{2,4}
connecting
v_{2}
and
v_{4}
within
R
.
By contracting the paths
P_{1,3}
,
P_{2,4}
to edges
we obtain a minor model of
K_{5}
in
H_{0}
, which is a contradiction with the assumption that
C_{i}\cap C_{j}\neq\emptyset
.

Using the disjointness property we finish the proof. Let
S_{i}=S\cap C_{i}
for
i\in[m]
.
The sets
(S_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
are pairwise disjoint and the sets
(Q_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
sum up to
T_{1}
, hence the assumption
c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|)
implies that
c\cdot|S_{i}|\leq|Q_{i}|
for some
i\in[m]
.
The set
S_{i}
is an unrestricted
(Q_{i},R)
-separator in
H
and an unrestricted
(Q_{i},Q_{j})
-separator in
H
for each
j\neq i
because
C_{i}\cap C_{j}=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
S_{i}
is an unrestricted
(Q_{i},\partial(H)\setminus Q_{i})
separator.
Since
c\cdot|S_{i}|\leq c\cdot|S|\leq|T_{2}|\leq|\partial(H)\setminus Q_{i}|
, we obtain a canonical
c
-separation.
∎

We now show that the existence of a
(2c)
-separation implies the existence of a
c
-separation of non-crossing subsets, which in turn implies the existence of a canonical
c
-separation.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
H
be a single-faced boundaried graph,
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\partial(H)
be disjoint and non-empty,
and
S
be an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator,
such that
2c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|)
.
Then
H
admits a canonical
c
-separation
with respect to any given single-faced embedding.

Proof.

Let us fix a single-faced embedding of
H
.
Due to Lemma
7.18
, it suffices to show that
S
is an unrestricted
(Q_{1},Q_{2})
-separator for some non-crossing
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subseteq\partial(H)
, such that
c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|Q_{1}|,|Q_{2}|)
.

Since
|T_{2}|\geq 2c\cdot|S|
, we can find two segments of
\partial(H)
that each have at least
c\cdot|S|
vertices from
T_{2}
. Hence there exists a canonical division
(Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2})
of
\partial(H)
, such that

c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|Q^{\prime}_{1}\cap T_{2}|,|Q^{\prime}_{2}\cap T_{2}|)
.
As the segments
(Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2})
partition
T_{1}\subseteq\partial(H)
and
|T_{1}|\geq 2c\cdot|S|
, we have
c\cdot|S|\leq|Q^{\prime}_{1}\cap T_{1}|
or
c\cdot|S|\leq|Q^{\prime}_{2}\cap T_{1}|
.
Suppose this inequality holds for
Q^{\prime}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Let
Q_{1}=Q^{\prime}_{i}\cap T_{1}
and
Q_{2}=T_{2}\setminus Q^{\prime}_{i}
.
Since
Q_{1}\subseteq T_{1}
and
Q_{2}\subseteq T_{2}
, the set
S
is an unrestricted
(Q_{1},Q_{2})
-separator.
∎

As the number of canonical divisions is quadratic in the size of the boundary, we can find a canonical
c
-separation in polynomial time.
Together with
Lemma

7.19
, this yields a 2-approximation algorithm for finding separations.

Lemma 7.20
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a single-faced boundaried graph
H
and an integer
c>0
, either correctly concludes that
H
is
(2c)
-inseparable or finds a canonical
c
-separation with respect to some single-faced embedding of
H
.

Proof.

Let us start with constructing a single-faced embedding of
H
: it can be done in polynomial time since it suffices to construct any plane embedding of the closure of
H
(see
3.13
).
By
Lemma

7.19
, a single-faced boundaried graph either is
(2c)
-inseparable or admits a canonical
c
-separation with respect to any single-faced embedding.
We enumerate all
|\partial(H)|^{2}
-many canonical divisions with respect to the constructed embedding.
For each canonical division
(T_{1},T_{2})
we check if there exists
S
such that
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
is a canonical
c
-separation.
This task reduces to adding two new vertices
t_{1},t_{2}
, adjacent respectively to
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, and finding a minimum
(t_{1},t_{2})
-separator.
If for some canonical division
(T_{1},T_{2})
we find
S
such that
c\cdot|S|\leq\min(|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|)
, we can return it: it must be an inclusion-wise minimal unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator.
Otherwise we report that
H
is
(2c)
-inseparable.
∎

Whenever we find a canonical
c
-separation we want to augment the boundary by marking the vertices in the separator
to split the given single-faced boundaried graphs into two smaller single-faced boundaried graphs.
We will add the marked vertices to the solution preserver and we proceed recursively.
A priori this construction may mark a large number of vertices so we need some kind of a measure to ensure that the constructed solution preserver cannot be too large.
Let
t
be the size of the original boundary,
s
be the size of the separator, and
t_{1},t_{2}
be the boundary sizes of the two smaller single-faced boundaried graphs on which we recurse.
Because we work with a canonical
c
-separation we have that
c\cdot s\leq\min(t_{1},t_{2})
.
This implies that even when
t_{1}+t_{2}>t
these numbers are in some sense balanced.
It turns out that it suffices to take
c=4
to ensure that
t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}<t^{2}
which provides us with a measure that decreases in each step and governs the number of possible recursive splits.

Lemma 7.21
.

Let
s\geq 0,\,t>0,\,t_{1}>0,\,t_{2}>0
be integers satisfying
t_{1}\geq 4s,t_{2}\geq 4s,\,t+2s\geq t_{1}+t_{2}
. Then
t^{2}\geq t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+s+1
.

Proof.

First consider the case
s=0
.
We have
t\geq t_{1}+t_{2}>0
so
t^{2}\geq(t_{1}+t_{2})^{2}=t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+2t_{1}t_{2}\geq t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+1
because
t_{1},t_{2}
are positive.

Now consider the case
s\geq 1
.
As
t_{1}+t_{2}-2s\geq 6s\geq 0
, the inequality
t\geq t_{1}+t_{2}-2s
is maintained with both sides squared.
We enroll the squared expression, rearrange the terms, and bound
4s^{2}\geq s+1
.

\displaystyle t^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(t_{1}+t_{2}-2s)^{2}=t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+4s^{2}+2t_{1}t_{2}-4st_{1}-4st_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+4s^{2}+{t_{1}}(t_{2}-4s)+{t_{2}}(t_{1}-4s)\geq t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2}+s+1.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Now we show that when splitting a single-faced boundaried graph along a canonical separation, we indeed obtain two smaller single-faced boundaried graphs.
We do not construct their embeddings directly but rather refer to the convenient criterion from
3.13
.
Note that the definition of a single-faced boundaried graph does not require it to be connected although connectivity will later follow from being
c
-inseparable.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
H
be a single-faced boundaried plane graph
and let
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
be a canonical 4-separation.
Then there exist sets
V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq V(H)
, so that

1.

(V_{1},V_{2})
is a partition of
V(H)\setminus(\partial(H)\cup S)
,

2.

N_{H}(V_{1})\subseteq T_{1}\cup S
and
N_{H}(V_{2})\subseteq T_{2}\cup S
,

3.

H\langle V_{1}\rangle,H\langle V_{2}\rangle
are single-faced boundaried graphs,

4.

|N_{H}(V_{1})|^{2}+|N_{H}(V_{2})|^{2}+|S|+1\leq|\partial(H)|^{2}
.

Furthermore, the sets
V_{1},V_{2}
can be found in polynomial time, when
H
and
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
are given.

Proof.

If
|S|=0
, then
T_{1},T_{2}
are disconnected in
H
.
We can group the vertices of
H-\partial(H)

into two sets
V_{1},V_{2}
, where
N_{H}(V_{i})\subseteq T_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Since
T_{1},T_{2}
are non-empty by Definition
7.17
, the numbers
0,|\partial(H)|,|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|
, satisfy the prerequisites of Lemma
7.21
, and so
|N_{H}(V_{1})|^{2}+|N_{H}(V_{2})|^{2}+|S|+1\leq|\partial(H)|^{2}
. Since
H
is single-faced, the same is easily seen to hold for
H\langle V_{1}\rangle
and
H\langle V_{2}\rangle
.

Let us assume
|S|\geq 1
for the rest of the proof.
Consider the connected components of
H-(\partial(H)\cup S)
.
None of them can have neighbors in both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
Therefore we can group the vertices of
V(H)\setminus(\partial(H)\cup S)
into two sets
V_{1},V_{2}
, where
V_{i}
is the union of the components with neighbors in
T_{i}\cup S
.
If a component has neighbors only in
S
, we can choose its allocation arbitrarily.

We are going to show that
H\langle V_{1}\rangle
is single-faced—the proof for
H\langle V_{2}\rangle
is symmetric.
Note that
N_{H}(V_{1})\subseteq T_{1}\cup S
.
Let
H_{0}
be obtained from
H
by adding two new vertices
t_{1},t_{2}
adjacent to respectively
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
Then
H_{0}
is planar and by Definition
7.17

S
is an inclusion-wise minimal
(t_{1},t_{2})
-separator in
H_{0}
.
Therefore, each vertex from
S
is adjacent to both
R_{H_{0}}(t_{1},S)
and
R_{H_{0}}(t_{2},S)
.
Let
H_{1}
be obtained from
H_{0}
by

1.

adding an edge
t_{1}t_{2}
—this preserves planarity,

2.

contracting
R_{H_{0}}(t_{2},S)
into
t_{2}
,

3.

contracting the edge
t_{1}t_{2}
.

The graph
H_{1}
is planar and contains
H\langle V_{1}\rangle
as a boundaried subgraph
H_{1}\langle V_{1}\rangle
, plus one additional vertex adjacent to every vertex in
T_{1}\cup S\supseteq N_{H_{1}}(V_{1})
.
Also,
H_{1}
is a contraction of the closure
\widehat{H}
of
H
, hence it is planar and connected.
Finally, let us remove from
H_{1}
all the vertices of
(T_{1}\cup S)\setminus N_{H_{1}}(V_{1})
, which trivially preserves planarity and preserves connectivity through
t_{1}
.
The resulting graph
is isomorphic to the closure of
H\langle V_{1}\rangle
.
We have thus obtained that the closure of
H\langle V_{1}\rangle
is planar and connected so by
3.13
the boundaried graph
H
is single-faced.

Finally, let us prove the inequality.
Since
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
is a 4-separation, it holds that
\min(|T_{1}|,|T_{2}|)\geq 4\cdot|S|
.
As
|T_{1}\cup S|+|T_{2}\cup S|\leq|\partial(H)|+2\cdot|S|
, the numbers
|S|,|\partial(H)|,|T_{1}\cup S|,|T_{2}\cup S|
satisfy the prerequisites of Lemma
7.21
, and so
|T_{1}\cup S|^{2}+|T_{2}\cup S|^{2}+|S|+1\leq|\partial(H)|^{2}
.
Since
N_{H}(V_{i})\subseteq T_{i}\cup S
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the claim follows.
∎

We are ready to decompose a single-faced boundaried graph
H
into boundaried subgraphs which are single-faced and 8-inseparable.
We define a single-faced decomposition of
H
as a boundaried decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{C})
of
H
(recall
Definition

7.2
), such that for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
it holds that
H\langle C\rangle
is single-faced.

Lemma 7.23
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a single-faced boundaried graph
H
,
returns its single-faced decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{C})
, so that
|Y|\leq|\partial(H)|^{2}
and for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
it holds that
H\langle C\rangle
is 8-inseparable.

Proof.

We decompose
H
recursively starting with a single-faced decomposition
(Y_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})=(\emptyset,\{V(H)\setminus\partial(H)\})
.
Let us define the measure
\mu(Y,\mathcal{C})=|Y|+\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}|N_{H}(C)|^{2}
.
We have
\mu(Y_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})=|\partial(H)|^{2}
.
We are going to refine the single-faced decomposition until all the boundaried graphs
H\langle C\rangle
, for
C\in\mathcal{C}
, are 8-inseparable, in each step decreasing the measure
\mu
.

Let
(Y_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})
be a single-faced decomposition of
H
.
We use
Lemma

7.20
with
c=4
to either conclude that all
H\langle C\rangle
, for
C\in\mathcal{C}
, are 8-inseparable (then the procedure terminates)
or to find a canonical 4-separation
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
with respect to some single-faced embedding of
H\langle C\rangle
for some
C\in\mathcal{C}
. Now we apply
Lemma

7.22
to
H\langle C\rangle
and
(T_{1},T_{2},S)
.
We obtain sets
V_{1},V_{2}\subseteq C\setminus S
,
which form a partition of
C\setminus S
and satisfy
N_{H}(V_{i})\subseteq N_{H}(C)\cup S
.
Furthermore, each
H\langle V_{i}\rangle
is single-faced.
We define a new single-faced decomposition
(Y_{i+1},\mathcal{C}_{i+1})
, where
Y_{i+1}=Y_{i}\cup S
and
\mathcal{C}_{i+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{C}_{i}
by replacing
C
with
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
.

Lemma

7.22
guarantees that
|N_{H}(V_{1})|^{2}+|N_{H}(V_{2})|^{2}+|S|+1\leq|N_{H}(C)|^{2}
, therefore
\mu(Y_{i+1},\mathcal{C}_{i+1})\leq\mu(Y_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})-1
.

In each step the measure
\mu(Y_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})
decreases, so the algorithm terminates after at most
\mu(Y_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})=|\partial(H)|^{2}
steps.
Let
(Y_{\ell},\mathcal{C}_{\ell})
be the final single-faced decomposition.
We have
|Y_{\ell}|\leq\mu(Y_{\ell},\mathcal{C}_{\ell})\leq\mu(Y_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})=|\partial(H)|^{2}
.
For each
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}
, the boundaried graph
H\langle C\rangle
is single-faced (by the invariant of a single-faced decomposition) and 8-inseparable (because the algorithm has terminated).
∎

7.3
From inseparability to a solution preserver

In this section we show when
G\langle A\rangle
is single-faced and
c
-inseparable then the empty set forms an
\mathcal{O}(1)
-preserver for
A
.
In other words, for any solution
S
each vertex from
S\cap A
can be replaced by
\mathcal{O}(1)
vertices in
N_{G}(A)
.
First we need several observation about balls in the metric given by the weak radial distance.
Recall that for a plane graph
G
and
u,v\in V(G)
the weak radial distance
w_{G}(u,v)
is one less than the minimum length of a sequence of vertices that
starts at
u
, ends in
v
, and in which every two consecutive vertices either share an edge or lie on a common
interior
face. Recall also that
\mathcal{W}^{\ell}_{G}(X)
denotes the ball of weak radial distance at most
\ell
around vertex set
X
(see Figure
5
for an illustration).
The reason why we consider the weak radial distance instead of just the radial distance (allowing to make paths also through the outer face) becomes evident in the following proof.

Lemma 7.24
.

Let
H
be a single-faced boundaried plane graph and let
X\subseteq V(H)
.
Suppose that
T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq\partial(H)
are non-crossing.
If
\mathcal{W}^{\ell}_{H}(X)
is an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator, then there exists
X^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\ell}_{H}(X)
such that
X^{\prime}
is an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator and
|X^{\prime}|\leq(2\ell+1)\cdot|X|
.

Proof.

Let
H^{\prime\prime}
be obtained from
H
by adding a new vertex
t_{1}
adjacent to all
t
in
T_{1}
and a new vertex
t_{2}
adjacent to all
t
in
T_{2}
.
Since
T_{1},T_{2}
are non-crossing,
t_{1},t_{2}
can be inserted without modifying the embedding of
H
, within the outer face.
Let us abbreviate
X^{i}=\mathcal{W}^{i}_{H}(X)
.
By the assumption,
X^{\ell}
is a
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
H^{\prime\prime}
.

Consider a directed graph
R
with vertex set
X^{\ell}
defined as follows.
For each
x\in X^{i}\setminus X^{i-1}
,
i\in[\ell]
, we add a single edge from
x
to any vertex
y\in X^{i-1}
such that
x,y
share an interior face or
xy\in E(H)
.
Such vertex
y
always exists by the definition of the metric
w_{H}
.
Since any
x\in(X^{\ell}\setminus X)
has out-degree 1 in
R
, we can define a function

r\colon(X^{\ell}\setminus X)\to X^{\ell}
given by the arcs of
R
.

Let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\ell}
be defined by the following procedure.
Initialize
X^{\prime}=X^{\ell}
and while there exists
x\in X^{\prime}\setminus X
of in-degree 0 in
R
, such that
X^{\prime}\setminus x
is still a
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator in
H^{\prime\prime}
, remove
x
from
X^{\prime}
and from
R
.
Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be the connected components in
H^{\prime\prime}-X^{\prime}
containing
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, respectively.

We shall prove that
|X^{\prime}|\leq(2\ell+1)\cdot|X|
.
Let
R_{x}\subseteq X^{\prime}
be the set of vertices
y\in X^{\prime}
admitting a path
y\to x
in
R
, including
x
.
Fix
x\in X
and suppose that there are 3 vertices
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in R_{x}
, each of in-degree 0 in
R
. Since none of these 3 vertices were removed during construction of
X^{\prime}
, each of them is connected to both
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in
H^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
H_{0}
be obtained from
H^{\prime\prime}
be adding a new vertex
u_{f}
for each interior face of
H
and connecting
u_{f}
to all vertices lying on this face.
Recall that
H^{\prime\prime}
was obtained by adding two vertices inside the outer face, so they do not interfere with inserting vertices within interior faces.
Therefore,
H_{0}
is still a planar graph.
Let
Y\subseteq V(H_{0})
be obtained from
R_{x}\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{2}\}
by adding all the adjacent face-vertices.
Since
x
and
r(x)
either share an edge or an interior face, the graph
H_{0}[Y]
is connected.
The vertices
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
and the sets
Y,C_{1},C_{2}
form branch sets of a
K_{3,3}
minor in
H_{0}
, which is a contradiction to the assumption that such vertices
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
exist.

We infer that
R_{x}
can contain at most 2 vertices of in-degree 0 in
R
, and because the out-degree in
R
is at most 1, we obtain that

|R_{x}\cap X^{i}|\leq 2
for
i\in[\ell]
and so

|R_{x}|\leq 2\ell+1
.
Since the sets
R_{x}
sum up to
X^{\prime}
, it follows that
|X^{\prime}|\leq(2\ell+1)\cdot|X|
.
∎

Lemma

7.24
implies that for a set
X
and integer
\ell
, the set
\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\ell}(X)
behaves similarly as a set of size
(2\ell+1)\cdot|X|
with respect to separating non-crossing subsets of the boundary.
The following lemma deepens this analogy: we show
that in a
c
-inseparable boundaried plane graph
H
, if the number of boundary vertices is large compared to the size of some set
X
and integer
\ell
, then there must be unique component of
H-\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\ell}(X)
that contains almost all boundary vertices and all the other components have at most
\mathcal{O}(c\cdot\ell\cdot d)
of them.

Lemma 7.25
.

Let
H
be a
c
-inseparable single-faced
r
-boundaried plane graph,
\ell
be a positive integer, and let
X\subseteq V(H)
be of size
d
.
Suppose that
3c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d\leq r
. Then there is a single connected component in
H-\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\ell}(X)
containing at least
r-c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
vertices from
\partial(H)
.

Proof.

Let
X^{\prime}=\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\ell}(X)
.
We will consider two cases depending on the maximal number of boundary vertices in a single component of
H-X^{\prime}
.
First let us assume that each such component contains at most
c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
elements from
\partial(H)
or
\partial(H)\subseteq X^{\prime}
.
Consider the cyclic ordering of
\partial(H)
given by the single-faced embedding.
Let us choose an arbitrary element
t_{1}\in\partial(H)
and fix the ordering
t_{1},\dots,t_{r}
of
\partial(H)
to start at this element.

Let
S_{i}=\{t_{i}\}
if
t_{i}\in X^{\prime}
and otherwise let
S_{i}=R_{H}(t_{i},X^{\prime})\cap\partial(H)

be the subset of
\partial(H)
reachable from
t_{i}
in
H-X^{\prime}
.
Let
j
be the smallest index for which
|\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}S_{i}|\geq c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
.
By assumption it holds that
|S_{j}|\leq c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
, so we have

|\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}S_{i}|\leq 2c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
.
We define
T_{1}=\{t_{i}\mid i\in[j]\}

and
T_{2}=T\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}S_{i}
.
Then
T_{1},T_{2}
are disjoint, non-crossing, and
X^{\prime}
is an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator.
Lemma
7.24
guarantees that there exists an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator
X^{\prime\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
of size at most
(2\ell+1)\cdot d
.
Note that for each
i\in[j]
satisfying
t_{i}\not\in X^{\prime}
, the set
S_{i}
is contained in a single connected component of
H-X^{\prime}
, hence it is also contained in a single connected component of
H-X^{\prime\prime}
which is disjoint from
T_{2}
.
Therefore
X^{\prime\prime}
is also an unrestricted
(\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}S_{i},T_{2})
-separator.
By the construction,
|\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}S_{i}|\in[c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d,\,2c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d]
.
The lower bound
3c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d\leq r
implies that
|T_{2}|=r-\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}S_{i}\geq c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
,
which contradicts the assumption that
H
is
c
-inseparable.

Suppose now that there exists a connected component
C
of
H-X^{\prime}
so that
|C\cap\partial(H)|>c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
.
Let
T_{1}=\{t\}
for an arbitrary
t\in C\cap\partial(H)
and
T_{2}=\partial(H)\setminus C
.
Clearly
X^{\prime}
is an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator and
T_{1},T_{2}
are non-crossing.
By Lemma
7.24
we obtain an unrestricted
(T_{1},T_{2})
-separator
X^{\prime\prime}
such that
|X^{\prime\prime}|\leq(2\ell+1)\cdot d
and
X^{\prime\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
.
The latter property implies that
X^{\prime\prime}
is also an unrestricted
(\partial(H)\cap C,T_{2})
-separator.
Since
H
is
c
-inseparable, we have

|T_{2}|<c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
.
Therefore
|C\cap\partial(H)|=r-|T_{2}|>r-c\cdot(2\ell+1)\cdot d
, and so
C
is the desired component.
∎

When
G\langle A\rangle
is single-faced and
c
-inseparable, then for a solution
S
in
G
we consider the set
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(S\cap A)
after fixing some single-faced embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
.
This will allow us to construct 3 connected separators between any vertex
x\in S\cap A
and the large component from
Lemma

7.25
in order to argue for their irrelevance in the solution.
To construct such separators we rely on the following proposition.
A plane triangulation is a plane graph with every face being incident to exactly three vertices. Recall that when using the word
separator
without any adjective, we refer to a
restricted
separator that does not intersect the elements being separated.

Proposition 7.26

(
[
MT01
, Prop. 8.2.3]
)
.

Let
u
and
v
be distinct vertices in a plane triangulation
G
.
If
S
is a minimal
(u,v)
-separator, then
G[S]
is an induced, non-facial cycle.

We remark that this result has been originally formulated for sets whose removal disconnect the graph
but the same argument holds for minimal
(u,v)
-separators.

Lemma 7.27
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph,
x\in X\subseteq V(G)
, and

v\in V(G)\setminus\mathcal{W}_{G}^{\ell}(X)
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then there exists either a vertex
y\in\mathcal{W}_{G}^{\ell}(X)\setminus X
which separates
v
from
x
or

\ell
vertex-disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{W}_{G}^{\ell}(X)\setminus X
forming a sequence of nested
(v,x)
-separators. In the latter case, each separator is inclusion-wise minimal and either induces a cycle or a path with both endpoints on the outer face.

Proof.

Consider the block-cut tree of
G
and the sequence of vertices
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m}
, where
u_{1}=x
,
u_{m}=v
, and
u_{2},\dots,u_{m-1}
are the articulation points between
x
and
v
in
G
. If
x,v
belong to the same biconnected component, then
m=2
.
More precisely,
\{u_{i}\}
is a
(v,x)
-separator for
i\in[2,m-1]
and there are no more singleton
(v,x)
-separators in
G
apart from those in the sequence.
Let
j
be the highest index such that
u_{j}\in X
.
Note that
j
is well defined, as
u_{1}=x\in X
,
and furthermore
j\leq m-1
.
If
u_{j+1}\in\mathcal{W}_{G}^{\ell}(X)
, then in particular
u_{j+1}\neq v
and we can set
y=u_{j+1}
to satisfy the claim.
Otherwise, let
B\subseteq V(G)
be the biconnected component of
G
containing
u_{j}
and
u_{j+1}
.
If suffices to show that the claim holds for
x^{\prime}=u_{j}\in X
and
v^{\prime}=u_{j+1}\not\in\mathcal{W}_{G}^{\ell}(X)
, therefore we have reduced the problem to the case when
x,v
lie in a single biconnected component
B
of
G
.
Let us focus on this case from now on.

Let the graph
G_{B}^{\prime}
be obtained from
B
by

1.

adding a new vertex
u_{f}
in the center of each face
f
in
B
,

2.

for each new vertex
u_{f}
, connecting it to all the vertices from
B
lying on the face
f
.

Let us note several properties of
G_{B}^{\prime}
.
Let the set
U_{0}
consist of the new single vertex put in place of the outer face of
G
, if that vertex belongs to
V(G_{B}^{\prime})
, or be empty if the outer face is not adjacent to any vertex of
B
.

1.

If
u,v\in V(B)
and
uv\in E(G_{B}^{\prime})
, then
uv\in E(G)
.

2.

If
S\subseteq V(B)
is a
(v,x)
-separator in
G_{B}^{\prime}-U_{0}
, then
S
is a
(v,x)
-separator in
G
.

3.

Since
B
is a biconnected component of
G
, each face of
B
forms a cycle and thus
G_{B}^{\prime}
is a plane triangulation.

4.

Let
S_{j}=\{u\in V(B)\mid w_{G}(X,u)=j\}
. Then
S_{j}\cup U_{0}
is a
(v,x)
-separator in
G_{B}^{\prime}
for
j\in[\ell]
.

The last property requires a short justification.
Suppose there exists a
(v,x)
-path
P^{\prime}
in
G_{B}^{\prime}-(S_{j}\cup U_{0}
) and let
P
