be the sequence of vertices from
V(B)
occurring on
P^{\prime}
.
Since
P^{\prime}
does not use the vertex corresponding to the outer face, each pair of consecutive vertices
y_{i},y_{j}
from
P
share an interior face, thus
|w_{G}(X,y_{i})-w_{G}(X,y_{j})|\leq 1
.
Since
w_{G}(X,x)=0
and
w_{G}(X,v)>\ell\geq j
,
P
must contain a vertex from
S_{j}
; a contradiction.

We shall now show that for each
j\in[\ell]
there exists a set
S^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq S_{j}
such that
G[S^{\prime}_{j}]
is connected and
S^{\prime}_{j}\cup U_{0}
is a
(v,x)
-separator in
G_{B}^{\prime}
of one of the two desired forms.
Let
S^{0}_{j}
be an inclusion-wise minimal subset of
S^{\prime}_{j}\cup U_{0}
which is still a
(v,x)
-separator in
G_{B}^{\prime}
.
Since
G_{B}^{\prime}
is a plane triangulation,
Proposition

7.26
applies and we get that
G_{B}^{\prime}[S^{0}_{j}]
is an induced cycle.
If
S^{0}_{j}\cap U_{0}=\emptyset
, then
S^{0}_{j}
induces a cycle in
G
.
Otherwise, recall that
U_{0}
contains at most one element representing the outer face.
Removing a single vertex from a cycle turns it into a path, hence
G_{B}^{\prime}[S^{0}_{j}\setminus U_{0}]=G[S^{0}_{j}\setminus U_{0}]
induces a path. Since the vertex in
U_{0}
was placed in the outer face, the path has both endpoints on the outer face of
G
.
Note that
S^{\prime}_{j}=S^{0}_{j}\setminus U_{0}
is a
(v,x)
separator in
G_{B}^{\prime}-U_{0}
.
Because
S^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq S_{j}\subseteq V(B)
, we obtain that
S^{\prime}_{j}
is the desired
(v,x)
-separator in
B
, and thus in
G
.

These separators are clearly vertex-disjoint and contained in
\mathcal{W}_{G}^{\ell}(X)\setminus X
.
The fact that they can be ordered into a nested sequence follows from their connectivity and minimality.
For
i\neq j
, the set
S^{\prime}_{i}
must be fully contained in some component of
G-S^{\prime}_{j}
, precisely either in
R_{G}(x,S^{\prime}_{j})
or in
R_{G}(v,S^{\prime}_{j})
.
From minimality we get that each vertex of
S^{\prime}_{j}
is adjacent to both
R_{G}(x,S^{\prime}_{j})
and
R_{G}(v,S^{\prime}_{j})
.
In the first case
S^{\prime}_{i}
must separate
x
from
S^{\prime}_{j}
and the second case it must separate
v
from
S^{\prime}_{j}
.
Therefore, either

R_{G}(x,S^{\prime}_{i})\subseteq R_{G}(x,S^{\prime}_{j})

or
R_{G}(x,S^{\prime}_{j})\subseteq R_{G}(x,S^{\prime}_{i})
.
∎

Note that even though the proof gives a natural order of separators
(S^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell}
, they might be nested in a different order.
This is because we require
S^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq\{u\in B\mid w_{G}(X,u)=i\}
, not
S^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq\{u\in B\mid w_{G}(x,u)=i\}
. This is necessary to guarantee that
S^{\prime}_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
, which will be an important property in the proof of
Lemma

7.29
.

When given a single-faced embedding of a
c
-inseparable graph
G\langle A\rangle
and a solution
S
in
G
, we are going to remove
S\cap A
from the solution and replace it with some subset of
N_{G}(A)
, of size comparable to
|S\cap A|
.
As the new solution removes some vertices from
N_{G}(A)
we need to ensure that this cannot disconnect the separators constructed in
Lemma

7.27
.

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
G
be a connected plane graph,
u,v\in V(G)
, and
S\subseteq V(G)
be a minimal
(u,v)
-separator such that
G[S]
either induces a cycle or a path with both endpoints on the outer face.
If
Y\subseteq V(G)\setminus\{u,v\}
is a subset of vertices lying on the outer face of
G
and
S\not\subseteq Y
, then
S\setminus Y
is a connected
(u,v)
-separator in
G-Y
.

Proof.

It is clear that
S\setminus Y
is a
(u,v)
-separator in
G-Y
and we only need to show that it is connected.
We will rely on the observation that, since
S
is minimal,
each vertex
s\in S
is adjacent to both
R_{G}(u,S)
and
R_{G}(v,S)
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be obtained from
G
by adding a new vertex
f
adjacent to all vertices in
Y
.
Since they lie on the outer face of
G
, the graph
G^{\prime}
is planar.

Suppose that
S
induces a cycle and that
S\setminus Y
is not connected.
Then there exist vertices
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y\cap S
and a partition of
S\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
into connected vertex sets
S_{1},S_{2}

so that each edge set
E_{G}(y_{i},S_{j})
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
is non-empty.
The sets
R_{G}(u,S),S_{1}\cup R_{G}(v,S),S_{2},\{y_{1},f\},\{y_{2}\}
form branch sets of a
K_{5}
-minor model in
G^{\prime}
, which gives a contradiction.

Now suppose that
S
induces a path with both endpoints on the outer face and that
S\setminus Y
is not connected.
Then there exists vertex
y\in Y\cap S
and a partition of
S\setminus\{y\}
into connected vertex sets
S_{1},S_{2}

so that each of them contains a vertex lying on the outer face.
The sets
R_{G}(u,S),R_{G}(v,S),\{f\},S_{1},S_{2},\{y\}
form branch sets of a
K_{3,3}
-minor model in
G^{\prime}
, which again leads to a contradiction.
∎

We are ready to show that when
G\langle A\rangle
is single-faced and 8-inseparable then
\emptyset
is an
\alpha
-preserver for
A
, where
\alpha=3\cdot 8\cdot 7=168
.
We first fix a single-faced embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
.
When
S
is a solution in
G
and
X=S\cap A
,
we consider terminal pairs separated by
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)
and
use
Lemma

7.25
to identify
a subset of
N_{G}(A)
of size comparable to
|X|
, which intersects all the separated terminal pairs.
We can add it to
S
, creating a larger solution
S^{\prime}
(see Figure
5
).
We need to argue that vertices from
X
can be now removed from
S^{\prime}
without invalidating the solution.
To this end, we use
Lemma

7.27
to construct 3 nested connected separators between any
x\in X
and
N_{G}(A)\setminus S^{\prime}
in
G-(S^{\prime}\setminus\{x\})
which allows us to use the planarity criterion from
Lemma

3.17
.

Figure 5
:
An illustration of the proof of
Lemma

7.29
.
In the plane embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
the boundary vertices from
N_{G}(A)
are drawn in blue; they lie on the outer face.
The red squares represent vertices
X=\{x,y,z\}
in the intersection of a solution
S
and the set
A
.
The sets
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{1}(X)
,
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{2}(X)
,
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)
are sketched with shades of gray so, e.g., the vertices incident to the dark gray faces belong to
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{1}(X)
.
Due to
Lemma

7.25
, when
G\langle A\rangle
is
c
-inseparable there exists a connected component
C
of
G\langle A\rangle-\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)
, containing all but
\mathcal{O}(|X|)
boundary vertices.
The new solution
S^{\prime}
is obtained from
S
by adding the vertices from
N_{G}(A)\setminus C
(enclosed by red circles) and removing vertices from
X
.
For each vertex
v\in X
we can construct 3 connected nested
(v,C)
-separators in the gray area, which certifies that inserting
v
back into the graph does not affect planarity.

Lemma 7.29
.

Let
A\subseteq V(G)
be such that
G\langle A\rangle
is an 8-inseparable single-faced boundaried planar graph.
Then for any planar modulator
S\subseteq V(G)
,
there exists a planar modulator
S^{\prime}\subseteq(S\setminus A)\cup N_{G}(A)
,
such that
S^{\prime}\setminus N_{G}[A]=S\setminus N_{G}[A]
and
|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|+168\cdot|S\cap A|
.

Proof.

Let
r=|N_{G}(A)|
,
X=S\cap A
, and
d=|X|
.
If
3\cdot 8\cdot 7\cdot d>r
, then we set
S^{\prime}=(S\setminus A)\cup N_{G}(A)
.
Since the graph
G[A]
is planar,
G-(A\cup S^{\prime})
is a subgraph of
G-S
, and
G-S^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of these graphs, we get that
S^{\prime}
is also a planar modulator of the desired size. (This simple case actually forms the worst-case scenario for the size of
S^{\prime}
.)

Suppose now that
3\cdot 8\cdot 7\cdot d\leq r
.
Note that all boundary vertices are connected in
G\langle A\rangle
due to 8-inseparability and, by the definition of being single-faced (
Definition

3.12
),
G\langle A\rangle
is connected.
Let us fix any single-faced embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
, so
the set
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)
is well-defined.
We apply Lemma
7.25
to
G\langle A\rangle
and
X=S\cap A
, with
c=8
and
\ell=3
, to infer that there exists a single connected component
C
of
G\langle A\rangle-\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)
containing at least
r-8\cdot 7\cdot d
vertices from
N_{G}(A)
.
We set
S^{\prime}=(S\setminus A)\cup(N_{G}(A)\setminus C)
, which satisfies
S^{\prime}\setminus N_{G}[A]=S\setminus N_{G}[A]
.
Clearly,
|N_{G}(A)\setminus C|\leq 8\cdot 7\cdot d
and so

|S^{\prime}|\leq|S|+8\cdot 7\cdot|S\cap A|
.

We need to show that
S^{\prime}
is a planar modulator.
If
N_{G}(A)\cap C\subseteq S
, then
N_{G}(A)\subseteq S^{\prime}
and we again obtain that
G-S^{\prime}
is a disjoint union of planar graphs.
Suppose otherwise that there exists
v\in(N_{G}(A)\cap C)\setminus S
.
Let
X_{0}
be an inclusion-wise minimal subset of
X
, such that
G-(S^{\prime}\cup X_{0})
is planar.
Note that
X_{0}
is well-defined because
S\subseteq S^{\prime}\cup X
and thus
G-(S^{\prime}\cup X)
is planar.

We are going to prove that
X_{0}=\emptyset
, thereby establishing that
S^{\prime}
is a solution.
Suppose otherwise and let
x\in X_{0}
.
We apply
Lemma
7.27

to
G\langle A\rangle
, and
v,x,X
as above.
We obtain that in
G\langle A\rangle
there exists either (a) a vertex
y\in\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)\setminus X
which separates
v
from
x
or (b) 3 nested vertex-disjoint connected
(v,x)
-separators
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)\setminus X
.
Since
\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)\subseteq N_{G}[A]\setminus C
and
C\ni v
induces a connected subgraph of
G\langle A\rangle
, these must also be
(x,C)
-separators in
G\langle A\rangle
.

Let
X_{1}=X_{0}\setminus\{x\}
.
We want to show that
G_{1}=G-(S^{\prime}\cup X_{1})
is still planar.
Let us note an easy observation (*) that whenever
Y\subseteq N_{G}[A]\setminus(S^{\prime}\cup X)
is a restricted
(x,C\setminus S^{\prime})
-separator in
G\langle A\rangle-S^{\prime}
, then
R_{G_{1}}(x,Y)\subseteq A
.
To see this, consider any path in
G\langle A\rangle
that starts at
x

and reaches
N_{G}(A)\setminus S^{\prime}\subseteq C
:
it clearly must go through
S^{\prime}\cup Y
, so it is not present in
G_{1}-Y
.

In case (a) there exists a vertex
y\in\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)\setminus X
which separates
x
from
C
in
G\langle A\rangle
.
If
y\in S^{\prime}
then by observation (*) for
Y=\emptyset
we get that
the connected component of
x
in
G_{1}
is contained in
A
so it is planar
and the remaining components of
G_{1}
are subgraphs of
G-(S^{\prime}\cup X_{0})
, so
G_{1}
is planar.
If
y\not\in S^{\prime}
, then by observation (*) for
Y=\{y\}
we get that
R_{G_{1}}(x,\{y\})\subseteq A
, so that
R_{G_{1}}[x,\{y\}]\subseteq N_{G}[A]
. Hence the latter again induces a planar subgraph of
G_{1}
.
For any other component
U
of
G_{1}-y

the graph
G_{1}\langle U\rangle
is
a subgraph of
G-(S^{\prime}\cup X_{0})
, hence it is planar, and each biconnected component of
G_{1}
is planar and so we infer that
G_{1}
is planar.
This contradicts the assumption that
X_{0}
is minimal.

In case (b) there are 3 nested connected
(x,C)
-separators
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{G\langle A\rangle}^{3}(X)\setminus X
.
If for any
i\in[3]
the set
Y_{i}
is contained in
S^{\prime}
, then by observation (*) for
Y=\emptyset

we get that the connected component of
x
in
G_{1}
is contained in
A
so it is planar
and the remaining components of
G_{1}
are subgraphs of
G^{\prime}-X_{0}
, so
G_{1}
is planar.
Otherwise let
Y^{\prime}_{i}=Y_{i}\setminus S^{\prime}
for
i\in[3]
.
Note that
S^{\prime}\cap N_{G}[A]\subseteq N_{G}(A)
lies on the outer face of
G\langle A\rangle
, so by
Lemma

7.28
the sets
Y^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{2},Y^{\prime}_{3}
form nested connected
(x,C)
-separators in
G\langle A\rangle-S^{\prime}
.
Note that
Y^{\prime}_{i}\cap X_{1}\subseteq Y_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
so
Y^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq N_{G}[A]\setminus(S^{\prime}\cup X)
, so these are present in the graph
G_{1}
.
For each
i\in[3]

we take advantage of observation (*) with
Y=Y^{\prime}_{i}
to
see that
R_{G_{1}}[x,Y^{\prime}_{i}]\subseteq N_{G}[A]
and so these sets induce planar subgraphs of
G_{1}
.
Furthermore, the graph
G_{1}-x=G-(S^{\prime}\cup X_{0})
is planar by assumption.
By

Lemma

3.17
the graph
G_{1}
is planar.
This again contradicts the minimality of
X_{0}
. We conclude that
G-S^{\prime}
is planar.
∎

We have shown that when
G\langle A\rangle
is single-faced and 8-inseparable then any solution to
G
can be made disjoint from
A
with a small cost.
This finishes the proof of
Proposition

7.13
(restated below) and the construction of solution preservers for single-faced boundaried graphs.

See
7.13

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

7.23
to compute, in polynomial time, a boundaried decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{C})
of
G\langle A\rangle
, so that
|Y|\leq|N_{G}(A)|^{2}
and for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
it holds that
G\langle C\rangle
is single-faced and 8-inseparable.
By
Lemma

7.29
, the empty set forms a 168-preserver for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
.
Hence,
Lemma

7.7
implies
that
Y
is a 168-preserver for
A
.
∎

8
Compressing the negligible components

After collecting the solution preservers, we obtain a planar modulator
X^{\prime}\subseteq G
with the following property: there exists a 170-approximate solution
S^{\prime}
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}
.
In the final step of the algorithm we want to compress each connected component
C
of
G-X^{\prime}
; these component are called
negligible
.
By
Lemma

7.11
we can augment the solution preservers computed for boundaried plane graphs with bounded radial diameter, so that the negligible components can be represented with boundaried plane graphs which are circumscribed.
Recall that in a circumscribed boundaried plane graph
H

the image of each vertex in
\partial(H)
belongs to the outer face of
H-\partial(H)
.
We refer to the set of vertices of
H-\partial(H)
lying on the outer face as
\Delta(H)\subseteq V(H)\setminus\partial(H)
; equivalently, this is the first outerplanarity layer of
H-\partial(H)
.
We are going to decompose
H
into cycles and bridges, making it easier to analyze
H
through the criterion from

Lemma

3.15
. Refer to Figure
6
for an illustration of the following concept.

Definition 8.1
.

A bridge decomposition of a circumscribed
r
-boundaried plane graph
H
is a pair
(Y,\mathcal{B})
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

Y\subseteq\Delta(H)
and
|Y|\leq 24\cdot r
,

2.

\mathcal{B}
is a subfamily of the connected components of
H-(\partial(H)\cup\Delta(H))
with
|\mathcal{B}|\leq 24\cdot r
,

3.

each connected component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y\cup\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{B}}V(A))
has at most 2 neighbors in
\partial(H)
, at most
10
neighbors in
Y
, and is adjacent to at most 10 components from
\mathcal{B}
.

Figure 6
:
Schematic illustration of a bridge decomposition of a circumscribed 9-boundaried graph
H
. The boundary
\partial(H)
is drawn as red squares. The vertices of
\Delta(H)
are shown by circles. Each connected component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup\Delta(H))
is a shaded region. The set
Y
consists of the blue circles and the set
\mathcal{B}
of the single component inside the blue curve. After removing the blue and red objects, the neighborhoods of the resulting components are bounded.

Observe that if we only could assume that the subgraphs from
\mathcal{B}
have constant size, then we could mark all the vertices in
Y\cup\bigcup_{B\in\mathcal{B}}V(B)
, and the leftover components would have only
\mathcal{O}(1)
neighbors each, making them amenable to protrusion replacement.
However these subgraphs may be large and we will need to compress them.

First we show that a bridge decomposition can be easily computed with known techniques for processing bounded-treewidth subgraphs.

Lemma 8.2
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a circumscribed
r
-boundaried plane graph
H
, returns a bridge decomposition of
H
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}
denote the family of all connected components of
H-(\partial(H)\cup\Delta(H))
.
Consider a circumscribed
r
-boundaried plane graph
H^{\prime}
obtained from
H
by contracting each component from
\mathcal{B}_{0}
into a single vertex.
It is clear that this operation does not affect the boundary and the outer face, so we can write
\partial(H^{\prime})=\partial(H)
and
\Delta(H^{\prime})=\Delta(H)
.
Let us refer to the set of vertices obtained by contracting components from
\mathcal{B}_{0}
as
B^{\prime}\subseteq V(H^{\prime})
.
We have a natural mapping
\phi_{B}\colon B^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}_{0}
which maps vertices of
B^{\prime}
to their pre-image before contraction.
The graph
H^{\prime}-\partial(H^{\prime})
is 2-outerplanar (see Definition
6.7
) therefore its treewidth is at most 5
[
Bod98
]
.
A corresponding tree decomposition of width 5 can be found in polynomial time
[
Bod96
]
.

We apply
Lemma

3.7
to
H^{\prime}
with
S=\partial(H^{\prime})
and
\eta=5
to mark a set
Y^{\prime}\subseteq V(H^{\prime})\setminus\partial(H^{\prime})
such that
|Y^{\prime}|\leq 24\cdot r
and
each connected component of
H^{\prime}-(\partial(H^{\prime})\cup Y^{\prime})
has at most 2 neighbors in
\partial(H^{\prime})
and at most
10
neighbors in
Y^{\prime}
.
Let
Y=Y^{\prime}\cap\Delta(H^{\prime})
be the set of marked vertices on the outer face,
\mathcal{B}=\{\phi_{B}(b)\mid b\in Y^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}\}
be the set of connected components from
\mathcal{B}_{0}
that got marked after contraction.
Each connected component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y\cup\bigcup_{B\in\mathcal{B}}V(B))
corresponds to a connected component of
H^{\prime}-(\partial(H^{\prime})\cup Y^{\prime})
and its neighborhood is the union of the corresponding branch sets.
The claim follows.
∎

The next lemma explains the correspondence between a bridge decomposition and the concept of a
C
-bridge.
The assumption that
H
is circumscribed plays an important role here. Recall the notion of overlap graph
O
from Lemma
3.15
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
(Y,\mathcal{B})
be a bridge decomposition of a circumscribed boundaried plane graph
H
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
.
Then there exists a cycle
C
such that
V(C)\subseteq\Delta(H)
and
B
is a connected component of
H-V(C)
. Furthermore, the graph
O(H-\partial(H),C)
has no edges, that is, no two
C
-bridges in
H-\partial(H)
overlap.

Proof.

Consider a circumscribed boundaried plane graph
H^{\prime}
obtained from
H
by contracting each component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup\Delta(H))
into a single vertex.
Let
b
be the vertex given by contracting
B
. Since
B\cap\Delta(H)=\emptyset
, vertex
b
does not lie on the outer face of
H^{\prime}
. Hence the image of
b
lies in an interior face
f
of
H[\Delta(H)]
consisting of vertices which lie on the outer face of
H
, which implies that the boundary cycle of
f
is a simple cycle
C
with
V(C)\subseteq\Delta(H)
. We proceed to show that
C
has the desired properties.

Observe that
N_{H^{\prime}}(b)\subseteq V(C)
: by planarity of the embedding,
b
cannot have neighbors on the exterior of
C
, while
b
cannot have neighbors embedded in the proper interior of
C
as they would have been part of the same connected component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup\Delta(H))
and therefore would have been contracted when forming
b
. Hence
N_{H^{\prime}}(b)\subseteq V(C)
which implies
N_{H}(B)\subseteq V(C)
. Since
H[B]
is connected, this implies
B
is a connected component of
H-V(C)
. It remains to prove that no two
C
-bridges in
H-\partial(H)
overlap.

Note that for each connected component
A
of
H-V(C)
that is embedded on the exterior of
C
, we have
|N_{G}(A)\cap V(C)|\leq 2
: if
A
had at least three neighbors on the cycle
C
, then one of these neighbors does not lie on the outer face of
H
since it is enclosed by
A
and a path around the cycle. The same argument shows that if
|N_{G}(A)\cap V(C)|=2
, then the two neighbors of
A
on
C
are consecutive along
C
.
Note that by definition, such a
C
-bridge does not overlap any other
C
-bridges.
This holds also when
|N_{G}(A)\cap V(C)|=1
.
Finally, no
C
-bridge that is a chord of
C
can be embedded on the exterior of
C
, as it would again separate a vertex of
C
from the outer face.

When it comes to
C
-bridges corresponding to connected components of
H-V(C)
or chords of
C
embedded on the interior of
C
, by
3.14
any two
C
-bridges which overlap are embedded on opposite sides of
C
, so there are no overlaps between
C
-bridges embedded in the interior. The claim follows.
∎

Let
G
be a graph,
D\subseteq V(G)
, and consider the embedding of
G\langle D\rangle
as a circumscribed boundaried plane graph
H
.
If
A\in\mathcal{B}
is a
C
-bridge for some cycle
C
within
\Delta(H)
, then any planar modulator
S
in
G
which is disjoint from
D
, remains valid if we replace
A
with an equivalent
C
-bridge, that is, having the same attachments at
C
and not violating planarity of
A\cup C
(see

Lemma

3.15
).
This holds in particular for a bridge obtained by contracting
A
into a single vertex.
However such a modification is insufficient to provide the backward safeness: for any solution of size at most
k
(possibly intersecting
D
) we need to be able to lift it back.
A possible “dirty” solution is to mark such vertices as undeletable.
This however would lead to producing an instance of a different problem (which could be harder to approximate) and would by insufficient to obtain
1.2
.
Instead, we will replace
A
with a
well
of depth
\mathcal{O}(k)
and perimeter
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
We perform this operation in several steps.
First we introduce the concept of drawing cycles within
C
surrounding the vertices from
A
.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
A
be a vertex subset of a plane graph
G
, so that the induced embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
is circumscribed.
We say that
A
is
c
-well-nested in
G
if the following conditions hold:

1.

the first
c
outerplanarity layers of
G[A]
are cycles of fixed length
m\geq 3
, the
i
-th outer cycle comprising vertices
v^{i}_{1},\dots,v^{i}_{m}
, ordered clockwise,

2.

for each
j\in[m]
, there is at most one vertex
u\in N_{G}(A)
such that
uv^{1}_{j}\in E(G)
,

3.

v^{i}_{j}
is adjacent to
v^{i+1}_{j}
for all
i\in[c-1]
,
j\in[m]
.

Figure 7
:
A result of 3-nesting the vertex set given by the five vertices in the center.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
A
be a vertex subset of a (boundaried) plane graph
G
, so that the induced embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
is circumscribed and
E_{G}(A,V(G)\setminus A)\geq 3
.
The act of
c
-nesting the set
A
means
replacing
G
by
G^{\prime}
given by

1.

enumerating the edges in
E_{G}(A,V(G)\setminus A)
in clockwise order with respect to the embedding of
G\langle A\rangle
; let us refer to them as
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{m}
,

2.

subdividing each edge
e_{i}

c
times, creating vertices
v^{1}_{i},v^{2}_{i},\dots,v^{c}_{i}
, counting towards the endpoint in
A
,

3.

connecting vertices
v^{j}_{i}
and
v^{j}_{i+1}
for
j\in[c],i\in[m-1]
, as well as
v^{j}_{m}
and
v^{j}_{1}
for each
j\in[c]
,

and replacing
A
with
A^{\prime}
being the union of
A
and all the new vertices.
See Figure
7
.
We treat the remaining vertices and the corresponding points in the embedding as unaffected, that is,
V(G^{\prime})\setminus A^{\prime}=V(G)\setminus A
.

The operation of
c
-nesting is clearly backward safe as the original graph is a minor of the graph after the modification.
We take note of several other useful properties of
c
-nesting.

Observation 8.6
.

Let
H
be a circumscribed boundaried plane graph with a bridge decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{B})
and
A\in\mathcal{B}
.
Suppose that
H^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
have been obtained by
c
-nesting the set
A
.
Then the following hold.

1.

H^{\prime}
is a circumscribed boundaried plane graph,

2.

(Y,\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
is a bridge decomposition of
H^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is given by replacing
A
with
A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}
,

3.

the set
A^{\prime}
is
c
-well-nested in
H^{\prime}
,

4.

the size of each of the
c
outermost layers in
H^{\prime}[A^{\prime}]
equals the number of edges in
E_{H^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},V(H^{\prime})\setminus A^{\prime})
, which is the same as the number of edges in
E_{H}(A,V(H)\setminus A)
,

5.

H
can be obtained back from
H^{\prime}
by removing or contracting edges which are non-incident to the boundary
\partial(H)=\partial(H^{\prime})
.

We now prove the forward safeness of
c
-nesting with respect to solutions disjoint from the subgraph being decomposed.

Lemma 8.7
.

Consider a graph
G
, vertex set
D\subseteq V(G)
,
so that
G\langle D\rangle
admits a circumscribed embedding, and let
H=G\langle D\rangle
denote the corresponding circumscribed boundaried plane graph.
Let
(Y,\mathcal{B})
be a bridge decomposition of
H
and
A\in\mathcal{B}
.
Suppose that
(H^{\prime},A^{\prime})
has been obtained by
c
-nesting the set
A
in
H
and let
G^{\prime}=G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]\oplus H^{\prime}
.
If
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus D
is a planar modulator in
G
, then
it is a planar modulator in
G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.3
there exists a cycle
C
such that
V(C)\subseteq\Delta(H)\subseteq D
and
A
is a connected component of
H-V(C)
.
Since
S\cap D=\emptyset
,

A
is also a connected component of
(G-S)-V(C)
.
We apply Lemma
3.15
with respect to
G-S
and
C
.
The overlap graph
O(G-S,C)
is not affected by
c
-nesting the set
A
, and the only
C
-bridge that has been modified is the one generated by component
A
of
(G-S)-V(C)
, which is replaced by
A^{\prime}
.
The graph induced by
A^{\prime}\cup V(C)
is a subgraph of

H^{\prime}
, hence it is planar.
Therefore
S
is still a planar modulator in
G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]\oplus H^{\prime}
.
∎

After
c
-nesting some set of vertices
A
for
c=\Omega(k)
, they become surrounded by
\Omega(k)
nested cyclic
A
-planarizing separators.
This suffices to make each vertex in
A

k
-irrelevant.
We would like now to remove the vertex set
A
but the number of vertices introduced during
c
-nesting might still be large if the number of edges outgoing from
A
was large.
In order to handle this, we first state a simple criterion for proving that some separator planarizes a vertex.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
G
be a graph,
A\subseteq V(G)
,
S\subseteq A
, and
v\in A\setminus S
.
If
R_{G\langle A\rangle}(v,S)\cap N_{G}(A)=\emptyset
then

R_{G\langle A\rangle}(v,S)=R_{G}(v,S)
.

Proof.

Assume the contrary: then there exists a path
P
in
G-S
that starts at
v
and ends at
u\not\in R_{G\langle A\rangle}(v,S)
.
The path
P
cannot be fully contained in
A
because then we would have
u\in R_{G\langle A\rangle}(v,S)
.
Therefore the path
P
intersects
N_{G}(A)
: let
P^{\prime}
be the subpath of
P
starting at
v
and ending with the first occurrence of a vertex
x
from
V(P)\cap N_{G}(A)
.
Then
P^{\prime}
witnesses that
x\in R_{G\langle A\rangle}(v,S)
but this contradicts the assumption that
R_{G\langle A\rangle}(v,S)\cap N_{G}(A)=\emptyset
.
The claim follows.
∎

The next lemma states then when the set
A\in\mathcal{B}
is
(k+5)
-nested in
G\langle A\rangle
and the number of edges outgoing from
A
is large compared to
k
and
|N_{G}(A)|
, then there exists an edge which is
k
-irrelevant.
Recall the notion of a pseudo-nested sequence from
Definition

3.20
.
In the following proof, we slightly abuse the notation and write
R_{G}(v,P)
to denote
R_{G}(v,V(P))
when
P
is a subgraph of
G
(a path or a cycle).

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
G
be a graph,
k
be an integer, and

D\subseteq V(G)
with
|N_{G}(D)|=r
.
Suppose that
G\langle D\rangle
has a
circumscribed embedding and let

(Y,\mathcal{B})
be a bridge decomposition of
G\langle D\rangle
with respect to this embedding.
Suppose that
A\in\mathcal{B}
is
(k+5)
-well-nested and satisfies
|E_{G}(A,N_{G}(A))|>2\cdot r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)^{3}
.
Then there exist vertices
v\in\partial_{G}(A)
,
u\in N_{G}(A)
, such that
uv\in E(G)
and one of the following holds:

1.

there exists a sequence of
k+3
nested
v
-planarizing connected vertex sets in
G
, or

2.

there exists a sequence of
k+3
pseudo-nested
v
-planarizing cycles in
G
.

Furthermore, such a pair
(u,v)
can be found in polynomial time.

Proof.

Let
A_{i}
be the
i
-th outermost cycle in
G[A]
, where
i\in[k+5]
, and let
m
denote the length of each cycle
A_{i}=(v^{i}_{1},\dots,v^{i}_{m})
.
Let
C
be a cycle for which
N_{G}(A)\subseteq V(C)\subseteq D
and
A
is a connected component of
G\langle D\rangle-V(C)
and therefore of
G-V(C)
, guaranteed to exist by Lemma
8.3
.
We shall consider three scenarios. We refer to Figure
8
for an illustration.

First, suppose there exists a vertex
u\in N_{G}(A)
such that
|N_{G}(u)\cap A|\geq 2k+7
.
Since the vertices from
N_{G}(u)\cap A
lie on the cycle
A_{1}
, we can choose an integer sequence
(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{2k+7})
so that (1) for
i\in[2k+7]
we have
j_{i}\in[m]
and
uv^{1}_{j_{i}}\in E(G)
, (2) the vertices
v^{1}_{j_{1}},v^{1}_{j_{2}},\dots,v^{1}_{j_{2k+7}}
lie in this order on the cycle
A_{1}
,
and (3) the edges
uv^{1}_{j_{1}},uv^{1}_{j_{2k+7}}
separate the exterior of cycle
A_{1}
into a bounded region containing all the remaining edges
uv^{1}_{j_{2}},\dots uv^{1}_{j_{2k+6}}
and an unbounded region containing all vertices from
N_{G}(D)
(see Figure
8
on the left, where the edges
uv^{1}_{j_{1}},\ldots,uv^{1}_{j_{2k+7}}
are the edges between
u
and the blue cycle
A_{1}
, from left to right).

Let
v=v^{1}_{j_{k+4}}
.
Intuitively, a cycle
C_{i}
consists of two paths starting in
u
: the two paths respectively go to the vertices
i
steps left and right of
v
, move inwards over
i+1
cycles of the well, connecting to each other via a subpath of the cycle
A_{i+1}
.

More precisely,
for
i\in[k+3]
we set
h(i,1)=j_{k+4-i}
and
h(i,2)=j_{k+4+i}
to denote the two indices that are in distance
i
from the middle.
We define a path
P^{1}_{i}
as
(u,v^{1}_{h(i,1)},v^{2}_{h(i,1)},\dots,v^{i+1}_{h(i,1)})
and similarly
P^{2}_{i}
as
(u,v^{1}_{h(i,2)},v^{2}_{h(i,2)},\dots,v^{i+1}_{h(i,2)})
.
We define a cycle
C_{i}
as a concatentaion of paths
P^{1}_{i}
,
P^{2}_{i}
and a path
that connects
v^{i+1}_{h(i,1)}
and
v^{i+1}_{h(i,2)}
within the cycle
A_{i+1}
;
there are two choices for the latter path and we choose the one that, together with
P^{1}_{i}
and
P^{2}_{i}
, separates
v
from
V(A_{i+2})
.

We show that
(C_{1},\dots,C_{k+3})
is a sequence of pseudo-nested
v
-planarizing cycles.
For each
i\in[k+3]
,
the cycle
C_{i}
in the plane graph
G\langle D\rangle
separates the plane into two regions: one contains
v
and the other one
V(A_{i+2})
and
N_{G}(D)
.
By
Lemma

8.8
we get that

R_{G}(v,C_{i})=R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,C_{i})\subseteq A
.
This implies that
R_{G}[v,C_{i}]
induces a planar graph.
Furthermore, let

1\leq i<j\leq k+3
.
By the construction,
V(C_{i})\cap V(C_{j})=\{u\}
.
The cycle
C_{i}
separates
v
from
V(C_{j})\setminus\{u\}
in
G\langle D\rangle
.
By the same argument as above, we obtain that

R_{G}(v,C_{i})\cap V(C_{j})=\emptyset
.

Before analyzing the second and third scenario, let us greedily construct a family of paths
\mathcal{P}
,
which is initialized as empty.
As long as we can find a path in
G\langle D\rangle
connecting
A
to
N_{G}(D)
which is internally vertex-disjoint from the already constructed paths, we choose a shortest path with this property and insert it to
\mathcal{P}
.
Such a family can be easily constructed in polynomial time. Recall that
A_{1}
is the set of vertices on the outer face of
G[A]
, i.e., its first outerplanarity layer.
By the definition of a
c
-well-nested set, each vertex
x\in A
can have at most one neighbor in
N_{G}(A)
, and it is possible only when
x\in A_{1}
.
Because we always choose a shortest path, which does not contain vertices from
A
in its interior, the
A
-endpoints of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
are therefore distinct and belong to
A_{1}
.
The usage of shortest paths also implies that (*) the interior of each path
P\in\mathcal{P}
does not contain any vertices from
N_{G}(D)
and (**) the set
V(P)
contains exactly one vertex from
N_{G}(A)
.

Figure 8
:
Illustration of three scenarios in the proof of
Lemma

8.9
. Each figure shows a circumscribed boundaried plane graph
G\langle D\rangle
whose boundary vertices are shown as red squares, for which the vertices of
\Delta(G\langle D\rangle)
are visualized with black circles. A bridge decomposition of the graph consists of
Y=\emptyset
, while
\mathcal{B}
consists of a single connected component
A
of
(G\langle D\rangle)-(\partial(G\langle D\rangle)\cup\Delta(G\langle D\rangle))
, bounded by the blue cycle
A_{1}
. Left: a sequence of pseudo-nested
v
-planarizing cycles constructed in the first scenario when some vertex
u\in N_{G}(A)
has many neighbors in
A
. Middle: a sequence of pseudo-nested
v
-planarizing cycles constructed in the second scenario. Right: A sequence of nested connected
v
-planarizing sets constructed in the third scenario. The cycle
C
is the cycle formed by the black round vertices. The segment
S
of this cycle is highlighted in bold. The paths from the family
\mathcal{P}
are drawn in green.

As the second scenario we consider the case
|\mathcal{P}|>r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)
.
As
|N_{G}(D)|=r
, this implies that for some
t\in N_{G}(D)
there exists a subfamily
\mathcal{P}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{P}

of
\ell>r\cdot(2k+7)
internally vertex-disjoint
(A,t)
-paths in
G\langle D\rangle
.
Since the
A
-endpoints of these paths are distinct and lie on the cycle
A_{1}
, we can choose an ordering
(P_{1},\dots,P_{\ell})
of
\mathcal{P}_{t}
and an integer sequence
(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{\ell})
so that (1) for
i\in[\ell]
we have
j_{i}\in[m]
and
P_{i}
is a
(t,v^{1}_{j_{i}})
-path, (2) the vertices
v^{1}_{j_{1}},v^{1}_{j_{2}},\dots,v^{1}_{j_{\ell}}
lie in this order on the cycle
A_{1}
,
and (3) the paths
P_{1},P_{\ell}
separate the exterior of cycle
A_{1}
into a bounded region containing all the remaining paths
P_{2},\dots P_{\ell-1}
and an unbounded region.
Since
A_{1}
is a cycle, we
can construct regions
R_{1},\dots,R_{\ell-1}
, so that
R_{i}
is the bounded subset of the plane surrounded by
P_{i}
,
P_{i+1}
, and
A_{1}
(exclusively), for each distinct
i,j\in[\ell-1]
we have
R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset
and for each
i\in[\ell-1]
the region
R_{i}
does not intersect any paths from
(P_{1},\dots,P_{\ell})
.

We say that index
i\in[\ell-1]
is
contaminated
if
R_{i}
contains a vertex from
N_{G}(D)
. Since the sets
R_{i}
are disjoint and
t\in N_{G}(D)
is not properly contained in any of them, there can be at most
r-1
contaminated indices and there exists a contiguous subsequence
[\ell_{1},\ell_{3}]\subseteq[\ell-1]
of length
2k+7
, which do not contain any contaminated indices.
For
i\in[k+3]
and
\ell\in[\ell_{1},\ell_{3}]

we define the extended path
Q^{i}_{\ell}
as the concatenation of the path
P_{\ell}
with the path
(v^{1}_{j_{\ell}},v^{2}_{j_{\ell}},\dots,v^{i+1}_{j_{\ell}})
within
A
.
Let
\ell_{2}
be the middle index in
[\ell_{1},\ell_{3}]
.
For
i\in[k+3]
, we define cycle
C_{i}
as the concatenation of the extended paths
Q^{i}_{\ell_{2}-i}
,
Q^{i}_{\ell_{2}+i}
(they intersect only at
t
) with the path
connecting the endpoints of the extended paths within
A_{i+1}
(see Figure
8
in the middle).
Similarly as before, there are two choices for the path in
A_{i+1}
and we choose the one that makes
V(C_{i})
a
(v,V(A_{i+2}))
-separator in
G\langle D\rangle
.

Let
v=v^{1}_{j_{\ell_{2}}}
.
We show that
(C_{1},\dots,C_{k+3})
is a sequence of pseudo-nested
v
-planarizing cycles.
Let
1\leq i<j\leq k+3
.
By the construction,
V(C_{i})\cap V(C_{j})=\{t\}
.
The cycle
C_{i}
in the plane graph
G\langle D\rangle
separates the plane into two regions: one containing
v
and the other one containing
V(C_{j})\setminus\{t\}
.
By the elimination of contaminated regions and property (*), the set
R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,V(C_{i}))
does not contain any vertices from
N_{G}(D)
.
By
Lemma

8.8
we get that

R_{G}(v,C_{i})=R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,C_{i})\subseteq D
.
This implies that
R_{G}[v,C_{i}]
induces a planar graph and
R_{G}(v,C_{i})\cap V(C_{j})=\emptyset
.

Finally, suppose
that none of the previous two scenarios occurs.
We have
|\mathcal{P}|\leq r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)
and
|N_{G}(A)|\geq|E_{G}(A,N_{G}(A))|\,/\,(2k+6)\geq 2\cdot r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)^{2}
.
Let
U=N_{G}(A)\cap\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}V(P)
.
By property (**) each path
P\in\mathcal{P}
goes through exactly one vertex from
N_{G}(A)

and so
|U|\leq r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)
.
Recall the cycle
C
in
G\langle D\rangle
for which
N_{G}(A)\subseteq V(C)
.
The set
U
divides the cycle
C
into
|U|
segments;
these segments are considered without their endpoints in
U
and some of them may be empty.
By the pigeonhole principle, one of these segments must contain
at least
(|N_{G}(A)|-|U|)\,/\,|U|\geq|N_{G}(A)|\,/\,(2\cdot|U|)\geq 2k+7
vertices from
N_{G}(A)
.
Let us refer to this segment as
S\subseteq V(C)
and equip it with the order inherited from the clockwise orientation of the cycle
C
.
Then there exists a sequence of distinct vertices
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{2k+7}
from
S\cap N_{G}(A)
, respecting the order above
and an integer sequence
(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{2k+7})
so that (1) for
i\in[\ell]
we have
j_{i}\in[m]
and
u_{i}v^{1}_{j_{i}}\in E(G)
, and (2) the vertices
v^{1}_{j_{1}},v^{1}_{j_{2}},\dots,v^{1}_{j_{2k+7}}
lie in this order on the cycle
A_{1}
.

We set
v=v^{1}_{j_{k+4}}
.
and
h(i,1)=j_{k+4-i}
and
h(i,2)=j_{k+4+i}
.
For
i\in[k+3]
we define a path
P^{1}_{i}
as

(u_{k+4-i},v^{1}_{{h(i,1)}},v^{2}_{{h(i,1)}},\dots,v^{i+1}_{{h(i,1)}})
and
P^{2}_{i}
as

(u_{k+4+i},v^{1}_{{h(i,2)}},v^{2}_{{h(i,2)}},\dots,v^{i+1}_{{h(i,2)}})
.
The path
P_{i}
is obtained as a concatenation of
P^{1}_{i}
, the path connecting
v^{i+1}_{{h(i,1)}}
and
v^{i+1}_{{h(i,2)}}
within
A_{i+1}
, and finally
P^{2}_{i}
.
As before, we choose the path within
A_{i+1}
to make
V(P_{i})
a
(v,V(A_{i+2}))
-separator in
G\langle A\rangle

(see Figure
8
on the right).
Note that
V(P_{i})\subseteq A\cup(S\cap N_{G}(A))
is disjoint from all the paths from
\mathcal{P}
.
We shall prove that
(V(P_{1}),\dots,V(P_{k+3}))
is a sequence of nested connected
v
-planarizing sets.

We want to show that for each
i\in[k+3]
it holds that
R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,P_{i})\cap N_{G}(D)=\emptyset
.
Suppose otherwise, that there exists a path
P^{v}
in
G\langle D\rangle-V(P_{i})
which connects
v
to
N_{G}(D)
.
This path must intersect the interior of some path
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
because of the greedy construction.
The set
V(P^{\prime})\cap V(C)
contains a vertex from outside
S
.
Therefore the set
V(P^{v})\cup V(P^{\prime})
contains a path
P^{\prime\prime}
connecting
v
to
V(C)\setminus S
in
G\langle D\rangle-V(P_{i})
.
Let us consider the intersection of
V(P^{\prime\prime})
with
V(C)
.
First,
V(P^{\prime\prime})
must intersect the subsegment of
S
between
u_{k+4-i}
and
u_{k+4+i}
(exclusively)
by the construction of
P_{i}
; let us refer to this subsegment as
S_{i}
.
Hence, there is a subpath of
P^{\prime\prime}
which connects
S_{i}
to
V(C)\setminus(P_{i}\cup S_{i})
, internally disjoint from
V(C)
.
The interior of this subpath must be contained in some
C
-bridge
B
other than the one generated by the component
A
of
G-V(C)
. However, as
u_{k+4-i}
and
u_{k+4+i}
are attachments of the bridge generated by the connected component
A
of
G-V(C)
, the bridges
A
and
B
overlap.
This is impossible due to
Lemma

8.3
.

We have shown that
R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,P_{i})\cap N_{G}(D)=\emptyset
, so by
Lemma

8.8
we get that
R_{G}(v,P_{i})=R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,P_{i})
and so
R_{G}[v,P_{i}]
induces a planar graph.
Let
i<j\leq k+3
.
By the construction,
V(P_{i})
and
V(P_{j})
are disjoint.
To see that
R_{G\langle D\rangle}(v,P_{i})\cap V(P_{j})=\emptyset
, observe that
V(P_{j})
belongs to the same component of
G\langle D\rangle-V(P_{i})
as
V(A_{i+2})
.
By the construction, this component does not contain
v
.

We have thus proven that there always exist a vertex
v
, with a neighbor in
N_{G}(A)
, that satisfies the conditions of the lemma.
The proof relies on degree counting and computing shortest paths, therefore it can be turned into a polynomial-time algorithm.
∎

We are ready to prove the main lemma of this section.
Given the boundaried graph
G\langle D\rangle
with a circumscribed embedding, we compute a bridge decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{B})
to identify
\mathcal{O}(r)
subgraphs that need to compressed.
For
A\in\mathcal{B}
, we first perform
c
-nesting for
c=k+5
, to make it amenable to
Lemma

8.9
.
This allows us to reduce the number of edges outgoing from
A
.
Next, we perform
c
-nesting again (this time it suffices to take
c=k+3
) to surround all the vertices in
A
with
k+3
cycles of length
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and remove vertices from
A
due to their
k
-irrelevance.
The properties of the bridge decomposition guarantees that marking all the vertices in
Y
and the union of closed neighborhoods from
\mathcal{B}
(in total
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
vertices now) yields a decomposition into subgraphs of constant neighborhood size, which can be reduced by protrusion replacement.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
G
be a graph,
k
be an integer, and

D\subseteq V(G)
with
|N_{G}(D)|=r
.
Suppose that
H=G\langle D\rangle
admits a
circumscribed embedding.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G,D,k
, and a circumscribed embedding of
H
,
outputs a labeled
r
-boundaried planar graph
H^{\prime}
, and a vertex set
F^{\prime}\subseteq V(H^{\prime})\setminus\partial(H^{\prime})
such that the following conditions hold.

1.

each connected component of
H^{\prime}-(\partial(H^{\prime})\cup F^{\prime})
has at most 32 neighbors,

2.

|F^{\prime}|\leq 50\cdot r^{3}\cdot(2k+7)^{4}
,

3.

if
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus D
is a planar modulator in
G
, then it is a planar modulator in
H^{\prime}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
.

4.

there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G,D,G^{\prime}=H^{\prime}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
, and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
k
, returns a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
|S^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

We begin by computing a bridge decomposition
(Y,\mathcal{B})
of
G\langle D\rangle
(
Lemma

8.2
).
For a set
A\in\mathcal{B}
with at least 3 edges in
E_{H}(A,V(H)\setminus A)

we will perform a series of graph
modifications and analyze a series of tuples
(H_{i},Y_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i},A_{i})
, where
H_{i}
is a boundaried plane graph,
(Y_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})
is a bridge decomposition of
H_{i}
, and
A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
.
The lower bound on the number of outgoing edges is necessary to perform
c
-nesting in a well-defined way.
As a goal, we want to replace
A
with a subgraph of moderate size and moderate neighborhood.
There are two kinds of safeness we need to ensure during the graph modification process.

Forward safeness
means that for every vertex set
S\subseteq V(G_{\partial}[\overline{D}])
if
S
is a planar modulator in
H_{i}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
, then it is also a planar modulator in
H_{i+1}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
.

Backward safeness
means that any planar modulator
S
in
H_{i+1}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
of size at most
k
can be turned, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator in
H_{i}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
of size at most
|S|
.

Let
\alpha=2\cdot r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)^{3}
and
A\in\mathcal{B}
be fixed.
We have
H_{1}=G\langle D\rangle
,
(Y_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1})
=
(Y,\mathcal{B})
, and
A_{1}=A\in\mathcal{B}
.
We obtain
(H_{2},Y_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2},A_{2})
by

(k+5)
-nesting the set
A_{1}
.
The forward safeness of this modification follows directly from
Lemma

8.7
.
The backward safeness holds because
H_{1}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
is a minor of
H_{2}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
(
8.6
(
5
)).
In the next step we reduce the number of outgoing edges from
A_{2}
in
H_{2}
.
As long as it holds that

|E_{H_{2}}(A_{2},V(H_{2})\setminus A_{2})|>\alpha
,
we apply
Lemma

8.9

to find vertices
v\in\partial_{H_{2}}(A_{2})
,
u\in N_{H_{2}}(A_{2})
, such that
uv\in E(H_{2})
and one of the following holds:

1.

there exists a sequence of
k+3
nested
v
-planarizing connected vertex sets in
H_{2}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
, or

2.

there exists a sequence of
k+3
pseudo-nested
v
-planarizing cycles in
H_{2}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
.

In both cases the edge
uv
is
k
-irrelevant due to Lemmas
3.19
and
3.21
, which implies backward safeness of removing
uv
.
The forward safeness of an edge removal is trivial.

At some point we reach a situation where
these reductions rules are no longer applicable.
Let
(H_{3},Y_{3},\mathcal{B}_{3},A_{3})
denote the graph and the decomposition after performing the edge deletions.
We have

|E_{H_{3}}(A_{3},V(H_{3})\setminus A_{3})|\leq\alpha
, which
implies that
|N_{H_{3}}(A_{3})|\leq\alpha
.
We obtain
(H_{4},Y_{4},\mathcal{B}_{4},A_{4})
by
(k+3)
-nesting the set
A_{3}
.
The forward and backward safeness again follow from
Lemma

8.7
and the minor relation.
By
8.6
(
4
) we know that the size of each of the
(k+3)
outermost layers in
H_{4}[A_{4}]
is at most
\alpha
and
|N_{H_{4}}(A_{4})|\leq\alpha
.
Consider now a vertex
v\in A_{4}
which does not belong to any of the
(k+3)
outermost layers in
H_{4}[A_{4}]
.
These layers form a sequence of
k+3
nested
v
-planarizing connected sets in
H_{4}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
, so
v
is
k
-irrelevant in
H_{4}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
.
We can thus remove all such vertices
and obtain the final tuple
(H_{5},Y_{5},\mathcal{B}_{5},A_{5})
.
The forward safeness is trivial and the backward safeness follows from the definition of
k
-irrelevance.
In the resulting graph we have
|A_{5}|\leq(k+3)\cdot\alpha
and
|N_{H_{5}}(A_{5})|\leq\alpha
.

With the recipe for compressing a single bridge component in
\mathcal{B}
without affecting the other parts of the graph, we can perform this replacement for every
A\in\mathcal{B}
satisfying
|E_{H}(A,V(H)\setminus A)|\geq 3
.
Let
(H^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})

be obtained from
(G\langle D\rangle,Y,\mathcal{B})
by such a procedure
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
denote the subfamily of sets with at least 3 outgoing edges, for which we have performed the modification.
We assign labels to
\partial(H^{\prime})
to make them consistent with the (implicit) labeling of
\partial(H)
.
We set
G^{\prime}=H^{\prime}\oplus G_{\partial}[\overline{D}]
. The properties of forward and backward safeness imply conditions (
3
)
and (4)—the existence of an algorithm that lifts solutions from
G^{\prime}
to
G
.
Let
F^{\prime}
be the union of
N_{H^{\prime}}[A]
over all
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{3}

plus the union of
N_{H^{\prime}}(A)
over all
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{3}
plus
Y^{\prime}
.
For
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{3}
it holds
|N_{H^{\prime}}(A)|\leq 2
and for
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
it holds
|N_{H^{\prime}}[A]|\leq(k+4)\cdot\alpha
.

We have
|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|=|\mathcal{B}|
,
|Y^{\prime}|=|Y|
, and so
|F^{\prime}|\leq|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}|\cdot(k+4)\cdot\alpha+|Y^{\prime}|\leq 24r\cdot(2\cdot r^{2}\cdot(2k+7)^{4}+1)\leq 50\cdot r^{3}\cdot(2k+7)^{4}
.

Finally, we want to estimate the neighborhood size of connected components
R^{\prime}
of
H^{\prime}-(\partial(H^{\prime})\cup F^{\prime})
.
If
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{3}
then clearly
|N_{H^{\prime}}(R^{\prime})|\leq 2
.
Otherwise, the vertex set
V(R^{\prime})
is a subset of
V(R)
, where
R
is some component of
H-(\partial(H)\cup Y\cup\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{B}}V(A))
.
Hence, by the properties of a bridge decomposition,
R
can have at most 2 neighbors in
\partial(H)
, at most 10 neighbors in
Y
, and can be adjacent to at most 10 components from
\mathcal{B}
.
This means that
R^{\prime}
also can have at most 2 neighbors in
\partial(H^{\prime})
and at most 10 neighbors in
Y^{\prime}
.
To estimate the number of neighbors of
R^{\prime}
in
N_{H^{\prime}}[A]
, for
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, first observe that these neighbors must belong to
N_{H^{\prime}}(A)
.
It remains to show that
R^{\prime}
can be adjacent to at most 2 vertices from
N_{H^{\prime}}(A)
for a fixed
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
Suppose otherwise, that there are 3 such vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
.
Because
(Y^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
is a bridge decomposition of
H^{\prime}
, they must lie on the outer face of
H^{\prime}-\partial(H^{\prime})
.
We can thus add a vertex
u
to
H^{\prime}-\partial(H^{\prime})
, adjacent to all
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
, while maintaining planarity.
However,
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
together with
u,V(R^{\prime}),V(A)
form a minor model of
K_{3,3}
; a contradiction.
In summary, the set
N_{H^{\prime}}(R^{\prime})
can comprise of at most 2 vertices from
\partial(H^{\prime})
, 10 vertices from
Y^{\prime}
, and
2\cdot 10
vertices in total from
N_{G^{\prime}}[A]
, where
A\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, which gives at most 32 neighbors of
R^{\prime}
.
∎

9
Wrapping up

We are ready to combine all the intermediate results into a proof of the main theorem.

Proposition

6.21
provides us with a strong modulator
X
of fragmentation
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and a family of embeddings for
G\langle C\rangle
for
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
of radial diameter
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
We will now summarize Sections
7
and
8
with
Proposition

9.1
.
First we use
Proposition

7.15
to compute a 170-preserver
S_{C}
for
C
,
and then
Lemma

7.11
to find a superset
Y_{C}
of
S_{C}
so that
N_{G}(C)\cup Y_{C}
is radially connected in
G\langle C\rangle
.
The union of sets
Y_{C}
together with
X
gives a modulator
X^{\prime}
in
G
containing some 170-approximate solution.
Furthermore, for
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X^{\prime})
we get a circumscribed embedding of
G\langle C^{\prime}\rangle
.

Lemma

8.10
allows us to mark
k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
vertices in
C^{\prime}
so that the remaining components have only
\mathcal{O}(1)
neighbors.
Such components can be reduced to be of size
\mathcal{O}(1)
using protrusion replacement.

Proposition 9.1
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given integers
k,d,r,s
, a graph
G
along with an
r
-strong planar modulator
X\subseteq V(G)
of fragmentation
s
,
and for each
C\in\mathcal{C}(G-X)
a plane embedding of
G\langle C\rangle
of radial diameter at most
d
,
returns a graph
G^{\prime}
, such that

1.

|V(G^{\prime})|=\mathcal{O}(d^{64}r^{32}k^{8}s^{2})
,

2.

\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq 170\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G)
,

3.

there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G,G^{\prime}
, and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
k
, outputs a planar modulator
S
in
G
such that

|S|\leq|S^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

We partition
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
into families
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}
, comprising those components that have at most 2 neighbors, and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}
, which contains all the remaining components.
As observed in the proof of
Lemma

7.14
, for

C\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
the empty set forms a 2-preserver.
For each
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}
,
we apply
Proposition

7.15
to compute a 170-preserver
S_{C}\subseteq C
of size
\mathcal{O}(d^{7}r^{4})
.
Note that
(X,\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}\cup\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3})
is a boundaried decomposition of
(G,\emptyset)
(a boundaried graph with an empty boundary).
By
Lemma

7.7
we obtain that
\widehat{X}=X\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}}S_{C}
forms a 170-preserver for
(G,\emptyset)
.
By definition, for any planar modulator
S
in
G
(in particular for the optimal one), there exists a planar modulator
\widehat{S}\subseteq\widehat{X}
such that
|\widehat{S}|\leq 170\cdot|S|
.

We proceed with compressing the components
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}
.
Recall that
|N_{G}(C)|\leq r
and we work with a plane embedding of
G\langle C\rangle
of radial diameter at most
d
.
We apply
Lemma

7.11
to
Y=S_{C}
to compute a set
Y_{C}\supseteq S_{C}
,
Y_{C}\subseteq C
of size
(2d+2)\cdot(|N_{G}(C)|+|S_{C}|)=\mathcal{O}(d^{8}r^{4})
such that for each connected component
A
of
G\langle C\rangle-(N_{G}(C)\cup Y_{C})
the boundaried plane graph
G\langle A\rangle
is circumscribed.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

7.4
there are at most
2\cdot(|N_{G}(C)|+|Y_{C}|)
such components with at least 3 neighbors.
Let
X_{0}=X\cup\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}}Y_{C}
; note that
\widehat{X}\subseteq X_{0}
.
Each connected component
C_{0}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X_{0})
is contained in some connected component
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X)
so
N_{G}(C_{0})\subseteq N_{G}(C)\cup Y_{C}
and thus
|N_{G}(C_{0})|=\mathcal{O}(d^{8}r^{4})
.
Again, we partition
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G-X_{0})
into families
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\leq 2}
, comprising those components that have at most 2 neighbors, and
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\geq 3}
, which contains all the remaining components.
The number of elements of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\geq 3}
is at most
\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 3}}(2\cdot(|N_{G}(C)|+|Y_{C}|))=\mathcal{O}(d^{8}r^{4}s)
.

Now for any
C_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\geq 3}
we can take advantage of the circumscribed embedding of
G\langle C_{0}\rangle
and apply

Lemma

8.10
to
G\langle C_{0}\rangle
.
This allows us to perform a replacement of
N_{G}[{C_{0}}]
with a compatible boundaried graph
H^{\prime}_{C_{0}}
such that any planar modulator disjoint from
{C_{0}}
(in particular one contained in
X_{0}
) remains valid and we can perform a lossless lifting of any solution of size at most
k
.
Furthermore, we are given a vertex set
F^{\prime}_{C_{0}}\subseteq V(H^{\prime}_{C_{0}})\setminus\partial(H^{\prime}_{C_{0}})
of size at most
50\cdot|N_{G}({C_{0}})|^{3}\cdot(2k+7)^{4}
so that each connected component of
H^{\prime}_{C_{0}}-(\partial(H^{\prime}_{C_{0}})\cup F^{\prime}_{C_{0}})
has at most 32 neighbors.
As before, we estimate the number of such components with at least 3 neighbors by
2\cdot|F^{\prime}_{C_{0}}|
(by
Lemma

7.4
).
We have upper bounded
|N_{G}(C_{0})|
by
\mathcal{O}(d^{8}r^{4})
, hence
|F^{\prime}_{C_{0}}|=\mathcal{O}(d^{24}r^{12}k^{4})
.

After performing this replacement for each
C_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\geq 3}
we obtain a new graph
G^{\prime}
such that the set
X_{0}
stays intact and any solution
S\subseteq X_{0}
remains valid.
Since
\widehat{X}\subseteq X_{0}
we get that
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq 170\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G)
.
Let
X^{\prime}=X_{0}\cup\bigcup_{{C_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\geq 3}}F^{\prime}_{C_{0}}
.
The fragmentation of
X^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
can be again bounded by
\sum_{{C_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\geq 3}}(2\cdot(|F^{\prime}_{C_{0}}|))=\mathcal{O}(d^{8}r^{4}s)\cdot\mathcal{O}(d^{24}r^{12}k^{4})=\mathcal{O}(d^{32}r^{16}k^{4}s)
.
Thanks to
Lemma

8.10
every component of
G^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
has at most 32 neighbors.

By
Lemma

6.2

the
Vertex planarization
problem admits a lossless protrusion replacer.
Let
\gamma
be the function from
Definition

6.1
and
g(r)=27(2r+7)(r+1)
.
Using
Lemma

6.6
we can replace any connected component of
G^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
which is larger than
\gamma(g(32))=\mathcal{O}(1)
with one smaller than
\gamma(g(32))
without affecting the value of
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
and so that any solution to the new instance can be losslessly lifted to a solution to the original instance.
We perform such a replacement for each component of
G^{\prime}-X^{\prime}
with at least 3 neighbors.
This yields a total number of vertices proportional to the fragmentation of
X^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, that is
\mathcal{O}(d^{32}r^{16}k^{4}s)
.

The number of connected components from
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime}-X^{\prime})
with at most 2 neighbors may be large so we handle them differently.
We partition these components into groups sharing the same neighborhood.
There are
{\binom{|X^{\prime}|}{2}}+|X^{\prime}|\leq|X^{\prime}|^{2}
possible neighborhoods.
Let
X_{0}\subseteq X^{\prime}
be of size at most 2 and let
C(X_{0})
denote the union of these components
C_{i}
from
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime}-X^{\prime})
whose neighborhood is exactly
N_{G^{\prime}}(C_{i})=X_{0}
.
Similarly as above we repeatedly perform protrusion replacement for each
C(X_{0})
.
The graph
G^{\prime}\langle C(X_{0})\rangle
is connected so
Lemma

6.6
applies and if
C(X_{0})
is larger than
\gamma(g(2))
then we can replace it
with a subgraph on at most
\gamma(g(2))
vertices.
This gives
\gamma(g(2))\cdot|X^{\prime}|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(d^{64}r^{32}k^{8}s^{2})
vertices in total.
∎

Finally, we combine
Proposition

9.1
with the propositions from the previous sections to obtain our main technical contribution.
The final exponent in the approximate kernelization evaluates to 7082.
In the solution-lifting part we require a strict inequality
|S^{\prime}|<\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}
to avoid off-by-one issues in the proof of
1.1
.

Theorem 9.2
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a graph
G
and an integer
k
,
either

1.

outputs a planar modulator in
G
of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G)
, or

2.

correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
, or

3.

outputs a graph
G^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{O}(k^{7082})
vertices and integer
\ell
, such that if
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
, then
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq 170\cdot(\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\ell)
.
Furthermore, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G,k,G^{\prime}
, and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size
|S^{\prime}|<\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}
,
outputs a planar modulator
S
in
G
such that

|S|\leq 3\cdot|S^{\prime}|+3\cdot\ell
.

Proof.

We will analyze a series of graphs
G,G_{1},G_{2},G_{3},G_{4}=G^{\prime}
obtained from
G
by applying the intermediate reductions.
For each reduction we need to prove (a) the forward safeness: that the existence of a planar modulator in
G_{i}
implies the existence of a planar modulator in
G_{i+1}
of comparable size, and (b) the backward safeness: that when given a planar modulator in
G_{i+1}
within particular size bound we can lift it to a planar modulator in
G_{i}
.

We begin with
Proposition

4.16
.
We either find a planar modulator in
G
of size
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
, or
correctly conclude that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
, or
construct a graph
G_{1}
together with a tree decomposition of width
\mathcal{O}(k^{36})
.
In the first two cases we can terminate the algorithm.
In the last case we also obtain
an integer
k_{1}\leq k
, such that if
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k
, then
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{1})=\mathrm{mvp}(G)-(k-k_{1})
.
Furthermore, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G,k,G_{1}
, and a planar modulator
S_{1}
in
G_{1}
of size at most
k_{1}
,
outputs a planar modulator
S
in
G
such that

|S|=|S_{1}|+(k-k_{1})
.

Next, we apply
Proposition

5.7
to
G_{1},k_{1}
, and the computed tree decomposition of width
t=\mathcal{O}(k^{36})
.
Again, if we conclude that
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{1})>k_{1}
then it implies that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
so we can terminate the algorithm.
Otherwise, we compute a graph
G_{2}
with an
r
-strong planar modulator
X_{2}
, where
r=2t+2
, and an integer
k_{2}\leq k_{1}
, such that

\mathrm{mvp}(G_{1})\leq k_{1}
implies
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{2})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G_{1})-(k_{1}-k_{2})

and the fragmentation of
X_{2}
in
G_{2}
is
s=\mathcal{O}(k^{49}t^{48})=\mathcal{O}(k^{1777})
.
Furthermore, given a planar modulator
S_{2}
in
G_{2}
, one can turn it into a planar modulator in
G_{1}
of size at most
|S_{2}|+3\cdot(k_{1}-k_{2})
in polynomial time.

We process the pair
(G_{2},X_{2})
with
Proposition

6.21
for the parameter value
k_{2}
.
We obtain a new graph
G_{3}
, an
r
-strong modulator
X_{3}
in
G_{3}
of fragmentation
s
,
and
a family of plane embeddings of
G_{3}\langle C\rangle
for each connected component
C\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{C}(G_{3}-X_{3})
, each with a radial diameter

d=\mathcal{O}(kr)=\mathcal{O}(k^{37})
.
We know that
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{3})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G_{2})
and there is
a lifting algorithm that can turn a solution
S_{3}
of size at most
{k_{2}}
in
G_{3}
into a solution of size at most
3\cdot|S_{3}|
in
G_{2}
.

The last modification applies
Proposition

9.1
to the decomposition computed so far and the parameter value
k_{2}
.
We obtain a graph
G_{4}
with
\mathcal{O}(d^{64}r^{32}k^{8}s^{2})=\mathcal{O}(k^{7082})
vertices (
37\cdot 64+36\cdot 32+8+1777\cdot 2=7082
), such that
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{4})\leq 170\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G_{3})
and any planar modulator
S_{4}
of size at most
k_{2}
in
G_{4}
can be turned, in polynomial time, into a planar modulator of size at most
|S_{4}|
in
G_{3}
.

We set
\ell=k-k_{2}
. We check the forward safeness:
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq k\Rightarrow\mathrm{mvp}(G_{1})\leq k_{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{mvp}(G_{2})\leq k_{2}=k-\ell

so
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{2})\leq\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\ell
.
Then
\mathrm{mvp}(G_{4})\leq 170\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G_{3})\leq 170\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G_{2})\leq 170\cdot(\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\ell)
.
Now we check the backward safeness.
Suppose that we are given a planar modulator in
S_{4}
in
G_{4}
of size less than
\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}\leq k_{2}
.
We follow the chain of the lifting algorithms backwards from
G_{4}
to
G
to compute the following planar modulators:

•

S_{3}
in
G_{3}
of size at most
|S_{4}|
,

•

S_{2}
in
G_{2}
of size at most
3\cdot|S_{3}|<k+1-3\cdot\ell
, (so
|S_{2}|\leq k-3\cdot\ell\leq k_{2}
),

•

S_{1}
in
G_{1}
of size at most
|S_{2}|+3\cdot(k_{1}-k_{2})\leq k-3\cdot(k-k_{1})\leq k_{1}
, and finally

•

S
in
G
of size at most
|S_{1}|+(k-k_{1})
.

We check that
|S|\leq|S_{2}|+3\cdot\ell\leq 3\cdot|S_{4}|+3\cdot\ell
.
The claim follows.
∎

The main theorem is now a simple corollary from
9.2
.
It remains to unroll the definition of the approximate kernelization and reformulate the guarantees in this language.
Recall that for a graph
G
, integer
k
,
and
S\subseteq V(G)
, we define
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)=\min(|S|,k+1)
if
S
is a planar modulator in
G
, and
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)=\infty
otherwise.
We have that
\mathsf{OPT}_{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}}(G,k)=\min(\mathrm{mvp}(G),k+1)
.
Capping the solution cost at
k+1
cannot increase the approximation factor of a solution, that is,
\frac{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)}{\mathsf{OPT}_{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}}(G,k)}\leq\frac{|S|}{\mathrm{mvp}(G)}
.
When
|S|\leq k
, then no capping occurs and we obtain equality.

See
1.1

Proof.

Let
(G,k)
be the input instance and recall the formalization of the parameterized minimization problem
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}
from Section
3
.
As the reduction algorithm, we run the reduction algorithm from
9.2
.
If it terminates with option (1) or (2), we output an arbitrary instance, e.g., a single-vertex graph and
k^{\prime}=0
.
Otherwise, we output
G^{\prime}
and
k^{\prime}=\lceil\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}\rceil
.

The solution-lifting algorithm, given
G,k,G^{\prime}
, and a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
, first executes the reduction algorithm from
9.2
again.
If it terminates with option (1) we ignore
S^{\prime}
and return the optimal solution.
Also, if it terminates with option (2) we ignore
S^{\prime}
and return
S=V(G)
with cost
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,V(G))=k+1
.
Note that when
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>k
then
OPT_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k)=k+1
hence
V(G)
is optimal with respect to the capped solution cost.
If the lifting algorithm terminates with option (3) but

|S^{\prime}|>\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}
, we return
S=V(G)
: we will check that we are allowed to do this.
Otherwise we take advantage of the solution-lifting algorithm from
9.2
.

Let
\beta=\frac{|S^{\prime}|}{\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})}\geq 1
and
\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}=\frac{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},S^{\prime})}{\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\geq 1
.
We have
\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}\leq\beta
.
If
(510\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}})\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G)\geq k+1
, then by definition any solution in
G
evaluates at
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}
to at most
k+1
and we get the desired
(510\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}})
-approximation no matter whether we use the solution-lifting algorithm or just return
V(G)
.

Suppose now that
(510\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}})\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G)<k+1
.
We are going to show that this implies
|S^{\prime}|<\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}\leq k^{\prime}

and that the size of the solution
S
returned by the solution-lifting algorithm is within the approximation guarantee.
From
9.2
we have
\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq 170\cdot(\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\ell)
and so

\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})<\frac{k+1}{3\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}}-170\cdot\ell\leq\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}\leq k^{\prime}
.
Therefore the optimum in
G^{\prime}
is not being capped and
\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})=\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},S^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})\leq\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}\cdot 170\cdot(\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\ell)

\displaystyle<\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}\cdot 170\cdot\left(\frac{k+1}{510\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}}-\ell\right)\leq\frac{k+1}{3}-170\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}\cdot\ell\leq\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}.

In the last inequality we estimate simply
170\cdot\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}\cdot\ell\geq\ell
.
This implies that
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},S^{\prime})\leq k^{\prime}
so
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},S^{\prime})=|S^{\prime}|
and
\beta=\beta^{\mathsf{cap}}
.
The inequality
|S^{\prime}|<\frac{k+1-3\cdot\ell}{3}

allows us to use the solution-lifting algorithm.
We estimate the size of the returned solution
S
.

|S|\leq 3\cdot|S^{\prime}|+3\cdot\ell=3\cdot\beta\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})+3\cdot\ell\leq 3\cdot\beta\cdot 170\cdot(\mathrm{mvp}(G)-\ell)+3\ell\leq 510\cdot\beta\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Polynomial-time approximation

As a consequence of
1.1
we give improved approximation algorithms for
Vertex planarization
.
This is obtained by pipelining the approximate kernelization with the following result by Kawarabayashi and Sidiropoulos.

Theorem 9.3

(
[
KS17
]
)
.

Vertex planarization
admits the following randomized approximation algorithms on
n
-vertex graphs.

1.

\mathcal{O}(n^{\varepsilon})
-approximation in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon)}
for any
\varepsilon>0
,

2.

\mathcal{O}((\log n)^{32})
-approximation in time
n^{\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\right)}
.

We give a general observation on the result of applying
\rho(n)
-approximation to the outcome of approximate kernelization.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\alpha,c,d,r\in\mathbb{N}
be constants and
\rho,f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be functions satisfying:

1.

Vertex planarization
admits an
\alpha
-approximate kernelization of size
f
, such that
f(k)\leq c\cdot k^{d}
for
k\geq r
, and

2.

\alpha\cdot\rho(k^{2d})\leq k
for
k\geq r
.

Suppose that
Vertex planarization
admits a (randomized)

\rho(n)
-approximation algorithm running in time
T(n)
where
n
is the number of vertices in the input.
Then there is a (randomized) algorithm that, given a graph
G
and integer
\ell
, runs in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{r+\mathcal{O}(1)})+T(c^{2d}\cdot\ell^{2d})
,
and either outputs a planar modulator in
G
of size at most
\alpha\cdot\rho(c^{2d}\cdot\ell^{2d})\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G)

or correctly concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>\ell
.

Proof.

If
\ell<r
we can find a solution of size at most
\ell
in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{r+1})
so suppose that
\ell\geq r
.
We execute the algorithm from
1.1
on the instance
(G,k)
where
k=c\cdot\ell^{2}
and obtain a new instance
(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})
,
where
G^{\prime}
has at most

n^{\prime}=c^{d+1}\cdot\ell^{2d}\leq(c\cdot\ell)^{2d}
vertices.
Next, we execute the
\rho(n)
-approximation algorithm on
G^{\prime}
and obtain a planar modulator
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
of size at most
\rho((c\cdot\ell)^{2d})\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})
.
We use the solution lifting algorithm to turn
S^{\prime}
into a planar modulator
S
in
G
so that

\frac{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)}{\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k)}\leq\alpha\cdot\frac{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},S^{\prime})}{\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\leq\alpha\cdot\frac{|S^{\prime}|}{\mathrm{mvp}(G^{\prime})}\leq\alpha\cdot\rho((c\cdot\ell)^{2d}).

We will show that when
\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq\ell
then actually
|S|\leq k
and so
\frac{\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)}{\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k)}=\frac{|S|}{\mathrm{mvp}(G)}
.
As
\ell\leq k
this already implies that
\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k)=\mathrm{mvp}(G)
.
It remains to show that
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)\leq k
which implies

\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)=|S|
.
From the estimation above, we have
\Pi_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k,S)\leq\alpha\cdot\rho((c\cdot\ell)^{2d})\cdot\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k)
but by the assumption on
\rho
and since
r\leq\ell\leq c\cdot\ell
and
\mathsf{OPT}_{\textsc{vp}}(G,k)=\mathrm{mvp}(G)\leq\ell
this is upper bounded by

(c\cdot\ell)\cdot\ell=k
.
Hence, if we get
|S|>k
, we can conclude that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>\ell
.
Otherwise,
S
is an
(\alpha\cdot\rho((c\cdot\ell)^{2d}))
-approximate solution.
∎

Since approximation factors of the form
(\log n)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and
\mathcal{O}(n^{\varepsilon})
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
satisfy the conditions of
Lemma

9.4
, we obtain the following consequence.

See
1.2

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

9.4
for polynomial function
f
and functions
\rho
from
9.3
.
To see part (1), consider
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2d}
, where
d
is the exponent in the kernel size from
1.1
, and
\rho(k)=\mathcal{O}(k^{\varepsilon})
.
Then
\rho(k^{2d})=o(k)
and the asymptotic conditions in
Lemma

9.4
are met.
We obtain an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and integer
\ell
, runs in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{r+\mathcal{O}(1)})+\ell^{\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{2d\varepsilon})}
, where
r
is a constant from
Lemma

9.4
depending on
\varepsilon
, and either returns a solution of size
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2d\varepsilon})\cdot\mathrm{mvp}(G)
or concludes that
\mathrm{mvp}(G)>\ell
.
We execute this algorithm for
\ell=1,2,3,\dots
until we obtain a solution.
Then we know that
\mathsf{OPT}=\mathrm{mvp}(G)\geq\ell
and the returned solution gives an
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{OPT}^{2d\varepsilon})
-approximation.
The claim follows by adjusting
\varepsilon
.

The proof of part (2) is analogous. We consider
\rho(k)=\mathcal{O}((\log k)^{32})
for which
\rho(k^{2d})=o(k)
.
The algorithm from
Lemma

9.4
runs in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}+\ell^{\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log\ell}{\log\log\ell}\right)}
and gives approximation factor
\alpha\cdot\rho(\ell^{2d})=\mathcal{O}((\log\ell)^{32})
.
∎

10
Planarization criteria

In this section we provide the proofs of the planarization criteria stated in Section
3
.
We first show how the criterion from
Lemma

3.15
can be used to prove that a graph remains planar after some modification.
The following lemma generalizes the case where
S
is a connected restricted
(v,V(C))
-separator which was an argument used in earlier work
[
JLS14
]
.
This stronger version will be later needed to prove
Lemma

3.21
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
G
be a connected graph,
C
be a cycle in
G
,
S\subseteq V(G)
, and
v\in V(G)\setminus(V(C)\cup S)
.
Suppose that
|S\cap V(C)|\leq 1
,
G[S]
is connected,
G[S\setminus V(C)]
is non-empty and connected,
R_{G}(v,S)\cap V(C)=\emptyset
, and the overlap graph
O(G-v,C)
is bipartite.
Then the overlap graph
O(G,C)
is bipartite as well.

Proof.

For a
C
-bridge
B
we denote by
A(B)\subseteq V(C)
its set of attachments on
C
.
We say that
B_{1},B_{2}
are
incomparable
if
A(B_{1})\not\subseteq A(B_{2})
and
A(B_{2})\not\subseteq A(B_{1})
.
We start by showing that inserting
v
into
G-v
cannot merge two incomparable
C
-bridges
B_{1},B_{2}
into one
C
-bridge.
Suppose otherwise.
Let
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
.
There exist a
(v,b_{1})
-path
P_{1}
in
G
with internal vertices in
V(B_{1})
and a
(v,b_{2})
-path
P_{2}
with internal vertices in
V(B_{2})
.
Suppose first that
S\cap V(C)=\emptyset
.
Then
S
is a connected restricted
(v,V(C))
-separator in
G
and both paths
P_{1},P_{2}
must cross
S
.
Let
P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2}
be obtained from
P_{1},P_{2}
by removing their endpoints; note that they are non-empty.
Then
V(P^{\prime}_{1})\cup V(P^{\prime}_{2})\cup S
induces a connected subgraph of
(G-v)-V(C)
and contains vertices from both
B_{1},B_{2}
; a contradiction with the assumption that
B_{1},B_{2}
are distinct
C
-bridges in
G-v
.

Suppose now that
S\cap V(C)=\{t\}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
b_{1}\neq t
, which can be achieved by swapping
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
if needed: since they are incomparable, at least one of them has an attachment point other than
t
.
Since
R_{G}(v,S)\cap V(C)=\emptyset
, the path
P_{1}
must intersect
S\setminus V(C)
.
As
G[S\setminus V(C)]
is connected, we obtain
S\setminus V(C)\subseteq V(B_{1})
and, since
G[S]
is connected,
t\in A(B_{1})
.
If there exists
b_{2}\in A(B_{2})\setminus\{t\}
, then by the same argument we get
S\setminus V(C)\subseteq V(B_{2})
, contradicting that
B_{1},B_{2}
are distinct
C
-bridges.
Therefore
A(B_{2})=\{t\}
, which contradicts that
B_{1},B_{2}
are incomparable.

Using the derived property we conclude the proof. If
v
is an isolated vertex of
G-V(C)
, then its insertion to
G-v
creates a new
C
-bridge.
The attachments of the bridge
\{v\}
must belong to
S\cap V(C)
(since
R_{G}(v,S)\cap V(C)=\emptyset
) so the new bridge can have only one attachment.
Such a bridge cannot overlap with any other bridge, so this cannot create any odd cycles in the overlap graph.
Otherwise, inserting
v
merges some bridges
B_{1},B_{2},\dots,B_{\ell}
(possibly
\ell=1
) into one and augments the set of attachments by
N_{G}(v)\cap V(C)
.
There cannot be an incomparable pair in
B_{1},B_{2},\dots,B_{\ell}
, so there is one
C
-bridge
B_{i}
so that
A(B_{j})\subseteq A(B_{i})
for
j\in[\ell]
.
If
B_{j}
is overlapping with some bridge
B^{\prime}
, then also
B_{i}
overlaps with
B^{\prime}
.
Similarly as in the previous paragraph, we have
S\cap V(C)\subseteq A(B_{i})
and also
N_{G}(v)\cap V(C)\subseteq S\cap V(C)
.
Therefore inserting
v
into
G-v
cannot augment the set of attachments of
B_{i}
.
The graph
O(G,C)
is thus obtained from
O(G-v,C)
by simply removing vertices representing
B_{j}
for
j\neq i
.
This also cannot create any odd cycles.
∎

This is sufficient to prove the “classic” planarity criterion (
Lemma

3.16
).
We present the following, slightly stronger, lemma, in which one of the separators does not need to be cyclic but only connected.
This directly implies
Lemma

3.16
and later will be useful for proving
Lemma

3.17
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
v_{0},v_{3}\in V(G)
and let
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq V(G)
be disjoint nested restricted
(v_{0},v_{3})
-separators in a connected graph
G
, so that
G[S_{2}]
has a Hamiltonian cycle and
G[S_{1}]
is connected. If
R_{G}[v_{0},S_{2}]
and
R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
induce planar graphs, then
G
is planar.

Proof.

First we show that if
G-v_{0}
is planar then
G
is planar as well.
Let
C
be a Hamiltonian cycle in
G[S_{2}]
.
By
Lemma

3.15
the overlap graph
O(G-v_{0},C)
is bipartite and for every
C
-bridge
B
in
G-v_{0}
the graph
B\cup C
is planar.
We apply
Lemma

10.1
for the set
S=S_{1}
which has empty intersection with
V(C)
,
R_{G}(v_{0},S)\cap V(C)=\emptyset
, and the graph
G[S]
is connected.
We obtain that the overlap graph
O(G,C)
is bipartite.
Let
B_{0}
be the
C
-bridge in
G
containing
v_{0}
.
Then
B_{0}\cup C
is a subgraph of
G[R_{G}[v_{0},V(C)]]
which is planar by assumption.
For any other
C
-bridge
B
in
G
, the graph
B\cup C
is a subgraph of
G-v_{0}
, hence it is planar as well.
Therefore, from
Lemma

3.15
we infer that
G
is planar.

Now we give a proof of the lemma by induction on the number of vertices in
V(G)\setminus R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
.
If
v_{0}
is the only vertex in
V(G)\setminus R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
then
G-v_{0}
is planar and the argument above implies planarity of
G
.
When
v_{0}
is an articulation point in
G
then
only one connected component
C_{0}
of
G-v_{0}
may be not fully contained in
R_{G}[v,S_{1}]
and by
3.18
it suffices to focus on the graph
G_{0}=G[V(C_{0})\cup\{v_{0}\}]
.
The sets
S_{1},S_{2}
as well as the vertex
v_{3}
must be contained in
V(G_{0})
.
If
v_{0}
is not an articulation point, we set
G_{0}=G
.
Let
v^{\prime}\in V(G)\setminus R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
be distinct from
v_{0}
and contained in
G_{0}-v_{0}
.
We have that
S_{1},S_{2}
are disjoint nested restricted
(v^{\prime},v_{3})
-separators in a connected graph
G_{0}-v_{0}
.
By induction we obtain that
G_{0}-v_{0}
is planar.
Applying the argument from the first paragraph concludes the proof.
∎

10.1
The new criterion

We move on to proving the new planarity criterion, where instead of 2 cyclic nested separators, we work with 3 connected nested separators.
We start with the following observation about the behavior of nested separators.

Observation 10.3
.

If
(S_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell}
is a sequence of nested vertex-disjoint restricted
(v_{0},v_{\ell+1})
-separators in a connected graph
G
such that
G[S_{i}]
is connected for all
i\in[\ell]
, then the following holds.

1.

For each
i\in[\ell-1]
(resp.
i\in\{2,\ldots,\ell\}
) we have
N_{G}[S_{i}]\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{i+1}]
(resp.
N_{G}[S_{i}]\subseteq R_{G}[v_{\ell+1},S_{i-1}]
).

2.

For each
i\in[\ell-1]
we have
R_{G}[v_{0},S_{i}]\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{i+1}]
and
S_{i}\subseteq R_{G}(v_{0},S_{i+1})
.

The following elementary lemma localizes paths connecting consecutive separators in a nested sequence.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
S_{1},S_{2}
be two nested vertex-disjoint restricted
(v_{0},v_{3})
-separators in a connected graph
G
, such that
G[S_{i}]
is connected for each
i\in[2]
. If
P
is an
(S_{1},S_{2})
-path in
G
whose internal vertices are disjoint from
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
, then
V(P)\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{2}]\cap R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
.

Proof.

Let
P^{\prime}
be the subpath formed by the interior vertices of
P
. Since each
G[S_{i}]
is connected and
G
is connected, we have
S_{i}\subseteq R_{G}[x,S_{i}]
for each
x\in\{v_{0},v_{3}\}
. If
V(P^{\prime})=\emptyset
, then together with the fact that
S_{1}\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{2}]
and
S_{2}\subseteq R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
by Observation
10.3
, the lemma follows.

So suppose
V(P^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and orient
P^{\prime}
so that it starts at a neighbor
w_{1}
of
S_{1}
and ends in a neighbor
w_{2}
of
S_{2}
. By the previous argument, it suffices to show that
V(P^{\prime})\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{2}]\cap R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
. By the first point of Observation
10.3
we have
w_{1}\in N_{G}[S_{1}]\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{2}]
and since
P^{\prime}
is by definition disjoint from
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
, reachability of the first vertex of
P^{\prime}
implies reachability of the entire subpath:
V(P^{\prime})\subseteq R_{G}[v_{0},S_{2}]
. Conversely, since
P^{\prime}
ends in
w_{2}\in N_{G}[S_{2}]\subseteq R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
and
P^{\prime}
does not intersect
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
, path
P^{\prime}
is contained entirely in
R_{G}[v_{3},S_{1}]
, which completes the proof.
∎

See
3.17

Proof.

Since the separators
S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
are nested,
S_{2}
is an
(S_{1},S_{3})
-separator. We first argue that we can assume that
S_{2}
is in fact
inclusion-wise minimal
with respect to simultaneously being a (restricted)
(S_{1},S_{3})
separator and inducing a connected graph, as follows. If
S^{\prime}_{2}\subsetneq S_{2}
is also a connected
(S_{1},S_{3})
separator, then the sequence
S_{1},S^{\prime}_{2},S_{3}
is also a sequence of nested
(v_{0},v_{4})
separators satisfying the lemma statement, since each
(S_{1},S_{3})
-separator is also an
(v_{0},v_{4})
-separator since they are nested. As the graphs induced by
R_{G}[v_{0},S_{3}]
and
R_{G}[v_{4},S_{1}]
are the same with respect to this sequence, the triple
S_{1},S^{\prime}_{2},S_{3}
also satisfies the preconditions to the lemma.

In the remainder may therefore assume that
S_{2}
is a
(v_{0},v_{4})
-separator which is inclusion-minimal with respect to being a connected
(S_{1},S_{3})
-separator.

We first deal with an easy case. If
|S_{2}|=1
, then the single vertex
x
in
S_{2}
is an articulation point that separates
v_{0}
from
v_{4}
. In this case, it is easy to verify that for each connected component
H
of
G-x
, the set
V(H)\cup\{x\}
is a subset of
R_{G}[v_{0},S_{3}]
or
R_{G}[v_{4},S_{1}]
and therefore induces a planar graph by assumption. Hence by Observation
3.18
, in this case we are guaranteed that
G
is planar.

In the remainder we assume that
S_{2}
is not a singleton, and is inclusion-minimal with respect to being a connected
(S_{1},S_{3})
-separator. For the following claim, recall that
R_{G}(x,S)
denotes the set of vertices reachable from
x
in
G-S
, that is, the vertex set of the connected component of
G-S
containing
x
.

Claim 10.5
.

G[S_{2}]
has a Hamiltonian path whose endpoint(s) are adjacent to both
R_{G}(v_{0},S_{2})
and
R_{G}(v_{4},S_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
T
be a spanning tree of the connected subgraph
G[S_{2}]
. Since
S_{2}
is minimal with respect to being a connected
(S_{1},S_{3})
-separator, each leaf
\ell
of
T
is adjacent to both the connected component of
G-S_{2}
containing
S_{1}
(on vertex set
R_{G}(v_{0},S_{2}
)) and the connected component of
